The probability that two elements with large 1 1 -eigenspaces generate a classical group
S.P. Glasby, Alice C. Niemeyer, Cheryl E. Praeger

Abstract
. With high probability, among O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) independent randomly selected elements from a finite n n -dimensional classical group, some pair of elements power to a 2 2 -element generating set for a naturally embedded classical subgroup of dimension O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) . The 2 2 -element generating set produced consists of certain elements with large 1 1 -eigenspaces, called stingray elements. Underpinning this result is a new theorem on the generation of a finite classical group by a pair of stingray elements. For example, we show that, for classical groups not containing SL n ​ ( q ) {\mathrm{SL}}_{n}(q) , the probability of generation is at least 0.983 0.983 except for one exceptional case. The explicit probability bounds we obtain will be applied to justify complexity analyses for new constructive recognition algorithms for finite classical groups. Keywords: finite classical groups, Aschbacher classes, almost simple, stingray element, random generation 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification: 20D06, 20P05, 20H20



1.
Introduction

It has been known since 1995 that two random elements of a finite nonabelian simple group
G
generate the group with probability approaching
1
as
|G|\to\infty
,
[
9
,
19
,
24
]
. That this might be true for
G=A_{n}
goes back to a conjecture in 1882 of Netto
[
29
, p.76.]
. More delicate arguments show that one may control the orders of the generating elements: for example, with high probability a simple group
G
will be generated by a random involution together with a second randomly selected element (as conjectured in
[
19
]
and proved in
[
25
,
26
]
). The main motivation for much of this work was the long-standing problem of determining which finite simple groups
G
were quotients of the classical modular group
{\mathrm{PSL}}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, equivalently deciding which simple groups
G
were
(2,3)
-generated, see
[
25
]
. Further work
[
28
]
showed that, for primes
r,s
not both
2
, the probability that two randomly chosen elements of a simple classical group
G
, of orders
r
and
s
, generate
G
tends to
1
as
|G|\to\infty
, provided the rank of
G
is sufficiently large.

For effective use of these results computationally, explicit probability bounds are required not just asymptotic ones, and these bounds should be available for classical groups of all ranks. Moreover, efficient methods both to construct and to recognise suitable generating elements are needed. An example where two prime order elements were used to recognise classical groups was given in
[
30
]
; these were so-called ‘ppd elements’ (Definition
2.2
(b)) and they could be efficiently constructed and recognised, and explicit bounds were available for their proportions.

With a variety of applications in mind, Burness, Liebeck and Shalev
[
6
]
showed that all maximal subgroups of a finite nonabelian simple group can also be generated efficiently. Moreover, several recognition algorithms for (quasi)simple groups require construction of
naturally embedded
smaller (quasi)simple groups; for example a subgroup
A_{k}
of a larger alternating group
A_{n}
is naturally embedded if it has one orbit of size
k
and fixes the remaining
n-k
points (see Subsection
1.2
), and a
k
-dimensional classical group is naturally embedded in a larger
n
-dimensional classical group if it acts naturally on a
k
-dimensional subspace, and fixes pointwise an
(n-k)
-dimensional complementary space. Most constructive recognition algorithms for finite classical groups require the construction of smaller naturally embedded classical subgroups: for example, the 1996 algorithm of Kantor and Seress
[
20
]
to recognise black-box classical groups constructs a naturally embedded
{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}(q)
(or
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{4}(q)
or
{\mathrm{SU}}_{4}(q)
) in an
n
-dimensional classical group, usually via constructing transvections or similar elements from certain
(n-2)
-ppd elements (Definition
2.2
(b)); while a 2013 classical recognition algorithm by Dietrich, Leedham-Green, Lübeck and O’Brien
[
8
, Section 3]
recursively constructs naturally embedded
k
-dimensional classical subgroups in an
n
-dimensional classical group where
k\in[n/3,2n/3]
using stingray elements (Definition
2.2
(c)). In an earlier algorithm for classical groups in odd characteristic, such naturally embedded classical subgroups were obtained by constructing involutions and their centralisers,
[
22
, Section 4]
.

In all the procedures mentioned above the first objective was to obtain a naturally embedded
k
-dimensional classical subgroup, with
k
very small, of an
n
-dimensional classical group. To achieve this recursively, by essentially halving the dimension each time, requires
\log n
steps. It was a request from our colleague Ákos Seress to the second and third authors that essentially led to the work presented in this paper. Ákos was convinced by computational evidence that one could achieve, in a single step, a natural embedding of a
k
-dimensional classical subgroup in an
n
-dimensional classical group with
k\approx\log n
. The construction would, as in
[
8
]
, involve ‘
e
-stingray elements’ (Definition
2.2
(c)), but this time with
e=O(\log n)
rather than
e=O(n)
. That such elements can be constructed efficiently by examining only
O(\log n)
random elements of the
n
-dimensional classical group was established in
[
31
, Theorem 3.3(b)]
; and in
[
13
, Section 3]
(see also
[
11
, Theorem 1.1]
) it was proved that a random
e_{1}
-stingray element and a random
e_{2}
-stingray element (with
e_{1},e_{2}
roughly
\log n
) should with high probability act on a non-degenerate
(e_{1}+e_{2})
-subspace and fix a complementary subspace pointwise. What remained was to prove that such a pair of stingray elements generates a naturally embedded
(e_{1}+e_{2})
-dimensional classical subgroup, with high probability. This is the main result of our paper (Theorem
1.3
) and was experimentally predicted by Frank Lübeck. Moreover, exploiting this result together with the results from
[
13
,
31
]
shows that a naturally embedded
d
-dimensional classical subgroup, for some
d\in[2\log n,8\log n]
can be constructed by examining
O(\log n)
random elements from the
n
-dimensional classical group (Theorem
1.1
; this is the result mentioned at the beginning of the abstract).

In Theorems
1.1
and
1.3
, by a
classical group of type
{\mathbf{X}}
, or more precisely,
of type
({\mathbf{X}},n,|{\mathbb{F}}|)
, we mean a group
G
satisfying
\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{n}(q)\leqslant G\leqslant\textup{G{\bf X}}_{n}(q)
in its action on the underlying vector space
V_{n}=\mathbb{F}^{n}
, with
{\mathbf{X}}
,
\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{n}(q)
,
\textup{G{\bf X}}_{n}(q)
,
{\mathbb{F}}
as in one of the lines of Table
1
. Note in particular that, for unitary groups, we write
{\mathrm{SU}}_{n}(q)
and
{\mathrm{GU}}_{n}(q)
and that these are subgroups of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{n}(q^{2})
. Theorem
1.1
is proved in Section
13.1
as a corollary of a more technical Proposition
13.4
. Note that, in Theorem
1.1
and Proposition
13.4
, the classical group may be an odd dimensional orthogonal group, while in Theorem
1.3
orthogonal groups must have even dimension as odd dimensional orthogonal groups contain no ‘stingray duos’.

.

Theorem 1.1
.

Suppose that
G
is a classical group of type
({\mathbf{X}},n,q)
with
n>8
as in Table
1
. Then for each
\eta>0
, there is a positive constant
k(\eta)
such that, with probability at least
1-\eta
, among
k(\eta)\log n
independent uniformly distributed random elements from
G
, some pair power to a
2
-element generating set for a naturally embedded
d
-dimensional classical subgroup of type
{\mathbf{Y}}
with
2\log n<d\leqslant c({\mathbf{X}})\log n
and
c({\mathbf{X}}),{\mathbf{Y}}
as in Table
1
.

We explain some terminology which we use in Theorem
1.3
, and comment on the type
{\mathbf{Y}}
in Table
1
.
An element
g
in a classical group
G
of type
{\mathbf{X}}
, with natural module
V_{n}
, is called an
e
-ppd stingray element
if
g
leaves invariant an
e
-dimensional subspace
U_{g}
of
V_{n}
, fixes pointwise a complementary subspace, and its order
|g|
is divisible by a primitive prime divisor (ppd) of
|{\mathbb{F}}|^{e}-1
(Definition
2.2
). Moreover, a pair
(g_{1},g_{2})
is an
(e_{1},e_{2})
-ppd stingray duo
if, for each
i
,
g_{i}
is an
e_{i}
-ppd stingray element relative to an
e_{i}
-dimensional subspace
U_{g_{i}}
of
V_{n}
, such that
U_{g_{1}}\cap U_{g_{2}}=0
so
V_{d}:=U_{g_{1}}\oplus U_{g_{2}}
has dimension
d:=e_{1}+e_{2}
, and
V_{d}
is non-degenerate if
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{L}}
. In proving Theorem
1.1
, the
2
-element generating set for the naturally embedded classical subgroup of type
{\mathbf{Y}}
will form an
(e_{1},e_{2})
-ppd stingray duo with each
e_{i}\in[\log n,2\log n]
and with natural module
V_{d}
, and we will apply Theorem
1.3
below to obtain the probability bound for generation, as a function of
d
and
q
(see Section
13
). As we observe from Table
1
, the type
{\mathbf{Y}}
is usually the same as
{\mathbf{X}}
but may be different, notably when
{\bf X}={\bf Sp}
and
q
is even. The reason for this difference is explained in Lemma
2.8
, namely, whenever the stingray duo generates a group acting irreducibly on
V_{d}
, the group generated is contained in an orthogonal subgroup of
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{d}(q)
.
For the last line of Table
1
we note that, when
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}
, then each
g_{i}
in the stingray duo acts irreducibly on the space
U_{g_{i}}
and this forces
U_{g_{i}}
to have minus type (Lemma
2.5
), and the non-degenerate direct sum
V_{d}=U_{g_{1}}\oplus U_{g_{2}}
can have type
\varepsilon^{\prime}\in\{+,-\}
(not necessarily equal to
\varepsilon
). We now introduce the concept of generating duos and their proportion; these are fundamental for Theorem
1.3
.

Definition 1.2
.

Let
G
be a finite classical group of type
({\mathbf{X}},d,|{\mathbb{F}}|)
as in one of the lines of Table
1
, and for
i=1,2
let
g_{i}^{G}
be a
G
-conjugacy class of
e_{i}
-ppd stingray elements, such that
e_{1}+e_{2}=d
(Definition
2.2
).
We say that an
(e_{1},e_{2})
-ppd stingray duo

(g,g^{\prime})\in g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}
is a
generating stingray duo
if
\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle
contains
\Omega\textup{{\bf Y}}_{d}(q)
with the type
{\mathbf{Y}}
as in the line for
{\mathbf{X}}
of Table
1
.
The
proportion of generating stingray duos
in
g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}
is defined as

(1)

\rho_{\mathrm{gen}}(g_{1},g_{2},G)\coloneq\frac{\mbox{Number of generating stingray duos in}\ g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}}{\mbox{Number of stingray duos in}\ g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}}.

Theorem 1.3
.

Suppose that
G
is a classical group of type
({\mathbf{X}},d,|{\mathbb{F}}|)
on
V
as in Table
1
, and that
e_{1},e_{2}
are integers such that
2\leqslant e_{2}\leqslant e_{1}
and
d=e_{1}+e_{2}>8
. If, for
i=1,2
,
g_{i}
is an
e_{i}
-ppd stingray element in
G
, then the proportion
\rho_{\mathrm{gen}}(g_{1},g_{2},G)
in (
1
) satisfies

\rho_{\mathrm{gen}}(g_{1},g_{2},G)\geqslant 1-\lambda_{\mathbf{X}}\cdot(q^{-1}+q^{-2})-\kappa_{{\mathbf{X}}}(q)\cdot q^{-d+3},

where
\lambda_{\mathbf{X}}=1
if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
and otherwise
\lambda_{\mathbf{X}}=0
and
\kappa_{{\mathbf{X}}}(q)
is given in Table
2
.
Moreover,
\rho_{\mathrm{gen}}(g_{1},g_{2},G)>0.983
unless either
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
or
({\mathbf{X}},q,e_{2})=({\mathbf{O}}^{+},2,2)
, and for these exceptions
\rho_{\mathrm{gen}}(g_{1},g_{2},G)
is greater than
0.885
if
({\mathbf{X}},q,e_{2})=({\mathbf{O}}^{+},2,2)
, or
0.555
if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
with
q\geqslant 3
, or
0.248
if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
with
q=2
.

In Section
3
we discuss our strategy for proving Theorem
1.3
, based on the Aschbacher subdivision
[
1
]
of maximal subgroups of the finite classical groups into nine families. We then consider these families in separate sections, and finally draw together the results in Section
13
to give a proof of Theorem
1.3
.

1.1.
A brief commentary on Theorem
1.3

(a) We treat the generic case where
|\mathbb{F}|^{e_{i}}-1
has a primitive prime divisor for each
i
, so the dimension
e_{1}+e_{2}
is assumed to be not too small (we assume that
e_{1}+e_{2}>8
). This assumption facilitates our analysis as it allows us to apply powerful results such as those in
[
15
,
17
]
which depend on the finite simple group classification.

(b) Part of the motivation for studying naturally embedded subgroups of finite classical groups comes from the need for explicit probability bounds to justify complexity analyses for new classical recognition algorithms. The bounds we obtain have indeed been adopted in the design of these new algorithms. Moreover the algorithms have been implemented, and early tests indicate that they work exceptionally well for classical groups in all characteristics, even in higher dimensions and over large fields, see Rademacher’s thesis
[
34
]
and
[
18
]
.

(c)
A special case of Theorem
1.3
was proved by Seress, Yalçinkaya and the third author in
[
33
, Theorem 2]
, namely where
e_{1}=e_{2}
and the random stingray elements are conjugate ppd-elements. This special case was fundamental in the analysis of the algorithm in
[
8
]
. Experiments conducted by Lübeck in connection with that algorithm suggested that the two stingray generators could be chosen independently and still generate a naturally embedded classical subgroup with high probability. This possibility was conjectured formally in
[
33
, Conjecture 1]
. Thus Theorem
1.3
confirms
[
33
, Conjecture 1]
.

Finally we note two additional points of difference between
[
33
, Theorem 2]
and Theorem
1.3
. Firstly,
[
33
, Theorem 2]
estimates the fraction

\frac{\mbox{Number of generating stingray duos in}\ g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}}{|g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}|}

while Theorem
1.3
gives a lower bound for
\rho_{\mathrm{gen}}(g_{1},g_{2},G)
as in (
1
) (note the different denominators for these proportions).
Secondly, Theorem
1.3
deals with all field sizes whereas
[
33
, Theorem 2]
requires
q\geqslant 3
for the orthogonal case. In this regard we note that
[
33
, Theorem 2]
, which was used to analyse the complexity of the algorithm in
[
8
]
, was stated in
[
8
]
only for
q>4
with
q
even, since the result in the then-available preprint of
[
33
]
was proved only for
q>4
, see
[
8
, Theorem 5.1]
. However, by the time the paper
[
33
]
was published, its main result
[
33
, Theorem 2]
had been proved for all
q\geqslant 3
.

1.2.
Naturally embedded subgroups for other finite simple groups

The major results of the paper address the question of constructing a naturally embedded classical subgroup
H
of a finite
n
-dimensional classical group with
H
of dimension
O(\log n)
.
We hope that there might be analogous generation results for subgroups of finite exceptional groups of Lie type which play a similar role to naturally embedded subgroups of finite classical groups, and which could be exploited computationally.

Here we discuss briefly the analogous question of constructing naturally embedded subgroups
A_{k}
of an alternating group
G=A_{n}
with
k
‘roughly’
\log n
. Let
\Omega=\{1,\dots,n\}
denote the set on which
G
acts naturally.

Step 1:
By
[
16
, Theorem 1]
,
for
n
sufficiently large, the proportion of elements of
G=A_{n}
for which some power is a
p
-cycle for a prime
p
between
\log n
and
(\log n)^{\log\log n}
, is at least
1-7/\log\log n
. By a
p
-cycle, we mean an element with one cycle of length
p
and
n-p
fixed points in
\Omega
. Thus after a constant number of independent random selections we can construct, with high probability, two elements
g
and
h
such that
g
is a
p
-cycle and
h
is an
r
-cycle, for primes
p,r
such that
\log n\leqslant p\leqslant r\leqslant(\log n)^{\log\log n}
. Suppose we have found such elements
g,h
, and let
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}
denote the
supports
of
g,h
respectively, that is, the sets of points forming the
p
-cycle of
g
and the
r
-cycle of
h
.

Step 2:
If
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}
are not disjoint or, equivalently, if
|\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}|<p+r
, then there is a good chance that
g,h
generate a naturally embedded alternating group
A_{k}
, as the next result shows.

Lemma 1.4
.

Let
G=A_{n}
and suppose that
g,h\in G
are a
p
-cycle and an
r
-cycle, with supports
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}
, respectively, where
p,r
are primes such that
p\leqslant r\leqslant n
. Suppose also that
k:=|\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}|
satisfies
\max\{p+3,r\}\leqslant k\leqslant\min\{p+r-1,n\}
. Then
H:=\langle g,h\rangle\cong A_{k}
is naturally embedded in
G
.

Proof.

Note that
H\leqslant A_{k}
since
g,h\in A_{n}
, and that
p\geqslant 3
as
A_{n}
has no 2-cycles.
Since
k\leqslant p+r-1
, the support intersection
\Delta\cap\Delta^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
, with
\ell:=|\Delta\cap\Delta^{\prime}|=p+r-k>0
. It follows that
H
is transitive on its support
\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}
, of cardinality
k
. Also
k=p+r-\ell<p+r\leqslant 2r
, and hence
h
acts primitively on its support
\Delta^{\prime}
of size
r>k/2
, and we conclude that
H
must be primitive on
\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}
. Then, as
k\geqslant p+3
, the
p
-cycle
g\in H
fixes at least
3
points of
\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}
and hence, by a theorem of Jordan, see for example,
[
10
, Theorem 3.3E]
, we conclude that
H=A_{k}
is naturally embedded in
G
.
∎

We note that this result is ‘sharp’ in a number of respects. For example, if
k=p+r
then
\Delta\cap\Delta^{\prime}=\varnothing
, and the group
H=\langle g,h\rangle\cong C_{p}\times C_{r}
acts intransitively on
\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}
. Also, if
k=p+2
with
p=r=2^{f}-1
(a Mersenne prime), then it is possible to have
H=\langle g,h\rangle\cong{\mathrm{PSL}}(2,2^{f})
in its
3
-transitive action on the projective line
\mathrm{PG}(1,2^{f})
.

Step 3:
If we are ‘lucky’, we can apply Lemma
1.4
immediately and find the naturally embedded alternating subgroup
H=\langle g,h\rangle=A_{k}
. However, since the primes
p,r
in Step 1 are at most
(\log n)^{\log\log n}
, it is likely that the supports
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}
are disjoint so that, as we noted above, the group
H=\langle g,h\rangle\cong C_{p}\times C_{r}
. In this case we try to conjugate
g
by a random element
x\in G=A_{n}
to obtain a conjugate
g^{x}
with support
\Delta^{x}
that intersects
\Delta^{\prime}
appropriately. In particular, if
|\Delta^{x}\cap\Delta^{\prime}|=1
then

k=|\Delta^{x}\cup\Delta^{\prime}|=p+r-1
, which is at least
\max\{p+3,r\}
(unless
p=r=3\geqslant\log n
, but this only holds for
n\leqslant 20
). Thus, whenever
n>20
, the conditions of Lemma
1.4
would hold for such a conjugate yielding a naturally embedded alternating subgroup
\langle g^{x},h\rangle=A_{p+r-1}
. By Lemma
1.5
below, we would find a conjugating element
x
with this property, with high probability, among
O(n/(\log n)^{2})
independently selected random elements.

Lemma 1.5
.

Let
G=A_{n}
and suppose that
g,h\in G
are a
p
-cycle and an
r
-cycle, with supports
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}
, respectively, where
p,r
are primes such that
p\leqslant r
and
p+r<n.
Suppose further that
\Delta\cap\Delta^{\prime}=\varnothing
.
Then

\frac{|\{x\in A_{n}\mid|\Delta^{x}\cap\Delta^{\prime}|=1\}|}{|A_{n}|}>\frac{rp(n-rp)}{(n-p+2)^{2}}.

In particular, if
\log n\leqslant p\leqslant r
and
pr\leqslant n/2
, then this proportion is greater than
(\log n)^{2}/2n
.

Proof.

Let
S:=\{x\in A_{n}\mid|\Delta^{x}\cap\Delta^{\prime}|=1\}
.
Then
x\in S
if and only if there is exactly one
i\in\Delta
such that
i^{x}\in\Delta^{\prime}
.

We count the number of such permutations
x\in S
.
There are
p
choices for the point
i
, and as
i^{x}\in\Delta^{\prime}
, there are
r
choices for
i^{x}
.
Each of the remaining
p-1
points in
\Delta
must be mapped to a point in
\{1,\ldots,n\}\backslash\Delta^{\prime}.
Thus there are

pr(n-r)(n-r-1)\cdots(n-r-(p-2))

choices for the image of
\Delta
under
x
.
Each of the points not in
\Delta
can be mapped to any point of
\{1,\ldots,n\}\backslash\Delta^{x}.

Thus, since
x
must lie in
A_{n}
, the total number is

|S|=\frac{1}{2}\cdot rp\left(\prod_{i=0}^{p-2}(n-r-i)\right)\cdot(n-p)!.

In particular,

\displaystyle\frac{|S|}{|A_{n}|}

\displaystyle=\frac{rp}{n-p+1}\prod_{i=0}^{p-2}\frac{n-r-i}{n-i}=\frac{rp}{n-p+1}\prod_{i=0}^{p-2}\left(1-\frac{r}{n-i}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{rp}{n-p+1}\left(1-\sum_{i=0}^{p-2}\frac{r}{n-i}\right)\geqslant\frac{rp}{n-p+2}\left(1-r\frac{p-1}{n-p+2}\right)

\displaystyle>\frac{rp}{(n-p+2)^{2}}(n-(r+1)(p-1))>\frac{rp(n-rp)}{(n-p+2)^{2}}

proving the first assertion. Note that
\frac{rp(n-rp)}{(n-p+2)^{2}}>rp(n-rp)n^{-2}
, and if
rp\leqslant n/2
this is at least
rp/2n
. If also
r\geqslant p\geqslant\log n
, then
|S|/|A_{n}|>(\log n)^{2}/2n
.
∎

2.
Groups and stingray elements

Let
q=p^{a}
be a power of a prime
p
and integer
a\geqslant 1
, and let
d
be a positive integer. Throughout, we make the following assumptions about the group
G
.

Hypothesis 2.1
.

We assume that
G
is a classical group of type
{\bf X}={\bf L},{\bf U},{\bf Sp}
, or
{\bf O}^{\varepsilon}
with
\varepsilon=\pm
, and satisfying

\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)\trianglelefteqslant G\leqslant\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
acting naturally
on
V={\mathbb{F}}^{d}
, where
d>8
and

\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q),\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q),|{\mathbb{F}}|=q^{u}
, are as in Table
1
. Note, in particular:

•

if
{\bf X}\neq{\bf L}
then
d
is even;

•

if
{\bf X}\neq{\bf U}
then
u=1
, while if
{\bf X}={\bf U}
then
u=2
and we view
{\mathrm{GU}}_{d}(q)
as a subgroup of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q^{2})
, and hence
{\mathrm{SU}}_{d}(q)
as a subgroup of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{d}(q^{2})
.

Next we formalise the concepts of stingray elements and stingray duos. In the light of Hypothesis
2.1
we
consider actions of elements of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q^{u})
on
{\mathbb{F}}^{d}_{q^{u}}
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
d,e,a
be integers such that
1\leqslant e\leqslant d
and
q=p^{a}
where
p

is prime. Let
u\in\{1,2\}
, and consider
{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q^{u})
acting naturally on the module
V={\mathbb{F}}^{d}_{q^{u}}
.

(a)

A
primitive prime divisor
of
(q^{u})^{e}-1
is a prime
r

dividing
(q^{u})^{e}-1
such that
r
does not divide
(q^{u})^{i}-1
for any
i
such that
1\leqslant i<e
;
we sometimes call such a prime an
e
-ppd prime
. Note that
q^{u}
has order

e
modulo
r
and hence
e
divides
r-1
; equivalently
r=ke+1
for some
k\geqslant 1.

(b)

Let
r
be a primitive prime divisor of
(q^{u})^{e}-1
. Then an element
g\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q^{u})
of order
a multiple of
r
is called an
e
-ppd element
.

(c)

An element
g\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q^{u})
is called an
e
-stingray element
if
g
preserves a
decomposition
V=U_{g}\oplus F_{g}
, where
F_{g}
is the
fixed point subspace of
g
and
g
acts
irreducibly (and nontrivially) on
U_{g}
with
\dim(U_{g})=e
. In particular
|g|\mid(q^{u})^{e}-1
.
An
e
-stingray element
g
of order a multiple of an
e
-ppd prime
r
is called
an
e
-ppd stingray element
(and in most of this paper
|g|
is equal to the
e
-ppd prime
r
).

(d)

For positive integers
e_{1},e_{2}
such that
e_{1}+e_{2}=d
, a pair
(g_{1},g_{2})
is an
(e_{1},e_{2})
-stingray duo
, if
e_{1}\geqslant e_{2}
and,
for
i=1,2
, the element
g_{i}\in G
is an
e_{i}
-stingray element and, moreover,

U_{g_{1}}+U_{g_{2}}=V
, or equivalently,
U_{g_{1}}\cap U_{g_{2}}=0
. Further,
(g_{1},g_{2})

is an
(e_{1},e_{2})
-ppd stingray duo
if, for each
i
,
g_{i}
is an
e_{i}
-ppd stingray element.

Remark 2.3
.

(a) For Theorem
1.3
, we are concerned with
(e_{1},e_{2})
-ppd stingray duos in a
d
-dimensional classical subgroup of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q^{u})
, where
d=e_{1}+e_{2}
, so we will be assuming that
(q^{u})^{e_{i}}-1
has a primitive prime divisor, for each
i=1,2
. Such primes are known to exist for almost all values of
q^{u},e_{i}
, the exceptions being
(q^{u},e_{i})=(2,6)
, and the case
e_{i}=2
with
q^{u}+1
a power of
2
, see
[
3
, Section 1.13]
. Thus, for example, if
e_{i}=2
then we will require
q^{u}\not\in\{3,7,31,127,\dots\}
.

(b) In particular if
e\geqslant 2
then each primitive prime divisor of
(q^{u})^{e}-1
is an odd prime.

(c) For an
e
-ppd element
g\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q^{u})
as in Definition
2.2
(b), if
|g|
is coprime to
p
then
V={\mathbb{F}}_{q^{u}}^{d}
has the form

V=\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}U_{i}\right)\oplus F
, where
g
fixes
F
elementwise, and fixes each
U_{i}
setwise, so
t\geqslant 1
. Moreover, if
|g|=r
, then
g
acts irreducibly (and nontrivially) on
U_{i}
and
\dim(U_{i})=e
for each
i
.

For Theorem
1.3
, the property of interest is whether or not a given
(e_{1},e_{2})
-ppd stingray duo
(g,g^{\prime})
in a
d
-dimensional classical group
G

is ‘generating’, see Definition
1.2
. If
(g,g^{\prime})
is not a generating stingray duo then we say that
(g,g^{\prime})
is
non-generating
. Our objective is to obtain a positive lower bound for the proportion
\rho_{\mathrm{gen}}(g_{1},g_{2},G)
of generating stingray duos in
g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}
, as defined in (
1
). Equivalently, we seek an upper bound strictly less than
1
for the quantity

(2)

\rho_{\mathrm{nongen}}(g_{1},g_{2},G)\coloneq\frac{\mbox{Number of non-generating stingray duos in}\ g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}}{\mbox{Number of stingray duos in}\ g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}}.

Note that
\rho_{\mathrm{gen}}(g_{1},g_{2},G)+\rho_{\mathrm{nongen}}(g_{1},g_{2},G)=1
. Moreover, in Proposition
2.6
(a) we show that the conjugacy classes
g_{i}^{G}
are independent of the group
G
satisfying
\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)\trianglelefteqslant G\leqslant\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
, and hence also the proportions of generating and non-generating stingray duos are independent of
G
. We therefore may (and usually do) write

(3)

\rho_{\mathrm{gen}}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}}):=\rho_{\mathrm{gen}}(g_{1},g_{2},G)
and
\rho_{\mathrm{nongen}}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}}):=\rho_{\mathrm{nongen}}(g_{1},g_{2},G)
.

Our strategy, which is discussed in detail in Section
3
, is to find an upper bound for
\rho_{\mathrm{nongen}}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
, and we observe that
\rho_{\mathrm{nongen}}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=\rho_{\mathrm{nongen}}(g_{1}^{\prime},g_{2}^{\prime},{\mathbf{X}})
for any choice of
g_{i}^{\prime}\in g_{i}^{G}
. For the remainder of this section we study properties of stingray elements and stingray duos in classical groups.

2.1.
Properties of Stingray Elements

We are primarily interested in stingray duos, but first we examine properties of individual stingray elements. Parts (a) and (b) of the first result were proved in
[
13
, Lemma 3.7]
. (Note that, although the hypothesis
{\bf X}\neq{\bf L}
is implicit in the hypotheses of
[
13
, Lemma 3.7]
, this is not used in the proof.) The proof of part (c) follows easily from the fact that, since
V=U_{g}\oplus F_{g}
, each subspace
W
satisfying
U_{g}\leqslant W\leqslant V
is equal to
W=U_{g}\oplus(F_{g}\cap W)
, and hence is
g
-invariant.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
g\in{\mathrm{GL}}(V)
be an
e
-stingray element, with
U_{g},F_{g}
as in Definition
2.2
. Then

(a)

0\neq v^{g}-v\in U_{g}
, for all
v\in V\backslash F_{g}
; and hence
U_{g}
is the unique
\langle g\rangle
-invariant submodule of
V
on which
g
acts non-trivially and irreducibly;

(b)

if
Z
is a
\langle g\rangle
-invariant submodule of
V
then either
Z\leqslant F_{g}
, or
U_{g}\leqslant Z
, and in the latter case the restriction
g|_{Z}
is an
e
-stingray element of
{\mathrm{GL}}(Z)
;

(c)

if
U_{g}\leqslant W\leqslant V
then
W
is
\langle g\rangle
-invariant.

The next lemma identifies a cyclic torus associated with an
e
-stingray element.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
G
be a group as in Hypothesis
2.1
of type
\bf X
,
and let
g\in G
be an
e
-stingray element with

U_{g},F_{g}
as in Definition
2.2
, where
1\leqslant e\leqslant d-1
.

(a)

If
{\bf X}\neq{\bf L}
, then
U_{g}
and
F_{g}
are
non-degenerate,
V=U_{g}\perp F_{g}
, and
U_{g}^{\perp}=F_{g}
.

(b)

Either
e=1
and
{\bf X}\in\{{\bf L},{\bf U},{\bf O}^{\varepsilon}\}
, or
e\geqslant 2
and the parity of
e
, and types of
U_{g}
and
F_{g}
are as given in
Table
3
; in particular, if
{\bf X}={\bf O}^{\varepsilon}
, then
U_{g}
has minus type.

(c)

Suppose that
({\bf X},\mbox{$e$-parity})\neq({\bf O}^{\varepsilon},\mbox{even})
. Then there is a unique cyclic torus
T
in
\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)
containing
g

such that
T
preserves the decomposition
V=U_{g}\oplus F_{g}
,
F_{g}
is
the fixed point space of
T
, and
|T|
is given in Table
3
. Moreover,

C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(g)=C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(T)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}T\times\textup{G{\bf X}}(F_{g})&\mbox{if ${\bf X}\neq{\bf O}^{\pm}$}\\
T\times{\mathrm{GO}}^{-\varepsilon}(F_{g})&\mbox{if ${\bf X}={\bf O}^{\varepsilon}$, with $\varepsilon=\pm$}\end{array}\right.

Also
g^{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}\cap T
has size
e
, and
N_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(T)
permutes this set transitively by conjugation.

Table 3.
Conditions for Lemma
2.5
when
e\geqslant 2
.

e
-parity

type of
U_{g}

type of
F_{g}

|T|

∘

,

even

q^{e/2}+1

Proof.

Part (a) is proved in
[
13
, Lemma 3.8(a)]
. If
e=1
, then the possibilities for
\bf X
follow from part (a). If
e\geqslant 2
, then the assertions in part (b) follow from
[
13
, Lemma 3.8(b)]
.

(c) If
e=1
then by part (b),
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
or
{\mathbf{U}}
, and
T:=C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(g)|_{U_{g}}\cong C_{q^{e}-1}
or
C_{q^{e}+1}
respectively. The order
|T|
may be read from
[
2
, Table 1]
. If
e\geqslant 2
, then the existence and uniqueness of
T
follows from the proof of the result
[
33
, Lemma 3.1(ii)]
by
replacing
t,W,U_{g},n
by
g,U_{g},F_{g},e
, as the proof there only used the
fact that
t\mid_{W}
(or
g\mid_{U_{g}}
in our case) is irreducible.
The arguments also extend to the case
G_{0}={\mathrm{SO}}^{\circ}_{d}(q)
as
U_{g}

is nondegenerate of minus type. Finally we examine
C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(g)
. Set
Y\coloneq\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)
. Since
T
leaves invariant the decomposition
V=U_{g}\oplus F_{g}
,
C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(g)\leqslant Y_{U_{g},F_{g}}=Y_{1}\times Y_{2}
, where

Y_{1},Y_{2}
are the isometry groups of
U_{g},F_{g}
(with types as in Table
3
). Moreover
1\times Y_{2}\leqslant C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(g)
and so
C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(g)=C_{Y_{1}}(g)\times Y_{2}
. Now
T
is a self-centralising (irreducible, cyclic) subgroup of
Y_{1}
, and as
g\in T
and
T
is cyclic, we have
C_{Y_{1}}(T)=T\leqslant C_{Y_{1}}(g)
. However since
g|_{U_{g}}
is irreducible,
C_{{\mathrm{GL}}(U_{g})}(g)
is a cyclic torus
\hat{T}
of order
q^{ue}-1
, and therefore
\hat{T}\cap Y_{1}
is a cyclic torus of
Y_{1}
containing
T
. It follows that
\hat{T}\cap Y_{1}=T=C_{Y_{1}}(g)
, and hence
C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(U_{g})}(g)=C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(U_{g})}(T)
is as in part (c).

Finally, as in the proof of
[
33
, Lemma 3.1(ii)]
, the normaliser

N_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(T)
leaves invariant
U_{g}
and
F_{g}
, and
|N_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(T)|=e\cdot|C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(T)|
; and moreover
g^{G}\cap T
has size
e
, and
N_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(T)
permutes this set transitively by conjugation.
∎

We use Lemma
2.5
to derive the important consequence that the
G
-conjugacy class
g^{G}
of an
e
-stingray element
g
is independent of the choice of
G
in Hypothesis
2.1
, and moreover a conjugacy class of
e
-ppd stingray elements is in bijection with a conjugacy class of
e
-stingray elements of
e
-ppd prime order.

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
G
be a group as in Hypothesis
2.1
of type
\bf X
, so
\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)\trianglelefteqslant G\leqslant\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
,
and let
g\in G
be an
e
-stingray element as in Definition
2.2
such that
1\leqslant e\leqslant d-1
and if
{\mathbf{X}}\in\{{\mathbf{Sp}},\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}\}
then
e
is even.
Then

(a)

G=C_{G}(g)\,\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
, and
g^{\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)}=g^{G}=g^{\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)}
is independent of
G
.

(b)

If
e\geqslant 2
and
|g|
is divisible by a primitive prime divisor
r
of
(q^{u})^{e}-1
, and if
m
is as in Table
4
then, letting
n\in\{m,|g|/r\}
and
h:=g^{n}
, the following hold:

(i)

h
is an
e
-ppd stingray element,
h\in\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}(V)
,
U_{h}=U_{g}
,
F_{h}=F_{g}
;

(ii)

C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(h)=C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(g)
, and the map
x\to x^{n}
defines a bijection
g^{G}\to h^{G}
.

Table 4.
The value of
m
for Proposition
2.6

X

L

U

Sp

O
±

m

q-1

q+1

1

2

Proof.

(a) Suppose first that
\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)=C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(g)\,\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}(V)
. Then each element
x\in G
is of the form
x=yz
for some
y\in C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(g)
and
z\in\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}(V)
. Since
G
contains
\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}(V)
the element
y=xz^{-1}\in G
and hence
y\in G\cap C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(g)=C_{G}(g)
. Thus
G=C_{G}(g)\,\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
. Moreover, we have
g^{\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)}\subseteq g^{G}\subseteq g^{\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)}=g^{C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(g)\,\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}(V)}=g^{\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)}
, and so equality holds.
Therefore to prove part (a) it suffices to prove that
G=C_{G}(g)\,\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
in the case where
G=\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)
. So assume that
G=\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)
.

For
{\bf X}={\bf L}
,
{\bf U}
or
{\bf Sp}
, the kernel of the determinant map
\det\colon\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)\to{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{u}}^{\times}
is
\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}(V)={\mathrm{S{\bf X}}}(V)
.
Lemma
2.5
(c) shows that
\det(C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(g))=\det(\textup{G{\bf X}}(F_{g}))=\det(\textup{G{\bf X}}(V))
, and so

\displaystyle|C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(g){\mathrm{S{\bf X}}}(V)|

\displaystyle=\frac{|C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(g)|\cdot|{\mathrm{S{\bf X}}}(V)|}{|C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(g)\cap{\mathrm{S{\bf X}}}(V)|}=|\det(C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(g))|\cdot|{\mathrm{S{\bf X}}}(V)|

\displaystyle=|\det(\textup{G{\bf X}}(V))|\cdot|{\mathrm{S{\bf X}}}(V)|=|\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)|.

Thus
\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)=C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(g)\,{\mathrm{S{\bf X}}}(V)
as claimed. So assume that
{\bf X}={\bf O}^{\varepsilon}
, with
\varepsilon=\pm
and
d
even (see Hypothesis
2.1
). Here
\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}(V)=\Omega^{\varepsilon}(V)
and
e
is even by assumption. Also by
[
21
, Lemma 4.1.1(i)]
and Lemma
2.5
,

1\times{\mathrm{GO}}^{-\varepsilon}(F_{g})\leqslant C_{{\mathrm{GO}}^{\varepsilon}(V)}(g)=T\times{\mathrm{GO}}^{-\varepsilon}(F_{g})\leqslant{\mathrm{GO}}^{-}(U_{g})\times{\mathrm{GO}}^{-\varepsilon}(F_{g})={\mathrm{GO}}^{\varepsilon}(V)_{U_{g}},

with
|T|=q^{e/2}+1
, and by
[
21
, Lemma 4.1.1(ii)]
,
(1\times{\mathrm{GO}}^{-\varepsilon}(F_{g}))\cap\Omega^{\varepsilon}(V)=1\times\Omega^{-\varepsilon}(F_{g})
, so

\frac{|{\mathrm{GO}}^{\varepsilon}(V)|}{|\Omega^{\varepsilon}(V)|}\geqslant\frac{|C_{{\mathrm{GO}}^{\varepsilon}(V)}(g)\Omega^{\varepsilon}(V)|}{|\Omega^{\varepsilon}(V)|}\geqslant\frac{|(1{\times}{\mathrm{GO}}^{-\varepsilon}(F_{g}))\Omega^{\varepsilon}(V)|}{|\Omega^{\varepsilon}(V)|}=\frac{|1{\times}{\mathrm{GO}}^{-\varepsilon}(F_{g})|}{|1{\times}\Omega^{-\varepsilon}(F_{g})|}=\frac{|{\mathrm{GO}}^{-\varepsilon}(F_{g})|}{|\Omega^{-\varepsilon}(F_{g})|}.

By
[
21
, Table 2.1.C]
,
|{\mathrm{GO}}^{-\varepsilon}(V)/\Omega^{-\varepsilon}(V)|=|{\mathrm{GO}}^{-\varepsilon}(F_{g})/\Omega^{-\varepsilon}(F_{g})|
is
2
if
q
is even, and
4

if
q
is odd. Since this holds for each
\varepsilon=\pm
, it follows that equality holds for all the inequalities in the above display, and in particular
|{\mathrm{GO}}^{\varepsilon}(V)|=|C_{{\mathrm{GO}}(V)}(g)\Omega^{\varepsilon}(V)|
, so
{\mathrm{GO}}^{\varepsilon}(V)=C_{{\mathrm{GO}}^{\varepsilon}(V)}(g)\,\Omega^{\varepsilon}(V)
. Thus part (a) is proved.

(b) The group
\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)/\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
has exponent
m
for
m
as in Table
4
.
(To see this for case
{\bf O}
it suffices to consider
q
odd. As
{\mathrm{GO}}^{\varepsilon}(V)
is generated by
reflections
[
35
, Corollary 11.42]
, the same is true for
{\mathrm{GO}}^{\varepsilon}(V)/\Omega^{\varepsilon}(V)
, so the exponent is
2
.) Hence
g^{m}\in\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
.
Also, since
e\geqslant 2
, the primitive prime
divisor
r
of
(q^{u})^{e}-1
does not divide
q^{u}-1
and hence
r
does not divide
m
in cases
{\bf L}
or
{\bf U}
. In addition, by Definition
2.2
(a),
r\geqslant e+1\geqslant 3
, and so
r
does not
m
in cases
{\bf Sp}
or
{\bf O^{\varepsilon}}
either. Thus in all cases
r
is
coprime to
m
and hence
m
divides
|g|/r
. This implies that
g^{|g|/r}\in\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
.
Thus we have proved that the element
h=g^{n}\in\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
, and
|h|
is divisible by
r
.

Since
h\in\langle g\rangle
,
h
fixes
F_{g}
pointwise, and leaves
U_{g}
invariant. Also since
|h|
is divisible by
r
, it follows that
h
acts irreducibly on
U_{g}
, so
h
is an
e
-ppd stingray element, and
U_{h}=U_{g}
,
F_{h}=F_{g}
. Moreover, since
h\in\langle g\rangle
, the unique cyclic torus containing
h
given by Lemma
2.5
(c) is the cyclic torus
T
containing
g
and hence
C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(g)=C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(h)
by Lemma
2.5
(c). Thus using part (a),
|g^{G}|=|g^{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}|=|\textup{G{\bf X}}(V):C_{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}(g)|=|h^{\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)}|=|h^{G}|
. The map
x\to x^{n}
clearly defines a surjection
g^{G}\to h^{G}
, and since
|g^{G}|=|h^{G}|
, this map is a bijection.
∎

In our analysis we frequently need to know the number of conjugates of certain overgroups of stingray elements. The final lemma of this subsection is very helpful for this.

Lemma 2.7
.

Suppose that
G=\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
of type
{\mathbf{X}}
with a proper subgroup
M
such that
N_{G}(M)=M
. If
g\in M
is a stingray element such that
g^{G}\cap M
is the single
M
-conjugacy class
g^{M}
, then

|\{L\in M^{G}\mid g\in L\}|=\frac{|C_{G}(g)|}{|C_{M}(g)|}

with
C_{G}(g)
given by Lemma
\ref{lem:uperp}{\rm(c)}
.

Proof.

Let
Y
denote the number of conjugates
L\in M^{G}
containing
g
, and note that
|M^{G}|=|G|/|M|
(since
N_{G}(M)=M
by assumption).
Then, the number of pairs
(L,g^{\prime})
such that
L\in M^{G}
,
g^{\prime}\in g^{G}
and
g^{\prime}\in L
, is equal to the number
|g^{G}|
of choices for
g^{\prime}
times
Y
. Also the number of these pairs is equal to the number
|M^{G}|=|G|/|M|
of choices for the group
L
times the number
|g^{G}\cap L|=|g^{G}\cap M|=|g^{M}|
of choices for
g^{\prime}
, for a given group
L
. Thus

Y=\frac{|M^{G}|\cdot|g^{M}|}{|g^{G}|}=\frac{|G|}{|M|}\cdot\frac{|M|}{|C_{M}(g)|}\cdot\frac{|C_{G}(g)|}{|G|}=\frac{|C_{G}(g)|}{|C_{M}(g)|}

and we recall that
C_{G}(g)
is given by Lemma
2.5
(c).
∎

Table 5.
Conditions for
(e_{1},e_{2})
-stingray duos in classical groups.

X

e_{i}
-parity

e_{i}
-range

d=e_{1}+e_{2}
and
U_{i}=V(g_{i}-1)

L

any

1\leqslant e_{2}\leqslant e_{1}\leqslant d-1

d
odd or even

U

odd

1\leqslant e_{2}\leqslant e_{1}\leqslant d-1

d

even

2\leqslant e_{2}\leqslant e_{1}\leqslant d-2

d
even

±

even

2\leqslant e_{2}\leqslant e_{1}\leqslant d-2

d
even, each
U_{i}
has minus type

2.2.
Stingray duos in classical groups

Let
(g_{1},g_{2})
be an
(e_{1},e_{2})
-stingray duo, as in Definition
2.2
, for a classical group of type
\bf X
contained in
{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q^{u})
, so
d=e_{1}+e_{2}
and
e_{1}\geqslant e_{2}\geqslant 1
. (See our comments later in this paragraph for why we allow
e_{2}=1
, especially in analysing the Aschbacher class
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{3}
.) We assume that
\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)\leqslant G\leqslant\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
and that
g_{1},g_{2}
are stingray elements in
\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
, not necessarily lying in
G
.
By Lemma
2.5
the value
e_{i}=1
may occur only for
{\bf X}\in\{{\bf L},{\bf U},{\bf O}^{\pm}\}
;
and if
{\bf X}=\bf O^{\pm}
then we assume that both of the
e_{i}
are even so that, in particular,
d=e_{1}+e_{2}
is even (and we do not consider
(e_{1},e_{2})
-stingray duos in
{\mathrm{GO}}_{d}^{\circ}(q)
with
d
odd). For convenience we summarise the parity and range of the
e_{i}
in Table
5
, based on these comments and Table
3
.
In Theorem
1.3
we assume further that
d=e_{1}+e_{2}
with each
e_{i}\geqslant 2
, and
d>8
. However in parts of our analysis we need to deal with classical groups of smaller dimension and duos with smaller
e_{i}
, for example, when considering classical subgroups over extension fields. Thus we assume that
e_{1}\geqslant e_{2}
in the discussion below, and often allow the possibility of
e_{2}=1
for types
{\bf X}\in\{{\bf L},{\bf U}\}
.

Our general aim is to show that, with high probability, an
(e_{1},e_{2})
-stingray duo generates a group containing
\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
, but, as we discussed in the paragraph before Definition
1.2
, the case of symplectic groups with
q
even is exceptional in this regard. The next lemma is a generalisation of
[
33
, Lemma 3.3]
, and justifies our definition of generating stingray duo in Definition
1.2
.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
G={\mathrm{Sp}}_{d}(q)={\mathrm{GSp}}_{d}(q)
, where
q
is even and
d=e_{1}+e_{2}
with each
e_{i}
even, and let
(g_{1},g_{2})
be an
(e_{1},e_{2})
-stingray duo in
G
as in Definition
2.2
. Then either
\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle
is reducible, or
\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle\leqslant{\mathrm{SO}}^{\varepsilon}_{d}(q)
for some
\varepsilon=\pm
.

Proof.

Let
H:=\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle
, and for each
i
let
U_{i}:=U_{g_{i}}
and
F_{i}:=F_{g_{i}}
as in Definition
2.2
, so that
V=U_{i}\oplus F_{i}
and
V=U_{1}\oplus U_{2}
.
Since
q
is even,
G={\mathrm{Sp}}_{d}(q)\cong\Omega_{d+1}(q)
. Let
\widehat{V}
be the associated orthogonal space of dimension
d+1
for
G
with nonsingular
1
-dimensional radical
R
and
V=\widehat{V}/R
. Then for each
i
,
g_{i}
acts as an
e_{i}
-stingray element on
\widehat{V}
, and by Lemmas
2.4
and
2.5
,
g_{i}
acts irreducibly on an
e_{i}
-dimensional subspace
\widehat{U}_{i}
of
\widehat{V}
and has an
(d-e_{i}+1)
-dimensional fixed point subspace
\widehat{F}_{i}
containing
R
(recall that
e_{i}\geqslant 2
). Thus
U_{i}=(\widehat{U}_{i}+R)/R
for each
i
, and
W\coloneq\widehat{U}_{1}+\widehat{U}_{2}
has dimension at most
e_{1}+e_{2}=d
. Since
V=U_{1}\oplus U_{2}
it follows that
\widehat{V}=(\widehat{U}_{1}+\widehat{U}_{2})\oplus R
, and
W=\widehat{U}_{1}\oplus\widehat{U}_{2}
is a hyperplane of
\widehat{V}
which intersects
R
trivially.
Moreover, by Lemma
2.4
(c),
W
is invariant under both
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
, and hence
H
is contained in the stabiliser in
G
of
W
. If
W
is a nondegenerate subspace of
\widehat{V}
, then the stabiliser of
W
is an orthogonal group and we have
H\leqslant{\mathrm{GO}}^{\varepsilon}_{d}(q)
for some
\varepsilon=\pm
, and since
g_{1},g_{2}\in{\mathrm{Sp}}_{d}(q)\leqslant{\mathrm{SL}}_{d}(q)
the subgroup
H=\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle\leqslant{\mathrm{SO}}_{d}^{\varepsilon}(q)
. On the other hand if
W
is degenerate then
H
leaves invariant the nonzero totally isotropic subspace
((W\cap W^{\perp})+R)/R
of
V
.
∎

We first record some basic properties about ppd stingray duos which allow us to reduce to the case where these elements have prime orders and lie in
\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
, and where the group
G
is
\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
G
be a classical group of type
X
satisfying

\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)\trianglelefteqslant G\leqslant\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
as in Hypothesis
2.1
, and
let
(g_{1},g_{2})
be an
(e_{1},e_{2})
-ppd stingray duo in
\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)\times\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
with
e_{1},e_{2}
as in Table
5
with
e_{1}\geqslant e_{2}\geqslant 2
. For each
i
, let
|g_{i}|
be a multiple of an
e_{i}
-ppd
r_{i}
of
q^{ue_{i}}-1
, and let
h_{i}=g_{i}^{|g_{i}|/r_{i}}
. Then the following hold.

(a)

The proportions defined in (
1
) and (
2
) satisfy

\displaystyle\rho_{gen}(g_{1},g_{2},\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q))

\displaystyle=\rho_{gen}(g_{1},g_{2},G)=\rho_{gen}(g_{1},g_{2},\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q))

\displaystyle\rho_{nongen}(g_{1},g_{2},\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q))

\displaystyle=\rho_{nongen}(g_{1},g_{2},G)=\rho_{nongen}(g_{1},g_{2},\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)),

so we write these proportions as
\rho_{gen}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
and
\rho_{nongen}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
, respectively.

(b)

Both
h_{i}
lie in
\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
where
h_{i}^{\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)}=h_{i}^{G}=h_{i}^{\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)}
, and
(h_{1},h_{2})
is an
(e_{1},e_{2})
-ppd stingray duo.

(c)

Moreover if
(h_{1},h_{2})
is a generating stingray duo, then
(g_{1},g_{2})
is also a generating stingray duo, so

\rho_{\mathrm{gen}}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\geqslant\rho_{\mathrm{gen}}(h_{1},h_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\rho_{\mathrm{nongen}}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant\rho_{\mathrm{nongen}}(h_{1},h_{2},{\mathbf{X}}).

Proof.

Applying Proposition
2.6
(a) to both
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
, the numerators and the denominators of the fractions defining
\rho_{gen}
and
\rho_{nongen}
in (
1
) and (
2
) are independent of
G
. This implies equality of the proportions asserted in part (a).

Part (b) follows immediately from Proposition
2.6
. Moreover we see from Proposition
2.6
that, for each
i
, the map
x\to x^{|g_{i}|/r_{i}}
is a bijection
g_{i}^{G}\to h_{i}^{G}
. It follows that the map

(x,y)\to(x^{|g_{i}|/r_{1}},y^{|g_{i}|/r_{2}})
is a bijection
g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}\to h_{1}^{G}\times h_{2}^{G}
that maps stingray duos to stingray duos; and, further, the domain and range are independent of the choice of the group
G
.

Suppose that
(h_{1},h_{2})
is a generating stingray duo, so by Definition
1.2
either
\langle h_{1},h_{2}\rangle=\Omega X_{d}(q)
, or
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
and
q
is even, and
\langle h_{1},h_{2}\rangle
is an orthogonal subgroup of
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{d}(q)
. Then also
\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle
contains
\Omega X_{d}(q)
, or
\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle
contains an orthogonal subgroup, respectively, so
(g_{1},g_{2})
is a generating stingray duo. Finally, the assertions about the proportions follow since the numbers of stingray duos in
g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}
and in
h_{1}^{G}\times h_{2}^{G}
are equal.
∎

3.
Strategy for proving Theorem
1.3

Let
G\leqslant{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q^{u})
be a classical group of type
\bf X
acting on the vector space
V=({\mathbb{F}}_{q^{u}})^{d}
, as in Hypothesis
2.1
, and let
(g_{1},g_{2})
be an
(e_{1},e_{2})
-ppd stingray duo for
G
, as in Definition
2.2
.
As discussed in Section
2
, to prove Theorem
1.3
, our objective is to obtain an upper bound strictly less than
1
for the proportion of non-generating stingray duos in
g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}
, namely for the quantity
\rho_{\mathrm{nongen}}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
defined in (
3
) (see also (
2
) and Lemma
2.9
(a)).
Clearly these proportions are independent of the choices of the elements
g_{1},g_{2}
in their
G
-conjugacy classes
g_{1}^{G},g_{2}^{G}
, and by Proposition
2.6
(a), these conjugacy classes are independent of the group
G
in the range
\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)\trianglelefteqslant G\leqslant\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
. Also, if
|g_{i}|
is a multiple of an
e_{i}
-ppd
r_{i}
of
(q^{u})^{e_{i}}-1
, then by Lemma
2.9
, it is sufficient to prove the theorem, replacing each
g_{i}
by
h_{i}:=g_{i}^{|g_{i}|/r_{i}}
, an element of
e_{i}
-ppd prime order
r_{i}
lying in
\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
. Thus we may (and shall) henceforth assume the following.

Hypothesis 3.1
.

(a)

V=({\mathbb{F}}_{q^{u}})^{d}
, and
G=\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
of type
{\mathbf{X}}
, with
{\mathbf{X}},u,\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
as in one of the lines of Table
1
.

(b)

d=e_{1}+e_{2}
with
2\leqslant e_{2}\leqslant e_{1}
as in Table
5
(usually
d>8
but this will often be specified explicitly). For
i=1,2
,
(q^{u})^{e_{i}}-1
has a primitive prime divisor
r_{i}
.

(c)

For
i=1,2
,
g_{i}\in G
is an
e_{i}
-ppd stingray element of order
r_{i}
, in particular
g_{i}\in\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
.

(d)

We usually use
(g,g^{\prime})
to denote a typical non-generating stingray duo with
g\in g_{1}^{G},g^{\prime}\in g_{2}^{G}
, so
H\coloneq\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle
is a proper subgroup of
\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
, and
H
is not an orthogonal group if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
with
q
even, see Lemma
2.8
.

Our strategy for obtaining a useful upper bound for
\rho_{\mathrm{nongen}}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
is to
identify a collection
\mathcal{M}({\mathbf{X}})
of proper subgroups of
G=\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
, usually (but not always) maximal subgroups, such that, for each non-generating stingray duo
(g,g^{\prime})
,
H=\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle\leqslant M\cap\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
for at least one
M\in{\mathcal{M}}({\mathbf{X}})
. Then we have

(4)

\rho_{\mathrm{nongen}}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=\frac{\mid\bigcup_{M\in{\mathcal{M}}({\mathbf{X}})}\{\mbox{stingray duos $(g,g^{\prime})$ in}\ g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}\ \mbox{with $\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle\leqslant M$}\}|}{\mbox{Number of stingray duos in}\ g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}}

By Aschbacher’s Theorem
[
1
]
, maximal subgroups of
G
not containing
\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)

belong to one of nine ‘categories’ (families of subgroups) denoted
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}
.
Our standard reference is the book of Kleidman and Liebeck
[
21
]
(where the exact
conditions are given for a subgroup in category
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{i}
to be maximal in
G
, for dimension
d\geqslant 13
).
Sometimes the categories
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{i}
are enriched to contain some non-maximal subgroups (sometimes all the subgroups of the maximal ones) or, for example in the case
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{L}}
and
e_{1}=d/2
,
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{1}
might include the stabilisers of all totally isotropic or non-degenerate
d/2
-subspaces even though the stabiliser
M
of a
d/2
-dimensional non-degenerate subspace
U
might be a proper subgroup of the stabiliser of the decomposition
V=U\oplus U^{\perp}
, so
M
has index two in
N_{G}(M)
.

Remark 3.2
.

(a)

In summary, the subgroups in
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathbf{X}})
will be maximal among the elements of
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{i}
, for various
i
, but are not necessarily maximal in
G
.
Thus the collection
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathbf{X}})
has a natural partition as a disjoint union
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathbf{X}})=\cup_{i=1}^{9}{\mathcal{M}}_{i}({\mathbf{X}})
with
{\mathcal{M}}_{i}({\mathbf{X}})\subseteq\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{i}
for each
i
.

(b)

The case
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
with
q
even (so
G={\mathrm{Sp}}_{d}(q)=\Omega\textup{{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
) deserves special mention. By Lemma
2.8
,
H\leqslant M
, where either
M
is a maximal reducible subgroup of
G
or
M
is a maximal (proper) subgroup of some subgroup
\Omega_{d}^{\pm}(q)
of
G
. In particular, it follows from Aschbacher’s Theorem
[
1
]
in this case that
H\leqslant M
for some
M\in\cup_{i\neq 8}\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{i}
, and hence we may assume that
{\mathcal{M}}_{8}({\mathbf{Sp}})=\emptyset
if
q
is even. Also in this case,
{\mathcal{M}}_{9}({\mathbf{Sp}})
will consist of the
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}
-subgroups of an orthogonal subgroup
\Omega_{d}^{\pm}(q)
of
G
.

We often approximate the contribution

\frac{\mid\bigcup_{M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{i}({\mathbf{X}})}\{\mbox{stingray duos $(g,g^{\prime})$ in}\ g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}\ \mbox{with $\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle\leqslant M$}\}|}{\mbox{Number of stingray duos in}\ g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}}

to
\rho_{\mathrm{nongen}}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
in (
4
) from subgroups in
{\mathcal{M}}_{i}({\mathbf{X}})
by the upper bound

\sum_{M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{i}({\mathbf{X}})}\frac{\mid\{\mbox{stingray duos $(g,g^{\prime})$ in}\ g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}\ \mbox{with $\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle\leqslant M$}\}|}{\mbox{Number of stingray duos in}\ g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}}.

We use several strategies to reduce this upper bound to avoid too much over-counting. Firstly, we try to choose the subsets
{\mathcal{M}}_{i}({\mathbf{X}})
as small as possible subject to the following: if
H
is contained in some subgroup lying in
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{i}
, for some
i
, then we must ensure
that there exists some
M\in\cup_{j=1}^{i}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}({\mathbf{X}})
with
H\leqslant M
. However, the set

{\mathcal{M}}_{i}({\mathbf{X}})
will not usually contain all the subgroups in
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{i}
, not even all those containing
H
. For example, if
i=1
and
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{L}}
, then we will see in Lemma
5.2
that the only maximal subgroups in
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{1}
containing an
(e_{1},e_{2})
-ppd stingray duo are stabilisers of nondegenerate
e_{j}
-subspaces (for
j=1,2
). Thus we may take

{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathbf{X}})
to consist of the stabilisers of non-degenerate subspaces of dimensions
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
(and no other subgroups). Secondly, we analyse the categories in order so that, by the time we consider cases where
H
is contained in a subgroup of
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{i}
, we may assume that
H
is not contained in any subgroup in
\cup_{1\leqslant j<i}\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{j}
.
To facilitate choosing the
{\mathcal{M}}_{i}({\mathbf{X}})
with this property, we define:

(5)

{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{\ast}({\mathbf{X}})=\emptyset,\quad\mbox{and for $1<i\leqslant 9$,}\quad{\mathcal{M}}_{i}^{\ast}({\mathbf{X}})=\cup_{1\leqslant j<i}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}({\mathbf{X}}).

Moreover, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant 9
, we let

(6)

\mathcal{S}_{i}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=\bigcup_{M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{i}({\mathbf{X}})}\left\{\mbox{duos $(g,g^{\prime})$ in $g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}$}\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{lll}&\mbox{$\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle\leqslant M$ but $\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle\nleq L$}\\
&\quad\qquad\mbox{for any $L\in{\mathcal{M}}_{i}^{\ast}({\mathbf{X}})$}\end{array}\right\}

and we define

(7)

{\rm Prob}_{i}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=\frac{\mid\mathcal{S}_{i}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\mid}{\mbox{Number of stingray duos in}\ g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}}.

We therefore have

(8)

\rho_{\mathrm{nongen}}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=\sum_{i=1}^{9}{\rm Prob}_{i}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}}).

We will find upper bounds for each
{\rm Prob}_{i}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
as a function of
d
and
q
. We make a few comments about the analysis.

•

For some subgroups
M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{i}({\mathbf{X}})
, it is possible to count the numbers of stingray duos they contain, leading to expressions for the numerator in (
7
) which allow some cancellation with the expressions for the denominator. For example, this occurs for
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{1}
with
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{L}}
in the proof of Proposition
5.3
.

•

For some
i>1
, the extra restriction that
\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle
does not lie in any subgroup from
{\mathcal{M}}_{j}({\mathbf{X}})
for
j<i
is useful. For example, for
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{2}
, we will see in Lemma
6.3
that the family
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}({\mathbf{X}})
can be restricted to maximal imprimitive subgroups of
G
preserving a decomposition of
V
as a direct sum of
d
subspaces of dimension
1
.

•

In all cases we use Lemma
3.3
and Propositions
4.2
and
4.4
to estimate the denominators in (
4
) and (
7
) (see also (
11
) below).

•

The last technique which we now describe is useful in cases where we can specify the subgroups
M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{i}(G)
containing a given
g
, see for example Proposition
5.3
(for
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{1}
) or Proposition
6.1
(for
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{2}
).
For each
i
, the family
{\mathcal{M}}_{i}({\mathbf{X}})
is a union of
G
-conjugacy classes of subgroups, and hence the set
\mathcal{S}_{i}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
in (
6
) is closed under conjugation so that the number of stingray duos in
\mathcal{S}_{i}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
with first entry
g\in g_{1}^{G}
is independent of the choice of
g
.
Therefore, for
M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{i}({\mathbf{X}})
such that
g\in g_{1}^{G}\cap M
, setting

(9)

\mathcal{S}_{i}(g,M)=\left\{g^{\prime}\in g_{2}^{G}\cap M\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{lll}&\mbox{$(g,g^{\prime})$ a stingray duo, $\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle\leqslant M$, but }\\
&\quad\quad\quad\mbox{$\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle\not\leqslant L$ for any $L\in{\mathcal{M}}_{i}^{\ast}({\mathbf{X}})$}\end{array}\right\}

the cardinality of
\mathcal{S}_{i}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
as given in (
6
) satisfies, for a fixed
g\in g_{1}^{G}
,

(10)

|\mathcal{S}_{i}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})|=|g_{1}^{G}|\cdot\left|\bigcup_{M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{i}({\mathbf{X}}),g\in M}\mathcal{S}_{i}(g,M)\right|\leqslant|g_{1}^{G}|\cdot\sum_{M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{i}({\mathbf{X}}),g\in M}\left|\mathcal{S}_{i}(g,M)\right|.

To relate this back to estimating
{\rm Prob}_{i}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
we use the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
G,{\mathbf{X}},e_{1},e_{2},g_{1},g_{2}
be as in Hypothesis
2.1
, and choose a fixed
g\in g_{1}^{G}
. Then

(11)

\displaystyle{\rm Prob}_{i}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\left|\bigcup_{M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{i}({\mathbf{X}}),g\in M}\mathcal{S}_{i}(g,M)\right|}{\left|N(d,q^{u},e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})\right|}\leqslant\sum_{M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{i}({\mathbf{X}}),g\in M}\frac{\left|\mathcal{S}_{i}(g,M)\right|}{\left|N(d,q^{u},e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})\right|},

with
\mathcal{S}_{i}(g,M)
as in (
9
) and

(12)

N(d,q^{u},e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})\coloneq\{g^{\prime}\in g_{2}^{G}\mid(g,g^{\prime})\ \mbox{is a stingray duo in $g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}$}\}.

Proof.

Let
x\in g_{1}^{G}
, let
A(x),B(x)
be the set of all elements
y\in g_{2}^{G}
such that
(x,y)\in\mathcal{S}_{i}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
, or
(x,y)
is a stingray duo in
g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}
, respectively. Then, with
\mathcal{S}_{i}(x,M)
as in (
9
), we have
A(x)=\bigcup_{M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{i}({\mathbf{X}}),x\in M}\mathcal{S}_{i}(x,M)
.
Also for each
z\in G
it is clear that
A(x)^{z}=A(x^{z})
and
B(x)^{z}=B(x^{z})
, and hence the sizes of these subsets do not depend on the choice of
x\in g_{1}^{G}
. Now fix
g\in g_{1}^{G}
. Then
B(g)=N(d,q^{u},e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})
as in (
12
) and it follows from (
7
) that

{\rm Prob}_{i}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=\frac{|g_{1}^{G}|\cdot|A(g)|}{|g_{1}^{G}|\cdot|B(g)|}=\frac{|A(g)|}{|B(g)|}=\frac{\left|\bigcup_{M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{i}({\mathbf{X}}),g\in M}\mathcal{S}_{i}(g,M)\right|}{\left|N(d,q^{u},e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})\right|}

and the assertions in (
11
) follow.
∎

4.
Bounds on numbers of subspaces and duos

For our analysis we need explicit bounds on the numbers of (nondegenerate)
e
-subspaces of
V
for the various types
{\mathbf{X}}
, and on related quantities. Since the parity of
e
is restricted as in Table
3
, we refine bounds available from, for example,
[
32
]
. To facilitate use of the result in
[
32
]
, we define, for integers
k,d,q
with
1\leqslant k\leqslant d
and
q\geqslant 2
, and
\\
tau=\pm 1
,

(13)

\omega(k,d;\tau q)\coloneq\prod_{i=k}^{d}(1-(\tau q)^{-i}),

and, if
k<d
,

(14)

\Delta(k,d;\tau q)\coloneq\frac{\omega(1,d;\tau q)}{\omega(1,k;\tau q)\omega(1,d-k;\tau q)}=\frac{\omega(k+1,d;\tau q)}{\omega(1,d-k;\tau q)}.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
d\geqslant 2
,
q\geqslant 2
, and let
k
satisfy
1\leqslant k\leqslant d-1
, and let
\Delta(k,d;q)
be as in (
14
). Then

1<\Delta(k,d;q)<(1-q^{-1}-q^{-2})^{-1};

and

\begin{array}[]{clllcl}(1+q^{-1})^{-1}&<&\Delta(k,d;-q)&<&1+q^{-k-1}&\mbox{if $k$ is even,}\\
(1-q^{-k-1})(1+q^{-1})^{-1}&<&\Delta(k,d;-q)&<&1&\mbox{if $k$ is odd.}\end{array}

Moreover, the bounds in Table
6
hold, and also
\Delta(k,d;q)<81/71<8/7
if
q\geqslant 9
.

Proof.

The bounds for
\Delta(k,d;q)
and
\Delta(k,d;-q)
are proved in
[
32
, Lemmas 3.1(b) and 3.2(b)]
. Applying these bounds yields the entries in Table
6
. Finally, if
q\geqslant 9
, then using the upper bound for
\Delta(k,d;q)
we have
\Delta(k,d;q)<(1-q^{-1}-q^{-2})^{-1}\leqslant(1-9^{-1}-9^{-2})^{-1}=81/71<8/7
.
∎

Let
V={\mathbb{F}}_{q^{u}}^{d}
be the natural module for
\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
. Let
{\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{V}{e}}
denote the set of all
e
-subspaces of
V
if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
, and the set of all nondegenerate
e
-subspaces of
V
if
{\mathbf{X}}\in\{{\mathbf{Sp}},{\mathbf{U}}\}
. If
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}
, let

{\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{V}{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\eta}
denote the set of all nondegenerate
e
-subspaces of
V
of type
\eta\in\{-,\circ,+\}
.
For a positive integer
e<d
and a
\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
-conjugacy class
\mathscr{C}
of
e
-ppd stingray elements, the estimates use the following sets

(15)

{\mathcal{U}}(d,q^{u},e,{\mathbf{X}})\coloneq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{V}{e}}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}\in\{{\mathbf{L}},{\mathbf{Sp}},{\mathbf{U}}\}$,}\\
{\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{V}{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{-}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}$.}\end{array}\right.

Also, let

(16)

{\mathcal{D}}(d,q^{u},e,{\mathbf{X}})\coloneq\{(U,U^{\prime})\mid U\in{\mathcal{U}}(d,q^{u},e,{\mathbf{X}}),U^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{U}}(d,q^{u},d-e,{\mathbf{X}}),V=U\oplus U^{\prime}\}.

The subspace pairs in
{\mathcal{D}}(d,q^{u},e,{\mathbf{L}})
and subspaces in
{\mathcal{U}}(d,q^{u},e,{\mathbf{X}})
may be used to partition the elements of
\mathscr{C}
as follows:

(17)

\mathscr{C}(\delta,{\mathbf{X}})\coloneq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{g\in\mathscr{C}\mid(U_{g},F_{g})=\delta\}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}$ and $\delta\in{\mathcal{D}}(d,q^{u},e,{\mathbf{L}})$,}\\
\{g\in\mathscr{C}\mid U_{g}=\delta\}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{L}}$ and $\delta\in{\mathcal{U}}(d,q^{u},e,{\mathbf{X}})$.}\\
\end{array}\right.

This partition leads to expressions for
|\mathscr{C}|
, which we then extend to expressions for counting stingray duos from a pair of conjugacy classes.
Some of the estimates we need follow from work in
[
14
]
for type
{\mathbf{L}}
, so we treat this type separately from the other classical types.

4.1.
Estimates for the linear type
{\mathbf{L}}
