To prove Proposition
7.1
, we work with the following assumptions.

Hypothesis 7.2

(for
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{3}
)
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.1
holds and

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})>0
. Let
(g,g^{\prime})
be a stingray duo in
g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}
such that
H\coloneq\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle
leaves invariant an extension field structure
V=\mathbb{F}_{q^{ub}}^{c}
, where
d=bc
for some prime
b
dividing
d
, and
H\leqslant M\coloneq G\cap({\mathrm{GL}}_{c}(q^{ub}).b)
, while
H\not\leqslant L
for any
L\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathbf{X}})\cup{\mathcal{M}}_{2}({\mathbf{X}})
(as in (
21
), (
23
), or (
24
)).

First we derive some restrictions on these parameters. Note that the proof of the following result uses the fact that
e_{2}\geqslant 2
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Assume that Hypothesis
7.2
holds. Then

(a)

b
divides
\gcd(e_{1},e_{2})
, and in particular
b<d
;

(b)

g,g^{\prime}\in G\cap{\mathrm{GL}}_{c}(q^{ub})<M
, and moreover,
(g,g^{\prime})
is a ppd
(e_{1}/b,e_{2}/b)
-stingray duo in
G\cap{\mathrm{GL}}_{c}(q^{ub})<M
;

(c)

the group
M
satisfies one of the lines of Table
10
, and in each case,
M
is self-normalising in
G
,
g_{1}^{G}\cap M=g^{M}
and
g_{2}^{G}\cap M=(g^{\prime})^{M}
.

In particular the content of the set
{\mathcal{M}}_{3}(\mathbf{X)}
given in Proposition
7.1
is justified.

Proof.

(a) Suppose first that
r_{1}=d
. Since
e_{1}\geqslant d/2
and
r_{1}=k_{1}e_{1}+1
for some positive integer
k_{1}
, we conclude that
r_{1}=e_{1}+1=d
. However, this is a contradiction since
e_{1}\leqslant d-2
. Hence
r_{1}\neq d
. Suppose next that
b=d
, so
M\leqslant{\mathrm{GL}}_{1}(q^{ud}).d
. Since
r_{1}\neq d
, it follows that
g\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{1}(q^{ud})
, and hence that
r_{1}\mid(q^{ud}-1)
. Thus
r_{1}
divides
(q^{u})^{\gcd(e_{1},d)}-1
, and as
r_{1}
is a primitive prime divisor of
q^{ue_{1}}-1
, this implies that
e_{1}\mid d
. Since
d=b
is prime, this means that
e_{1}=d
, which is a contradiction since
e_{1}\leqslant d-2
. Thus
b<d
.

In particular,
b\leqslant d/2\leqslant e_{1}<r_{1}
, and hence
g\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{c}(q^{ub})
.
This implies that the image
\textup{im}(g-1)
(which is an
e_{1}
-dimensional
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{u}}
-subspace) is also an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{ub}}
-subspace, and hence
b\mid e_{1}
. Since
d=e_{1}+e_{2}
, we also have
b\mid e_{2}
, and part (a) is proved.

(b) Now
r_{1}\geqslant e_{1}+1
and
b\mid e_{1}
by part (a), so
r_{1}>e_{1}\geqslant b
. Hence
g\in G\cap{\mathrm{GL}}_{c}(q^{ub})<M
. Similarly
b\mid e_{2}
, so
r_{2}>e_{2}\geqslant b
, and thus
g^{\prime}\in G\cap{\mathrm{GL}}_{c}(q^{ub})
as
r_{2}
is prime. It follows, from the definition of a primitive prime divisor, that
r_{i}
is a primitive prime divisor of
(q^{ub})^{e_{i}/b}-1
, for each
i
. Hence
(g,g^{\prime})
is a ppd
(e_{1}/b,e_{2}/b)
-stingray duo in
G\cap{\mathrm{GL}}_{c}(q^{ub})
, proving part (b).

(c) Suppose that the isometry group listed in the second column of
[
21
, Table 4.3.A]

has type
{\bf Y}\in\{{\bf L},{\bf U},{\bf Sp},{\bf O}\}
acting on a vector space
({\mathbb{F}}_{q^{ub}})^{d/b}
. We begin by discussing the columns of Table
10
labeled ‘
{\mathbf{X}}
’ and ‘
M\geqslant
’.
We show below that Line 6 of
[
21
, Table 4.3.A]
does not arise in Table
10
; and that in Line 7 of
[
21
, Table 4.3.A]
the quantity
d/2
is even, so the condition
\varepsilon=(-)^{d/2}
in
[
21
, Table 4.3.A]
becomes
\varepsilon=+
. Given these facts, it follows from
[
21
, Table 4.3.A]
and the containment
I_{\sharp}\leqslant I

on
[
21
, p. 111, line
-3
]
, that
M
contains
Y\cdot b
where
Y={\bf GY}_{d/b}(q^{ub})
is the full isometry group, and
b
denotes a field automorphism. It turns out that
Y\cdot b=M
, although
we will only need
Y\cdot b\leqslant M
. This verifies the ‘
{\mathbf{X}}
’ and ‘
M\geqslant
’ columns of Table
10
.
By part (a),
b<d
so
c>1
, as noted in particular in Line 1 of Table
10
.

Next we deal with the parity conditions claimed for the
e_{i}/b
, and also we show that Line 6 of
[
21
, Table 4.3.A]
does not arise.
The parity conditions are consequences of the facts that
g,g^{\prime}
are
(e_{i}/b)
-stingray elements of
G\cap{\mathrm{GL}}_{c}(q^{ub})
, for the appropriate
e_{i}
by part (b), and the parity restrictions given by Lemma
2.5
(b). We argue as follows.
The facts that
e_{1}/b,e_{2}/b
are both even in Line 3, and both odd in Lines 2 and 4, of Table
10
, follow directly from Lemma
2.5
(b) applied to
M
. In the remaining lines of Table
10
, and also in Line 6 of
[
21
, Table 4.3.A]
, we have
{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}
, so
e_{1},e_{2}
are even and so
d
is even, and hence
\varepsilon=\pm
. In Line 5 of Table
10
, and also in Line 6 of
[
21
, Table 4.3.A]
,
M
is an orthogonal group and so, by Lemma
2.5
(b), either
e_{i}/b=1
or
e_{i}/b
is even.
If
e_{i}/b
is odd, for some
i
, then
e_{i}/b=1
and so
b=2
(since
e_{i}
is even) and
e_{i}=2
. This implies that
i=2
and
e_{2}=2
, and also that
e_{1}/2>1
(since
d>8
) so
e_{1}/2
is even and
d/2=e_{1}/2+e_{2}/2
is odd. Thus for Line 5 of Table
10
the
e_{i}/b
are both even, while for Line 6 of
[
21
, Table 4.3.A]
(which does not appear in Table
10
) we do have
M
of type
{\mathrm{GO}}^{\circ}_{d/2}(q^{2}).2
with
(d/2)q
odd. In this case, since
e_{2}/b=1
,
g^{\prime}
would be a ppd
1
-stingray element of
{{\mathrm{GO}}^{\circ}}_{d/2}(q^{2})
of odd prime order
r_{2}\geqslant e_{2}+1
. Therefore
g^{\prime}
would fix pointwise a non-degenerate
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}
-subspace of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{2}}^{d/2}
of co-dimension
1
, and hence
g^{\prime}
would act as a reflection, and so would have order 2, which is a contradiction. Thus Line 6 of
[
21
, Table 4.3.A]
does not arise.
Finally in Line 6 of Table
10
(which is Line 7 of
[
21
, Table 4.3.A]
),
b=2
and
M
is a unitary group so
e_{1}/2,e_{2}/2
are both odd by Lemma
2.5
(b). Thus the parities of the
e_{i}/b
are as stated in Table
10
.

In all the Lines 2–6 of Table
10
,
e_{1}/b,e_{2}/b
have equal parity, and hence
c=d/b=e_{1}/b+e_{2}/b
and
d
are even. In particular in Lines 4 and 6, where
b=2
we have
d/2
even, so all assertions in the ‘Conditions’ column are valid.

Finally we verify the assertions in the ‘Consequences’ column. We already observed that
c>1
in Line 1, and that
c=d/b
is even in all other Lines. For Line 2, we have
e_{2}\geqslant b\geqslant 3
, and since
d>8
and
d
is even we also have
d\geqslant 10
. In Lines 3 and 5, each
e_{i}/b
is even and hence
c=e_{1}/b+e_{2}/b\geqslant 4
and
e_{1}\geqslant e_{2}\geqslant 4
; also since
d>8
, either
b\geqslant 3
or
b=2
and
c\geqslant 6
(as
c
is even), and in either case
d\geqslant 12
. Lastly in Lines 4 and 6 we have
b=2
and each
e_{i}/b
is odd; since
c=d/2
is even and
d>8
, it follows that either
e_{1}\geqslant e_{2}\geqslant 6
or
e_{2}=2
with
e_{1}\geqslant 10
, and in either case
d\geqslant 12
. Moreover, in Line 4,
q
is odd and if
e_{2}=2
then we must have
q\geqslant 5
since
3^{2}-1
has no primitive prime divisor. Also in Line 6,
(e_{2},q)\neq(2,3)
since
3^{2}-1
has no primitive prime divisor. Thus all assertions in the ‘Consequences’ column are valid, and hence all entries in Table
10
are valid. This proves the first assertion of part (c).

Recall that
G=\textup{G{\bf X}}_{bc}(q)
. In each line of Table
10
,
Y\coloneq G\cap{\mathrm{GL}}_{c}(q^{ub})
is a classical group, which is normal in
M
of index
b
, and
M=N_{G}(M)=N_{G}(Y)
. By part (b),
(g,g^{\prime})
is a ppd
(e_{1}/b,e_{2}/b)
-stingray duo in
Y
. Consider first the element
g
(similar comments apply to
g^{\prime}
). By Lemma
2.5
(c) applied to
Y
, there is a unique cyclic torus
T_{Y}
in
Y
containing
g
such that
C_{Y}(g)=C_{Y}(T_{Y})
, and there is a single
Y
-conjugacy class of such tori. There are
|Y:N_{Y}(T_{Y})|
of these tori, and each of them contains exactly
|g_{1}^{G}\cap M|/|Y:N_{Y}(T_{Y})|
conjugates of
g_{1}
. Now applying Lemma
2.5
to
G
, there is a unique cyclic torus
T
of
G
containing
g
and
C_{G}(g)=C_{G}(T)
, with the form given in Lemma
2.5
(c). It follows that
T_{Y}
is contained in
T
. Again applying Lemma
2.5
(c) to
G
and
Y
,
g^{G}\cap T
has size
e_{1}
and
N_{G}(T)
acts transitively on this set by conjugation; and
g^{Y}\cap T_{Y}
has size
e_{1}/b
and
N_{Y}(T)
acts transitively on this set by conjugation. Now
|N_{M}(T_{Y}):N_{Y}(T_{Y})|=b
and
g^{M}\cap T_{Y}
splits into
b
orbits of
N_{Y}(T_{Y})
, each of size
e_{1}/b
, which are permuted cyclically by
N_{M}(T)
. It follows that
T_{Y}
contains
e_{1}
conjugates of
g_{1}
and hence
g_{1}^{G}\cap T=g_{1}^{G}\cap T_{Y}
, and
N_{M}(T_{Y})
acts transitively by conjugation on this set. It follows that
g_{1}^{G}\cap M=g^{M}
. An analogous argument yields
g_{2}^{G}\cap M=(g^{\prime})^{M}
.

Finally, part (c) implies that the content of the set
{\mathcal{M}}_{3}({\mathbf{X}})
given in Proposition
7.1
is justified.
∎

For a given
{\mathbf{X}}
, and for each prime
b
dividing
\gcd(e_{1},e_{2})
, let
{\mathcal{M}}_{3}({\mathbf{X}},b)
denote the set of subgroups in
{\mathcal{M}}_{3}({\mathbf{X}})
occurring in Table
10
for this particular prime
b
. Then

(27)

{\mathcal{M}}_{3}({\mathbf{X}})=\bigcup_{b}{\mathcal{M}}_{3}({\mathbf{X}},b)\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant\sum_{b}{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};b),

where
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};b)
is
the contribution to (
11
) from subgroups
M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{3}({\mathbf{X}},b)
containing the element
g
. We show for each
b
in Lemma
7.4
(a), that
{\mathcal{M}}_{3}({\mathbf{X}},b)
contains at most one
G
-conjugacy class.

Lemma 7.4
.

Assume that Hypothesis
7.2
holds and let
{\mathcal{M}}_{3}({\mathbf{X}},b)
and
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};b)
be as in (
27
). Then

(a)

for each
{\mathbf{X}}
and each prime
b
dividing
\gcd(e_{1},e_{2})
, either
{\mathcal{M}}_{3}({\mathbf{X}},b)=\emptyset
, or
{\mathcal{M}}_{3}({\mathbf{X}},b)
is a single
G
-conjugacy class; and

(b)

for each
{\mathbf{X}}
,
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=0
if and only if the conditions in Table
9
hold.

(c)

Moreover, suppose that
{\mathbf{X}}\in\{{\mathbf{Sp}},\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}\}
,
b=2
, and
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};2)>0
. Then one of the following holds:

(i)

e_{1}\equiv e_{2}\equiv 0\pmod{4}
, and
{\mathcal{M}}_{3}({\mathbf{X}},2)
is the
G
-conjugacy class of subgroups
M
in Line
3
of Table
10
if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
, or in Line
5
of Table
10
if
{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}
.

(ii)

e_{1}\equiv e_{2}\equiv 2\pmod{4}
, and
{\mathcal{M}}_{3}({\mathbf{X}},2)
is the
G
-conjugacy class of subgroups
M
in Line
4
of Table
10
with
q
odd if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
, or in Line
6
of Table
10
with
\varepsilon=+
if
{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}
.

In particular, the first assertion in Proposition
7.1
about when
\mathcal{M}_{3}({\mathbf{X}})
is empty follows from part
(b)
.

Proof.

(a) We note first that, for each line of Table
10
, there is a single
G
-conjugacy class of such subgroups
M
and, if the conditions for this line all hold then the index
b
normal classical subgroup of
M
contains
ppd
(e_{1}/2,e_{2}/2)
-stingray duos in
g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}
, so
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};b)>0
.
Conversely, by Lemma
7.3
, if
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})>0
then there exists a prime
b
such that
b
divides
\gcd(e_{1},e_{2})
and exactly one of the lines of Table
10
holds for each such
b
(note the different parity conditions on the
e_{i}/b
when there are two lines for
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
or
\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}
).
In particular part (a) follows from these comments.

(b) and (c). If there exists an odd prime
b
dividing
\gcd(e_{1},e_{2})
, then all the conditions of Line 1, 2, 3, or 5 of Table
10
hold (according to the the type
{\mathbf{X}}
),
and so, by Lemma
7.3
,
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};b)>0
.
In particular if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{U}}
, then the
e_{i}
are odd, and so we conclude from the previous paragraph that
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})>0
if and only if
\gcd(e_{1},e_{2})>1
, proving part (b) for
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{U}}
. Now consider
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
. We have seen that if
\gcd(e_{1},e_{2})
is divisible by an odd prime then
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})>0
. Suppose next that both
e_{i}
are even. Then
b=2
divides
\gcd(e_{1},e_{2})
, and the group
M={\mathrm{GL}}_{d/2}(q^{2}).2
in Line 1 of Table
10
yields stingray duos proving
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};2)>0
. These observations together with the previous paragraph show that
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})>0
if and only if
\gcd(e_{1},e_{2})>1
, proving part (b) for
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
.

For the rest of the proof we may assume that
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
or
\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}
for some
\varepsilon=\pm
, so the
e_{i}
are both even. By the previous paragraph,
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})>0
if
\gcd(e_{1},e_{2})
has an odd prime divisor. Hence to complete the proof of part (b) we may assume that
\gcd(e_{1},e_{2})=2^{f}
for some
f\geqslant 1
, and to prove part (c) we must consider
b=2
.

Suppose first that
e_{1}\equiv e_{2}\equiv 0\pmod{4}
. Then the conditions of Line 3 or 5 of Table
10
hold, and so, as observed in the first paragraph of the proof,
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};2)>0
, and
{\mathcal{M}}_{3}({\mathbf{X}},2)
is the
G
-conjugacy class of subgroups
M
in Line
3
or
5
of Table
10
.
Next suppose that
e_{1}\equiv e_{2}\equiv 2\pmod{4}
, so both
e_{1}/2
and
e_{2}/2
are odd. For
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
, if
q
is even then
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{Sp}};2)=0
by Lemma
7.3
, while if
q
is odd then the conditions of Line 4 of Table
10
hold, and as observed in the first paragraph above,
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{Sp}};2)>0
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{3}({\mathbf{Sp}},2)
is the
G
-conjugacy class of subgroups
M
in Line
4
of Table
10
.
For
{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}
, if
\varepsilon=-
then
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};2)=0
as none of the lines of Table
10
holds, while if
\varepsilon=+
then the conditions of Line 6 of Table
10
hold, and as observed in the first paragraph above,
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};2)>0
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{3}({\mathbf{X}},2)
is the
G
-conjugacy class of subgroups
M
in Line
6
of Table
10
.
The remaining possibility is that

e_{1}/2,e_{2}/2
have different parities;
in this case we have
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};2)=0
by Lemma
7.3
. This completes the proofs of parts (b) and (c).
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

Assume that Hypothesis
7.2
holds with
d>8
, and suppose that, for some prime
b
dividing
\gcd(e_{1},e_{2})
,

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};b)>0
(see (
27
)). Then

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};b)<\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}b\cdot q^{-2e_{1}e_{2}(1-1/b)}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}$}\\
2.56\cdot b\cdot q^{-2e_{1}e_{2}(1-1/b)}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{U}}$}\\
4.6\cdot b\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}(1-1/b)}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}$ with $\frac{e_{1}}{b},\frac{e_{2}}{b}$ even}\\
{1.45}\cdot b\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}/2}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}$ with $\frac{e_{1}}{2},\frac{e_{2}}{2},q$ odd, $q\geqslant 5$, $b=2$}\\
{2}\cdot b\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}/2}&\mbox{{if ${\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}$ with $\frac{e_{1}}{2},\frac{e_{2}}{2}$ odd, $q=3,e_{2}\geqslant 6$, $b=2$}}\\
5.34\cdot b\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}(1-1/b)}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{-}}$ with $\frac{e_{1}}{b},\frac{e_{2}}{b}$ even}\\
9.43\cdot b\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}(1-1/b)}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{+}}$ with $\frac{e_{1}}{b},\frac{e_{2}}{b}$ even}\\
17.6\cdot b\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}/2}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{+}}$ with $\frac{e_{1}}{2},\frac{e_{2}}{2}$ odd, $b=2$.}\\
\end{array}\right.

Moreover, if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{O}}^{+}
with
e_{2}=2
,
e_{1}=d-2
and
b=2
, then
d\geqslant 12
,
q\neq 3
, and
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};2)<12.585\cdot b\cdot q^{-(d-2)}
if
q=2
, and
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};2)<4.005\cdot b\cdot q^{-(d-2)}
if
q\geqslant 4.

Proof.

By (
27
),

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant\sum_{b}{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};b)
, where the sum is over all primes
b
dividing
\gcd(e_{1},e_{2})
. Suppose from now on that
b
is a prime dividing
\gcd(e_{1},e_{2})
and
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};b)>0
. Then by Lemma
7.4
,
{\mathcal{M}}_{3}({\mathbf{X}},b)
is a single
G
-conjugacy class, and by Lemma
7.3
, each
M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{3}({\mathbf{X}},b)
is self-normalising in
G
and the conditions of exactly one Line
j
of Table
10
hold; and also for each
i=1,2
,
g_{i}^{G}\cap M
is a single
M
-conjugacy class.

We use the notation of this section; in particular
g\in g_{1}^{G}
lying in a chosen
M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{3}({\mathbf{X}},b)
. Then by Lemma
2.7
, the number of
L\in{\mathcal{M}}_{3}({\mathbf{X}},b)
such that
g\in L
is
|C_{G}(g_{1})|/|C_{M}(g)|
. Thus, by (
11
),

(28)

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};b)\leqslant\frac{|C_{G}(g_{1})|}{|C_{M}(g)|}\cdot\frac{|\mathcal{S}_{3}(g,M)|}{|N(d,q^{u},e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}=\frac{|C_{G}(g_{1})|}{|N(d,q^{u},e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}\cdot\frac{|\mathcal{S}_{3}(g,M)|}{|C_{M}(g)|},

with
|N(d,q^{u},e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|
as in (
12
), and
\mathcal{S}_{3}(g,M)
as in (
9
), namely the set of all
g^{\prime}\in g_{2}^{G}\cap M
such that
(g,g^{\prime})
is a stingray duo and
\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle
is irreducible and primitive on
V
. The first factor in the last expression in (
28
) is determined in Proposition
4.2
(c) for
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{L}}
or
4.4
(c) for
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
.
We need to estimate the second factor in this expression.

As in the proof of Lemma
7.3
(c), let
Y
be the normal classical subgroup of
M
of index
b
. Applying Lemma
2.5
to
Y
and to
G
, there is a unique cyclic torus
T_{Y}
in
Y
containing
g
such that
C_{Y}(g)=C_{Y}(T_{Y})
, and a unique cyclic torus
T
in
G
containing
g
such that
C_{G}(g)=C_{G}(T)
. Moreover (see the proof of Lemma
7.3
(c)),
T_{Y}\leqslant T
and
C_{M}(g)=C_{Y}(T_{Y})=C_{Y}(g)
.

To understand the set
\mathcal{S}_{3}(g,M)
, we note that, by Lemma
7.3
,
g_{2}^{G}\cap M
is a single
M
-conjugacy class
(g^{\prime})^{M}
, and, as discussed in the proof of Lemma
7.3
(c),
(g^{\prime})^{M}
splits into
b
conjugacy classes of
Y
which are permuted cyclically by
N_{M}(T_{Y})
. Thus the number of
h\in g_{2}^{G}\cap M
such that
(g,h)
is a stingray duo is
b
times the number of such elements
h\in(g^{\prime})^{Y}
. This latter number is

|N(d/b,q^{ub},e_{1}/b,{\mathbf{Y}})|
, where
Y
is classical of type
{\mathbf{Y}}
. Notice that, in each line of Table
10
,
Y
is the full isometry group of type
{\mathbf{Y}}
, and hence the ratio
|C_{Y}(g)|/|N(d/b,q^{ub},e_{1}/b,{\mathbf{Y}})|
is given by Proposition
4.2
or
4.4
applied to
Y
.
Thus (
28
) becomes

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};b)\leqslant\frac{|C_{G}(g_{1})|}{|N(d,q^{u},e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}\cdot\frac{b\cdot|N(d/b,q^{ub},e_{1}/b,{\mathbf{Y}})|}{|C_{Y}(g)|},

and we substitute the values of the two factors given by Proposition
4.2
or
4.4
applied to
G
and
Y
. If
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
, this yields

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}};b)\leqslant\frac{(q^{e_{1}}-1)(q^{e_{2}}-1)}{q^{2e_{1}e_{2}}}\cdot\frac{b\cdot(q^{b})^{2(e_{1}/b)(e_{2}/b)}}{((q^{b})^{e_{1}/b}-1)((q^{b})^{e_{2}/b}-1)}=b\,q^{-2e_{1}e_{2}(1-1/b)}

proving the lemma for type
{\mathbf{L}}
.
Next, consider
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{U}}
. The bound
5/8\leqslant 1-3/(2q^{2})\leqslant\mathbf{k}(d,q^{2},e_{1},{\mathbf{U}})
holds as
d>8
, and
\mathbf{k}(d/b,q^{2b},e_{1}/b,{\mathbf{U}})<1
holds for all
b\mid e_{1}
. Therefore
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{U}};b)
is at most

\displaystyle\ \ \frac{(q^{e_{1}}+1)(q^{e_{2}}+1)}{\mathbf{k}(d,q^{2},e_{1},{\mathbf{U}})\cdot|\mathcal{U}(d,q^{2},e_{1},{\mathbf{U}}|}\cdot\frac{b\cdot\mathbf{k}(d/b,q^{2b},e_{1}/b,{\mathbf{U}})\cdot|\mathcal{U}(d/b,q^{2b},e_{1}/b,{\mathbf{U}})|}{((q^{b})^{e_{1}/b}+1)((q^{b})^{e_{2}/b}+1)}

\displaystyle<\frac{b\cdot|\mathcal{U}(d/b,q^{2b},e_{1}/b,{\mathbf{U}})|}{(5/8)\cdot|\mathcal{U}(d,q^{2},e_{1},{\mathbf{U}})|}.

Now the upper and lower bounds in Proposition
4.4
give

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};b)<=\frac{b\cdot(q^{b})^{2(e_{1}/b)(e_{2}/b)}}{(5/8)\cdot(5/8)\cdot q^{2e_{1}e_{2}}}=\frac{64}{25}\cdot b\cdot q^{-2e_{1}e_{2}(1-1/b)}=2.56\cdot b\cdot q^{-2e_{1}e_{2}(1-1/b)}

which is the required bound for type
{\mathbf{U}}
.
Next we consider
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
or
\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}
for the cases where both
e_{1}/b
and
e_{2}/b
are even. Then, using the bounds
1/4\leqslant 1-3/(2q)\leqslant\mathbf{k}(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})<1
, and setting
\nu=1
if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
and
\nu=\varepsilon
if
{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}
, Proposition
4.4
(c) shows that
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};b)
is at most

\displaystyle\phantom{\leqslant\;\,}\frac{(q^{e_{1}/2}+1)(q^{e_{2}/2}+\nu)}{\mathbf{k}(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})\cdot|\mathcal{U}(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}\cdot\frac{b\cdot\mathbf{k}(d/b,q^{b},e_{1}/b,{\mathbf{X}})\cdot|\mathcal{U}(d/b,q^{b},e_{1}/b,{\mathbf{X}})|}{((q^{b})^{e_{1}/2b}+1)((q^{b})^{e_{2}/2b}+\nu)}

\displaystyle<\frac{b\cdot|\mathcal{U}(d/b,q^{b},e_{1}/b,{\mathbf{X}})|}{(1/4)\cdot|\mathcal{U}(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}.

Then the upper and lower bounds in Proposition
4.4
show that

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};b)<\frac{b\cdot{\mathbf{b}}(q^{b},{\mathbf{X}})\cdot(q^{b})^{(e_{1}/b)(e_{2}/b)}}{(1/4)\cdot{\mathbf{a}}(q,{\mathbf{X}})\cdot q^{e_{1}e_{2}}}=\frac{4\cdot b\cdot{\mathbf{b}}(q^{b},{\mathbf{X}})}{{\mathbf{a}}(q,{\mathbf{X}})}\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}(1-1/b)}.

By Table
7
, using
q\geqslant 2
and
q^{b}\geqslant 4
, the factor
4\cdot{\mathbf{b}}(q^{b},{\mathbf{X}})/{\mathbf{a}}(q,{\mathbf{X}})
is
4(8/7)/1=32/7<4.6
if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
, and is

4(160/239)/(1/2)<5.34
if
{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{-}}
, and
4(128/239)/(5/22)<9.43
if
{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{+}}
. Thus the required bounds hold in these cases.

For the remaining two cases (Lines 4 and 6 of Table
10
),
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
or
\mathbf{O^{+}}
,
b=2
, both
e_{1}/2
and
e_{2}/2
are odd, and the classical group
Y={\mathrm{GU}}_{d/2}(q)
. Further, by Table
10
,
d\geqslant 12
and
d/2
is even. Now, using the bounds
1-3/(2q)\leqslant\mathbf{k}(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})<1
, and then using the upper and lower bounds in Proposition
4.4
(c)–(d), shows that

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};2)
is at most

\displaystyle\ \ \frac{(q^{e_{1}/2}+1)(q^{e_{2}/2}+1)}{\mathbf{k}(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})\cdot|\mathcal{U}(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}\cdot\frac{2\cdot\mathbf{k}(d/2,q^{2},e_{1}/2,{\mathbf{U}})\cdot|\mathcal{U}(d/2,q^{2},e_{1}/2,{\mathbf{U}})|}{(q^{e_{1}/2}+1)(q^{e_{2}/2}+1)}

\displaystyle<\frac{2\cdot|\mathcal{U}(d/2,q^{2},e_{1}/2,{\mathbf{U}})|}{(1-3/(2q))\cdot|\mathcal{U}(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}<\frac{2\cdot q^{2(e_{1}/2)(e_{2}/2)}}{(1-3/(2q))\cdot{\mathbf{a}}(q,{\mathbf{X}})\cdot q^{e_{1}e_{2}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{(1-3/(2q))\cdot{\mathbf{a}}(q,{\mathbf{X}})}\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}/2}.

For
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
, if
q\geqslant 5
, then

\frac{2}{(1-3/(2q))\cdot{\mathbf{a}}(q,{\mathbf{Sp}})}\leqslant\frac{2}{(7/10)\cdot 1}<2.9,

and hence
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{Sp}};2)<2.9\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}/2}
. On the other hand, in Line 4 of Table
10
, we may also have
q=3
with
e_{2}\geqslant 6
, and here we have

\frac{2}{(1-3/(2q))\cdot{\mathbf{a}}(q,{\mathbf{Sp}})}\leqslant{\frac{2}{(1/2)\cdot 1}}=4

yielding
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{Sp}};2)<4\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}/2}
.

Now we consider
{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{+}}
. Here
d\geqslant 12
and
q\geqslant 2
, and so

\frac{2}{(1-3/(2q))\cdot{\mathbf{a}}(q,\mathbf{O^{+}})}\leqslant\frac{2}{(1/4)\cdot(5/22)}=35.2,

and our general bound is
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},\mathbf{O^{+}};2)<35.2\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}/2}
holds.
For the special case where
e_{2}=2
,
e_{1}=d-2
we note that
q\neq 3
since
3^{2}-1
has no primitive prime divisor, and for
q\neq 3
we use the expression for
|\mathcal{U}(d,q,d-2,{\mathbf{O}}^{+})|
in Equation (
20
), namely

|\mathcal{U}(d,q,d-2,{\mathbf{O}}^{+})|=q^{2(d-2)}\cdot{\mathbf{a}}^{*}(q,\mathcal{O}^{+}),\ \mbox{where}\ {\mathbf{a}}^{*}(q,\mathcal{O}^{+})=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\Delta\left(\frac{d{-}2}{2},\frac{d}{2};q^{2}\right)\cdot\Gamma(1)

and arguing as above we have

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{O}}^{+};2)<\frac{2}{(1-3/(2q))\cdot{\mathbf{a}}^{*}(q,\mathbf{O^{+}})}\cdot q^{-(d-2)}.

Next we use the definition of
\Delta(k,d;q)
in Equation (
14
),
and the definition of
\Gamma(1)
in Equation (
18
). Since
d/2
is even, these yield:

\displaystyle{\mathbf{a}}^{*}(q,{\mathbf{O}}^{+})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\Delta\left(\frac{d{-}2}{2},\frac{d}{2};q^{2}\right)\cdot\Gamma(1)=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{\omega(\frac{d}{2},\frac{d}{2};q^{2})}{\omega(1,1;q^{2})}\cdot\left(1-\frac{q^{-(d-2)/2}+q^{-1}}{1+q^{-d/2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{(1-q^{-d})}{(1-q^{-2})}\cdot\left(\frac{1-(q-1)q^{-d/2}-q^{-1}}{1+q^{-d/2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{(1-q^{-d/2})}{(1-q^{-2})}\cdot(1-(q-1)q^{-d/2}-q^{-1}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{2}{(1-3/(2q))\cdot{\mathbf{a}}^{*}(q,{\mathbf{X}})}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{(1-3/(2q))}\cdot\frac{2(1-q^{-2})}{(1-q^{-d/2})(1-(q-1)q^{-d/2}-q^{-1})}.

For
q=2
this expression is less than
25.17
and for
q\geqslant 4
it is less than
8.01
, and we have proved the final assertions of the lemma.
∎

We need a technical lemma before we prove Proposition
7.1
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
q,d,e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfy
q\geqslant 2
,
d=e_{1}+e_{2}
and
2\leqslant e_{2}\leqslant e_{1}\leqslant d-2
. Let
B
be the set of odd prime divisors of
\gcd(e_{1},e_{2}).
Then the following are true.

(a)

For
m\geqslant 3
, the function

h(x)=xq^{m/x}
is strictly decreasing for
x\in[1,\sqrt{m}].

(b)

The bound
\sum_{b\in B}b\cdot q^{e_{1}e_{2}/b}<3.01\cdot q^{e_{1}e_{2}/3}
holds.

(c)

The bound
q^{-e_{1}e_{2}}\sum_{b\in B}b\cdot q^{e_{1}e_{2}/b}<q^{-e_{1}e_{2}/2}\cdot 3.01\cdot q^{-(d-3)/2}
holds. Moreover, if either
e_{2}\neq 3
or
3\nmid e_{1}
, then
q^{-e_{1}e_{2}}\sum_{b\in B}b\cdot q^{e_{1}e_{2}/b}<q^{-e_{1}e_{2}/2}\cdot 3.01\cdot q^{-(d-6)}
.

Proof.

(a) The derivative of
h(x)
is
h^{\prime}(x)=q^{m/x}(1-\frac{m}{x}\log q)
. Since
q\geqslant 2
and
m\geqslant 3
, we have

\sqrt{m}<m\log q
, and it follows that
h^{\prime}(x)<0
for all
x\in[1,\sqrt{m}]
.
Therefore,
h(x)
is decreasing for
x\in[1,\sqrt{m}].

(b) Set
m\coloneq e_{1}e_{2}\geqslant 4
. This bound holds if
B=\emptyset
as the sum is zero, and if
B=\{b\}
has one element because
b\geqslant 3
and
bq^{m/b}\leqslant 3q^{m/3}
by part (a). Suppose now that
B=\{b_{1},\dots,b_{\ell}\}
where
\ell\geqslant 2
and
3\leqslant b_{1}<\cdots<b_{\ell}
. Then
15\leqslant b_{1}b_{2}\leqslant\gcd(e_{1},e_{2})\leqslant e_{2}\leqslant d/2
and hence
d\geqslant 30
. Since
b_{1}\cdots b_{\ell}\mid\gcd(e_{1},e_{2})\mid e_{2}
, we have, using part (a), that
\sum_{b\in B}b\cdot q^{m/b}\leqslant b_{1}q^{m/b_{1}}+(\ell-1)b_{2}q^{m/b_{2}}\leqslant 3q^{m/3}+(\ell-1)5q^{m/5}
. Now
b_{1}\cdot b_{2}^{\ell-1}\leqslant b_{1}\cdots b_{\ell}\leqslant e_{2}
, so

5^{\ell-1}\leqslant b_{2}^{\ell-1}\leqslant e_{2}/b_{1}\leqslant e_{2}/3
, and hence

\ell-1\leqslant\log_{5}(e_{2}/3)
. Therefore

\sum_{b\in B}b\cdot q^{m/b}\leqslant q^{m/3}\left(3+\log_{5}\left(\frac{e_{2}}{3}\right)\cdot 5\cdot q^{-2e_{1}e_{2}/15}\right)\leqslant q^{m/3}f(e_{2},q)

where
f(e_{2},q)=3+5\log_{5}(e_{2}/3)q^{-2e_{2}^{2}/15}
. Now
f(e_{2},q)
is clearly a decreasing function of
q
so
f(e_{2},q)\leqslant f(e_{2},2)
and we will show that
f(e_{2},2)
is a decreasing function of
e_{2}
. Part (b) will follow from this since it implies that
f(e_{2},q)\leqslant f(15,2)=3+5\cdot 2^{-30}<3.01
.

Now we prove that
f(e_{2},2)
is a decreasing function of
e_{2}
. Since
\log_{5}(e_{2}/3)=\ln(e_{2})/\ln(5)-\log_{5}(3)
where
\ln
denotes the natural log, it suffices to show that
h(e_{2}):=\ln(e_{2})\cdot 2^{-2e_{2}^{2}/15}
is a decreasing function of
e_{2}
. Setting
c=2^{-2/15}
(a constant), we have
h(e_{2})=\ln(e_{2})\cdot c^{e_{2}^{2}}
, so
h^{\prime}(e_{2})=c^{e_{2}^{2}}\left(2e_{2}\ln(e_{2})\ln(c)+\frac{1}{e_{2}}\right)
and the condition
h^{\prime}(e_{2})<0
is equivalent to
2e_{2}^{2}\ln(e_{2})\ln(c)+1<0
and this is true because
2(15)^{2}\ln(15)\ln(2^{-2/15})+1=-60\ln(15)\ln(2)+1<0
. In summary,
\sum_{b\in B}b\cdot q^{m/b}\leqslant 3.01\cdot q^{m/3}
holds as claimed.

(c) The stated inequality holds when
e_{2}=2
because then
B
is empty and the sum is zero. So suppose that
e_{2}\geqslant 3
and set
m\coloneq e_{1}e_{2}
.
Then by part (b),
q^{-m}\sum_{b\in B}b\cdot q^{m/b}\leqslant q^{-m/2}\cdot 3.01\cdot q^{-m/6}
. However,
q^{-m/6}=q^{-e_{1}e_{2}/6}\leqslant q^{-(d-3)3/6}=q^{-(d-3)/2}
which proves the first inequality of part (c). Now suppose that either
e_{2}\neq 3
or
3\nmid e_{1}
, and consider the second bound. Observe that if
e_{2}\geqslant 6
, then
e_{1}e_{2}/6\geqslant(d-6)6/6=d-6
and the claimed bound holds by the argument just given, so we may suppose that
e_{2}\leqslant 5
. Also if
|B|=0
, then the sum is zero and the bound holds; while if
|B|\geqslant 2
, then we would have
e_{2}\geqslant 15
which is not the case.
Hence
|B|=1
, say
B=\{b\}
, where
b
is an odd prime dividing
\gcd(e_{1},e_{2})
. Since
e_{2}\leqslant 5
it follows that
e_{2}=b\in\{3,5\}
. As
b\mid e_{1}
, the possibility
e_{2}=b=3
does not arise by our assumptions. Hence
e_{2}=b=5
, and
\sum_{b\in B}bq^{e_{1}e_{2}/b}=5q^{e_{1}e_{2}/5}
,
so that
q^{-e_{1}e_{2}}\sum_{b\in B}bq^{e_{1}e_{2}/b}=q^{-e_{1}e_{2}/2}5q^{-3e_{1}e_{2}/10}
.
Note that
5q^{-3e_{1}e_{2}/10}=5q^{-3(d-5)/2}
and that the inequality
5q^{-3(d-5)/2}\leqslant 3q^{-d+6}
(which is sufficient to obtain the second bound) is equivalent to

q^{(d-3)/2}\geqslant 5/3
. Since
d\geqslant 2e_{2}=10
we have
q^{(d-3)/2}\geqslant 2^{7/2}>5/3
, and hence the second bound holds and part (c) is proved.
∎

Proof of Proposition
7.1
.
Necessary and sufficient conditions for
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=0
follow from Lemma
7.4
(b). Assume that
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})>0
. Then by (
27
),
we have
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant\sum_{b}{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};b)
, where the sum is over the primes
b
dividing
\gcd(e_{1},e_{2})
. An upper bound for each
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};b)
is given in Lemma
7.5
, and our task is to bound the sum of these quantities for each type
{\mathbf{X}}
. We begin with the type
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
, as it is simplest and illustrates the general method. Here, by Lemma
7.5

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})\leqslant q^{-2e_{1}e_{2}}\ \sum_{b}b\cdot q^{2e_{1}e_{2}/b},

summing over primes
b
dividing
\gcd(e_{1},e_{2}).
By separating out the term for
b=2
and applying Lemma
7.6
(c) (with
q^{2}
in place of
q
) to the sum over the odd primes
b
for
d\geqslant 9
, we find

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})\leqslant q^{-2e_{1}e_{2}}\ \sum_{b}b\cdot q^{2e_{1}e_{2}/b}\leqslant\left(2+3.01\cdot q^{-(d-3)}\right)\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}}<2.05\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}}.

The argument for
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{U}}
is almost identical except that in this case the
e_{i}
are both odd, so
d\geqslant 10
and all the primes
b
are odd, and there is an additional constant factor
2.56
by Lemma
7.5
.
By Lemma
7.6
(c) (with
q^{2}
in place of
q
) and
d\geqslant 10
,

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{U}})\leqslant 2.56\cdot 3.01q^{-(d-3)}\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}}\leqslant 0.07\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}}.

The remaining types are
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
and
{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}
, and Lemma
7.5
provides an upper bound for
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};b)
for each type and for each prime
b
, and in some cases two different upper bounds for
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};b)
when
b=2
depending on the (necessarily equal) parities of
e_{1}/2
and
e_{2}/2
, and the field size
q
,
see also Table
10
. We define constants
c({\mathbf{X}})
such that

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};b)<c({\mathbf{X}})\cdot b\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}(1-1/b)}
for odd primes
b
, and constants
c({\mathbf{X}})^{\prime}
(depending on
q
and the parities of
e_{1}/2,e_{2}/2
) such that

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};2)<c({\mathbf{X}})^{\prime}\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}/2}
.
Thus the upper bounds
for
{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
also depend on
q
and these parities.
Using Lemma
7.5
, we set
c({\mathbf{Sp}})=4.6
, and

c({\mathbf{Sp}})^{\prime}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}4.6&\mbox{if $e_{1}\equiv e_{2}\equiv 0\pmod{4}$}\\
1.45&\mbox{if $e_{1}\equiv e_{2}\equiv 2\pmod{4}$, $q$ is odd, and $q\geqslant 5$}\\
0&\mbox{if $e_{1}\equiv e_{2}\equiv 2\pmod{4}$, $q$ is even}\\
2&\mbox{if $e_{1}\equiv e_{2}\equiv 2\pmod{4}$, $q=3$}\\
\end{array}\right.

Likewise we set
c(\mathbf{O^{+}})=9.43
and
c(\mathbf{O^{-}})=5.34
,
and

c(\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}})^{\prime}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}c(\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}})&\mbox{if $e_{1}\equiv e_{2}\equiv 0\pmod{4}$}\\
0&\mbox{if $e_{1}\equiv e_{2}\equiv 2\pmod{4}$, and $\varepsilon=-$}\\
17.6&\mbox{if $e_{1}\equiv e_{2}\equiv 2\pmod{4}$, $\varepsilon=+$, and $e_{2}\geqslant 6$}\\
12.585&\mbox{if $e_{1}\equiv 2\pmod{4}$, $e_{2}=2$, $\varepsilon=+$, and $q=2$}\\
4.005&\mbox{if $e_{1}\equiv 2\pmod{4}$, $e_{2}=2$, $\varepsilon=+$, and $q\geqslant 4$}\\
\end{array}\right.

Then we have by Equation (
27
) and Lemma
7.5

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant\sum_{b}{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};b)\leqslant c({\mathbf{X}})^{\prime}\cdot 2\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}/2}\ +c({\mathbf{X}})\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}}\ \sum_{b>2}b\cdot q^{e_{1}e_{2}/b},

where we sum over odd primes
b
dividing
\gcd(e_{1},e_{2}).

We apply Lemma
7.6
(c)
noting that
d\geqslant 12
(see Table
10
) and that
e_{2}\neq 3
to obtain

\displaystyle{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(2\cdot c({\mathbf{X}})^{\prime}+c({\mathbf{X}})\cdot 3.01\cdot q^{-(d-6)}\right)\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}/2}.

Evaluating the right hand side taking
q^{-(d-1)}\leqslant q_{\rm min}^{-6}
, where
q_{\rm min}
is the least value for
q
in each case, yields the constants in Table
8
, completing the proof of Proposition
7.1
.

8.
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{4}
and
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{7}:
Stabilisers of tensor decompositions

In this section we consider together the Aschbacher categories
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{4}
and
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{7}
,
and we prove that the proportions
{\rm Prob}_{4}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})={\rm Prob}_{7}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=0
whenever
d>4
. Recall that
G=\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)
. For these categories, each maximal subgroup is the stabiliser of a tensor decomposition
V=U_{1}\otimes U_{2}\otimes\dots\otimes U_{t}
, where either (i)
t=2
and
U_{1},U_{2}
are not isometric, say of dimensions
d_{1},d_{2}\geqslant 2
, and
d=d_{1}d_{2}
, or (ii)
t\geqslant 2
and the
U_{i}
are isometric of the same dimension, say
b\geqslant 2
, and
d=b^{t}
. In case (i) the stabiliser is
G\cap({\mathrm{GL}}_{d_{1}}(q^{u})\circ{\mathrm{GL}}_{d_{2}}(q^{u}))
and is in category
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{4}
, while in case (ii) the stabiliser is
G\cap(({\mathrm{GL}}_{b}(q^{u})\circ\dots\circ{\mathrm{GL}}_{b}(q^{u}))\rtimes S_{t})
and is in category
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{7}
.

Proposition 8.1
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.1
holds. Then
{\mathcal{M}}_{4}({\mathbf{X}})={\mathcal{M}}_{7}({\mathbf{X}})=\emptyset
and

{\rm Prob}_{4}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})={\rm Prob}_{7}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=0.

Proof.

Let
V=U_{1}\otimes U_{2}\otimes\dots\otimes U_{t}
as above, and let
d_{i}=\dim(U_{i})
for each
i
, so that
d=d_{1}\cdots d_{t}
and each
d_{i}\geqslant 2
. Let

(g,g^{\prime})
be an
(e_{1},e_{2})
-stingray duo in
g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}
such that
H=\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle
is irreducible and leaves this tensor decomposition invariant.
We claim that
g\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{d_{1}}(q^{u})\circ\dots\circ{\mathrm{GL}}_{d_{t}}(q^{u})
. If this were not the case then
d_{1}=\dots=d_{t}=b
, say, so
d=b^{t}\geqslant 2^{t}
, and
g
projects onto a nontrivial element of
S_{t}
. Since
r_{1}=|g|
is prime, it follows that
r_{1}\leqslant t
. Now
r_{1}=ke_{1}+1
for some integer
k
, and so we have
t\geqslant r_{1}\geqslant e_{1}+1\geqslant d/2+1\geqslant 2^{t-1}+1
, and this is impossible for any
t\geqslant 2
. This proves the claim.

Since
g\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{d_{1}}(q^{u})\circ\dots\circ{\mathrm{GL}}_{d_{t}}(q^{u})
, and
r_{1}=|g|
is prime and does not divide
q
, it follows that
r_{1}
divides
q^{ui}-1
for some
i\leqslant\max\{d_{1},\dots,d_{t}\}
. Further, since
r_{1}
is a primitive prime divisor of
q^{ue_{1}}-1
we conclude that

d/2\leqslant e_{1}\leqslant i\leqslant\max\{d_{1},\dots,d_{t}\}\leqslant d/(\min\{d_{1},\dots,d_{t}\})^{t-1}\leqslant d/2.

Thus
t=2
,
\min\{d_{1},d_{2}\}=2

and
d/2=e_{1}=i=\max\{d_{1},d_{2}\}
, say
d_{1}=d/2
and
d_{2}=2
. Since
d>4
, we have
d_{1}\geqslant 3
, and since
r_{1}
is a primitive prime divisor of
q^{ud_{1}}-1
, it follows that
r_{1}
does not divide
|{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(q^{u})|
, and hence
g
is of the form
g=h\otimes 1\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{d/2}(q^{u})\circ{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(q^{u})
. Also, since
e_{2}=d-e_{1}=d/2
, the same argument shows that
g^{\prime}
is of the form
g^{\prime}=h^{\prime}\otimes 1
. Thus
H=\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle
leaves invariant the
d_{1}
-dimensional subspace
U_{1}\otimes 1
, which contradicts the assumption that
H
is irreducible.

We conclude that no stingray duos
(g,g^{\prime})
with these properties exist, and hence the proportions
{\rm Prob}_{4}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
and
{\rm Prob}_{7}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
are zero. This also means that the families
{\mathcal{M}}_{4}({\mathbf{X}})
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{7}({\mathbf{X}})
are empty for all types
{\mathbf{X}}
.
∎

Remark 8.2
.

The proof of Proposition
8.1
shows that, if a single ppd
e
-stingray element with
e\geqslant d/2
leaves invariant a tensor decomposition
V=U\otimes W
of
V
with
\dim(U)\geqslant\dim(V)
, then
e=d/2=\dim(U)
and
\dim(W)=2
.

9.
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{5}:
Stabilisers of subfields

We use the notation and assumptions from Hypothesis
3.1
, and
in this section we estimate the proportion
{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
of stingray duos
(g,g^{\prime})\in g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}
for which
\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle
preserves an ‘subfield structure’ on
V
, which we explain as follows. We require
ua>1
(where
q=p^{a}
and
G=\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(q)\leqslant{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q^{u})
).
There is a prime
r
dividing
ua
so, in particular, the field
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{u}}
has a unique maximal subfield
{\mathbb{F}}_{0}
of order
q_{0}\coloneq q^{u/r}=p^{ua/r}
, and the
{\mathbb{F}}_{0}
-span of a basis for
V
will be an
{\mathbb{F}}_{0}
-space
V_{0}=\mathbb{F}_{0}^{d}
. The group
\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle
will be contained in the stabiliser
M
in
G
of some such
V_{0}
up to scalars and so, as
{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q^{u})
is transitive on the set of bases of
V
,
M
will be conjugate in
{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q^{u})
to a subgroup of the form
G\cap(Z\circ{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q_{0}))
, where
Z\cong Z_{q^{u}-1}
is the (central) subgroup of scalar matrices of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q^{u})
. Each group
M
has a normal subgroup which is a classical group
Y
depending on both
{\mathbf{X}}
and
r
. We show in Lemma
9.3
that the prime
r
must divide
a
, and that the possibilities for
Y
are as in Table
11
.
The subgroups in
{\mathcal{M}}_{5}(G)
will
be maximal subject to preserving a ‘subfield structure’ on
V
.
Our main result is Proposition
9.1
, and a formal proof is given at the end of the subsection after several preliminary results.

Proposition 9.1
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.1
holds, with
d>8
and
q=p^{a}
for
p
a prime and
a\geqslant 1
. Then
{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=0
if one of
(i)

a=1
, or
(ii)
each prime divisor of
a
divides
\lcm(e_{1},e_{2})
, or
(iii)

{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{L}}
and
a=2
or
q<8
. Otherwise

{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})<\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}4.04\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}+(d-1)/2}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}$}\\
2.11\cdot q^{-(4/3)e_{1}e_{2}+(2/3)(d-1)}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{U}}$}\\
1.82\cdot q^{-(2/3)e_{1}e_{2}+d/3}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}$}\\
2.47\cdot q^{-(2/3)e_{1}e_{2}+d/3}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{+}}$}\\
5.46\cdot q^{-(2/3)e_{1}e_{2}+d/3}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{-}}$.}\\
\end{array}\right.

Moreover we take
{\mathcal{M}}_{5}({\mathbf{X}})=\emptyset
if one of
(i)–(iii)
above holds, and otherwise we take

{\mathcal{M}}_{5}({\mathbf{X}})
as the union, over primes
r
dividing
a
such that
r
does not divide
\lcm(e_{1},e_{2})
, of the
G
-conjugacy classes of groups
M
with normal classical subgroups
Y
as in Table
11
.

Table 11.
Possibilities for the classical group
Y
for
{\mathcal{M}}_{5}({\mathbf{X}})

in Proposition
9.1
, where
q=p^{a}
and the prime
r
divides
a

,

To prove Proposition
9.1
, we work with the following assumptions:

Hypothesis 9.2

(for
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{5}
)
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.1
holds, where
d>8
and
q=p^{a}
for
p
a prime and some
a\geqslant 2
, and also that
{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})>0
. Let
(g,g^{\prime})
be a stingray duo in
g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}
such that
H\coloneq\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle
leaves invariant a ‘subfield structure’ on
V
. Thus there is a prime
r
dividing
ua
and a basis
B
of
V
such that
H
leaves invariant the
{\mathbb{F}}_{0}
-span
V_{0}
of
B
up to scalars, where
q_{0}=|{\mathbb{F}}_{0}|=p^{ua/r}=q^{u/r}
. We may identify
B
with the standard basis of
V
so that
H\leqslant M\coloneq G\cap(Z\circ{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q_{0}))
, where
Z\cong Z_{q^{u}-1}
is the subgroup of scalar matrices of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q^{u})
. We also assume that
H\not\leqslant L
for any
L\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathbf{X}})\cup\dots\cup{\mathcal{M}}_{4}({\mathbf{X}})
.

First we obtain some restrictions on the parameters, and on the possible maximal subgroups
M
in
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{5}
containing
H
.

Lemma 9.3
.

Assume that Hypothesis
9.2
holds. Then

(a)

the prime
r
divides
a
but does not divide
\lcm(e_{1},e_{2})
, and for
i=1,2
, the prime
r_{i}
(see Hypothesis
3.1
)
is a primitive prime divisor of
q_{0}^{e_{i}}-1
. Further, if
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{L}}
then
a\geqslant 3
, and in particular
q=p^{a}\geqslant 8
;

(b)

the group
M
has a classical normal subgroup
Y
, with
Y
as in one of the lines of Table
11
, and in each case
M=N_{G}(Y)
is self-normalising in
G
;

(c)

g,g^{\prime}\in Y
, and moreover,
(g,g^{\prime})
is a ppd
(e_{1},e_{2})
-stingray duo in
Y
,
g_{1}^{G}\cap M=g^{M}=g^{Y}
, and
g_{2}^{G}\cap M=(g^{\prime})^{M}=(g^{\prime})^{Y}
.

In particular, the content of the set
{\mathcal{M}}_{5}(\mathbf{X)}
given in Proposition
9.1
is justified.

Proof.

It follows from
[
21
, Table 4.5.A]
that
M
has a classical normal subgroup
Y
such that
M=N_{G}(M)=N_{G}(Y)
, and either
Y
satisfies one of Lines 1–3 of Table
11
(with
r
odd if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{U}}
) or

(i)

{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}
and
Y={\mathrm{GO}}^{\varepsilon^{\prime}}_{d}(q^{1/r})
with
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{r}
; or

(ii)

{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{U}}
,
r=2
, and
Y={\mathrm{GO}}^{\varepsilon}_{d}(q)
, for some
\varepsilon=\pm
, or
Y={\mathrm{Sp}}_{d}(q)
.

We shall show that, in case (i), the prime
r
is odd so that
\varepsilon=\varepsilon^{\prime}
and Line 4 of Table
11
holds; and we shall show that neither of the possibilities in case (ii) arises. From this it follows from Table
11
that in each line
r
divides
a
; if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
then
q\geqslant p^{r}\geqslant p^{2}
, while if
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{L}}
, then the prime
r
is odd so
a\geqslant r\geqslant 3
and
q\geqslant p^{r}\geqslant p^{3}\geqslant 8
(yielding information in the ‘Consequences’ column of Table
11
).

First we consider the elements
g,g^{\prime}\in M
. Since
e_{1}\geqslant e_{2}\geqslant 2
, neither of their orders
r_{1},r_{2}
divides
|Z|=q^{u}-1
, and hence
g,g^{\prime}\in Y\leqslant{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q_{0})
. Consider the element
g
(the situation for
g^{\prime}
is identical). Since
g
is an
e_{1}
-stingray element on
V
, the characteristic polynomial
c_{g}(t)=\det(tI_{d}-g)
for
g
on
V
has the form
f(t)(t-1)^{d-e_{1}}
, with
f(t)
irreducible over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{u}}
of degree
e_{1}
. Moreover, since
g\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q_{0})<{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q^{u})
where
q_{0}=q^{u/r}
, the polynomial
c_{g}(t)
has coefficients in
{\mathbb{F}}_{0}
, and hence also
f(t)
has coefficients in
{\mathbb{F}}_{0}
. Thus
f(t)
is irreducible over
{\mathbb{F}}_{0}
and
r_{1}=|g|
divides
q_{0}^{e_{1}}-1
. If
r
divides
e_{1}
then
q_{0}^{e_{1}}-1=(q^{u})^{e_{1}/r}-1
, and hence
r_{1}
divides
(q^{u})^{e_{1}/r}-1
, which is a contradiction. Hence
r
does not divide
e_{1}
. Now let
j
be the least positive integer such that
r_{1}
divides
q_{0}^{j}-1
. Then
j\leqslant e_{1}
since we just proved that
r_{1}
divides
q_{0}^{e_{1}}-1
. Also
j\geqslant e_{1}
since
r_{1}
also divides
(q_{0}^{j})^{r}-1=q^{uj}-1
and by definition
r_{1}
is a primitive prime divisor of
(q^{u})^{e_{1}}-1
. Hence
j=e_{1}
. Thus
r_{1}
is a primitive prime divisor of
q_{0}^{e_{1}}-1
, and it follows that
g
is a ppd
e_{1}
-stingray element of
Y
acting on
V_{0}
. An identical argument shows that
r
does not divide
e_{2}
, that
r_{2}
is a primitive prime divisor of
q_{0}^{e_{2}}-1
, and that
g^{\prime}
is a ppd
e_{2}
-stingray element of
Y
. In particular we have shown that
r\nmid\lcm(e_{1},e_{2})
.

Next we prove our assertions about the possibilities in (i) and (ii) above.
If
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{U}}
, then the
e_{i}
are odd (see Table
3
). However if in this case the prime
r=2
and
Y
is an orthogonal or symplectic group, then the
e_{i}
should be even by Lemma
2.5
(b) and Table
3
, which is a contradiction. Hence the exceptional cases in (ii) above do not arise. Moreover, if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
or
\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}
, then both
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are even (see Table
3
) and since
r\nmid\lcm(e_{1},e_{2})
, the prime
r
must be odd. In case (i) above this means that
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{r}=\varepsilon^{\prime}
and hence that Line 4 of Table
11
holds. This justifies all the entries of Table
11
, and, as mentioned above, we now have that
r
divides
a
.
The fact that in all cases
M=N_{G}(Y)
is self-normalising follows from
[
21
, Proposition 4.5.1]
.
This completes the proofs of parts (a) and (b), and justifies the content of the set
{\mathcal{M}}_{5}({\mathbf{X}})
given in Proposition
9.1
.

We showed above that
g,g^{\prime}\in Y
. By Lemma
2.5
(c) applied to
g
as an element of
G
, and of
Y
, we see that there are unique cyclic tori
T
of
G
, and
T_{0}
of
Y
containing
g
such that
C_{G}(g)=C_{G}(T)
and
C_{Y}(g)=C_{Y}(T_{0})
. Moreover
T,T_{0}
preserve the decompositions
V=U_{g}\oplus F_{g}
and
V_{0}=U_{0,g}\oplus F_{0,g}
, with
U_{g},F_{g}
and
U_{0,g},F_{0,g}
as in Definition
2.2
for
g
as an element of
G,Y
respectively.
It follows that
F_{0,g}=F_{g}\cap V_{0}
and also that
U_{0,g}=U_{g}\cap V_{0}
by the uniqueness property in Lemma
2.4
(c). Then, since
g\in Y
, we have
g\in T_{0}<T
. The same properties hold for
g^{\prime}
as an element of
G
and
Y
, with subspaces
U_{0,g^{\prime}}=U_{g^{\prime}}\cap V_{0}
and
F_{0,g^{\prime}}=F_{g^{\prime}}\cap V_{0}
. Since
(g,g^{\prime})
is a stingray duo in
G
we have
U_{g}\cap U_{g^{\prime}}=0
, and hence also
U_{0,g}\cap U_{0,g^{\prime}}=0
, so
(g,g^{\prime})
is a stingray duo in
Y
. Since
r_{i}
is a primitive prime divisor of
q_{0}^{e_{i}}-1
, for each
i
by part (a),
(g,g^{\prime})
is a ppd
(e_{1},e_{2})
-stingray duo in
Y
, and the first assertion in part (c) is proved.

Finally we consider
g_{i}^{G}\cap M
. Now
Y
is normal in
M
and the index
|M:Y|
is coprime to
r_{i}
(see
[
21
, Propositions 4.5.3, 4.5.4, 4.5.10]
). It follows that
g_{i}^{G}\cap M=g_{i}^{G}\cap Y
. By Lemma
2.5
(c), the torus
T
in the previous paragraph contains exactly
e_{1}
elements of
g_{1}^{G}
, and these form a single orbit under the conjugation action of
N_{G}(T)
. Applying this result to
g
as an element of
Y
, we see that the torus
T_{0}
contains exactly
e_{1}
elements of
g^{Y}
and these form a single orbit under the conjugation action of
N_{Y}(T_{0})
. Since
g^{Y}\subseteq g_{1}^{G}
, it follows that
g_{1}^{G}\cap T=g_{1}^{G}\cap{T_{0}}=g^{N_{Y}(T_{0})}
. Finally, since each element of
g_{1}^{G}\cap M
lies in a cyclic torus
T_{0}^{\prime}
which is conjugate in
Y
to
T_{0}
, it follows that
g_{1}^{G}\cap M
forms a single
Y
-conjugacy class, namely
g^{Y}=g^{M}
. An identical argument shows that
g_{2}^{G}\cap M=(g^{\prime})^{M}=(g^{\prime})^{Y}
. This completes the proof of part (c).
∎

It follows from Lemma
9.3
that, for each
{\mathbf{X}}
, the set
{\mathcal{M}}_{5}({\mathbf{X}})
is partitioned according to the primes
r
dividing
a
and not dividing
\lcm(e_{1},e_{2})
: let
{\mathcal{M}}_{5}({\mathbf{X}},r)
denote the set of subgroups in
{\mathcal{M}}_{5}({\mathbf{X}})
occurring in line
{\mathbf{X}}
of Table
11
for this particular prime
r
. Then

(29)

{\mathcal{M}}_{5}({\mathbf{X}})=\bigcup_{r}{\mathcal{M}}_{5}({\mathbf{X}},r)\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant\sum_{r}{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};r),

where
{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};r)
is
the contribution to (
11
) from subgroups
M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{5}({\mathbf{X}},r)
containing the element
g
. We show in Lemma
9.4
(a) that each non-empty
{\mathcal{M}}_{5}({\mathbf{X}},r)
is a single
G
-conjugacy class.

Lemma 9.4
.

Assume that Hypothesis
9.2
holds, and let
{\mathcal{M}}_{5}({\mathbf{X}},r)
and
{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};r)
be as in (
29
), for each
{\mathbf{X}}
and each prime
r
dividing
a
but not dividing
\lcm(e_{1},e_{2})
. Then

(a)

the set
{\mathcal{M}}_{5}({\mathbf{X}},r)
is a single
G
-conjugacy class; and

(b)

the following upper bounds hold

{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};r)<\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}4\cdot q^{-(1-1/r)(2e_{1}e_{2}-d+1)}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}$}\\
2.08\cdot q^{-(1-1/r)(2e_{1}e_{2}-d+1)}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{U}}$}\\
1.8\cdot q^{-(2e_{1}e_{2}-d)(1-1/r)/2}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}$}\\
2.44\cdot q^{-(2e_{1}e_{2}-d)(1-1/r)/2}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{+}}$}\\
5.4\cdot q^{-(2e_{1}e_{2}-d)(1-1/r)/2}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{-}}$.}\\
\end{array}\right.

Proof.

(a) By the discussion at the beginning of this section, for each
r
as in part (a), the group
{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q^{u})
acts transitively by conjugation on the subgroups
M
stabilising a subfield structure over the field of order
q^{u/r}
, so if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
then the subgroups
M=N_{G}(Y)
as in Line 1 of Table
11
form a single
{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q)
-conjugacy class of subgroups of type
{\mathbf{L}}
. For all other types, and for each prime
r
, the fact that the subgroups
M=N_{G}(Y)
in the relevant line of Table
11
form a single
G
-conjugacy class of subgroups
N_{G}(Y)
with
Y
classical of type
{\mathbf{X}}
essentially follows from ‘Witt’s Lemma’ on the uniqueness of these geometries; we refer to
[
21
, Propositions 2.3.1, 2.4.1 and 2.5.3]
for the unitary, symplectic and orthogonal types, respectively. This proves part (a).

(b) We estimate the contribution
{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};r)
to
{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
from the subgroups in
{\mathcal{M}}_{5}({\mathbf{X}},r)
. Recall that
q_{0}=q^{1/r}=p^{a/r}
. By part (a), these subgroups are all
G
-conjugate, and we choose a subgroup
M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{5}({\mathbf{X}},r)
and an element
g\in g_{1}^{G}\cap M
. By Lemmas
2.7
and
9.3
(c), the number of
G
-conjugates
L\in{\mathcal{M}}_{5}({\mathbf{X}},r)
such that
g\in L
is
|C_{G}(g_{1})|/|C_{M}(g)|
.
Thus, by (
11
),

(30)

{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};r)\leqslant\frac{|C_{G}(g_{1})|}{|C_{M}(g)|}\cdot\frac{|\mathcal{S}_{5}(g,M)|}{|N(d,q^{u},e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}=\frac{|C_{G}(g_{1})|}{|N(d,q^{u},e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}\cdot\frac{|\mathcal{S}_{5}(g,M)|}{|C_{M}(g)|},

with
|N(d,q^{u},e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|
as in (
12
), and
\mathcal{S}_{5}(g,M)
as in (
9
), namely the set of all
g^{\prime}\in g_{2}^{G}\cap M
such that
(g,g^{\prime})
is a stingray duo and
\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle
is not contained in a subgroup of
{\mathcal{M}}_{j}({\mathbf{X}})
for any
j\leqslant 4
.
The first factor in the last expression in (
30
) is determined in Proposition
4.2
or
4.4
.
We need to estimate the second factor in this expression.
Note that, by Lemma
9.3
(b),
M=N_{G}(Y)
with
Y
a classical subgroup as in Table
11
, and so we have
|C_{M}(g)|=x({\mathbf{X}})\cdot|C_{Y}(g)|
, where
x({\mathbf{X}})
is
(q-1)/(q_{0}-1)
or
(q+1)/(q_{0}+1)
for
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
or
{\mathbf{U}}
, and is at most
2
for the other types. Also, by Lemma
9.3
(c), if
g^{\prime}\in g_{2}^{G}\cap M
then
g^{\prime}\in Y
and
g_{2}^{G}\cap M=(g^{\prime})^{Y}
. Observe that the type
{\mathbf{Y}}
of
Y
is
{\mathbf{X}}
by Table
11
. Thus

|\mathcal{S}_{5}(g,M)|\leqslant\#\{h\in(g^{\prime})^{Y}\mid\mbox{$(g,h)$ is a stingray duo on $V_{0}$}\}=|N(d,q_{0},e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|,

with
N(d,q_{0},e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})
as in (
12
). Now the conditions of Proposition
4.2
or
4.4
hold with
Y,q_{0},g,g^{\prime}
replacing
G,q,g_{1},g_{2}
, yielding an expression for
|C_{Y}(g)|/|N(d,q_{0},e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|
. Putting this information together we have

(31)

{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};r)\leqslant\frac{|C_{G}(g_{1})|}{|N(d,q^{u},e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}\cdot\frac{|N(d,q_{0},e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}{x({\mathbf{X}})\cdot|C_{Y}(g)|},

and we now apply Proposition
4.2
or
4.4

separately for each type.
For
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
, we have

{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}};r)\leqslant\frac{(q^{e_{1}}-1)(q^{e_{2}}-1)}{q^{2e_{1}e_{2}}}\cdot\frac{q_{0}^{2e_{1}e_{2}}}{(q-1)/(q_{0}-1)\cdot(q_{0}^{e_{1}}-1)(q_{0}^{e_{2}}-1)}.

Now
(q^{e_{1}}-1)/(q_{0}^{e_{1}}-1)=1+q_{0}^{e_{1}}+\dots+q_{0}^{e_{1}(r-1)}<2q_{0}^{e_{1}(r-1)}=2q^{e_{1}(1-1/r)}
, and similarly
(q^{e_{2}}-1)/(q_{0}^{e_{2}}-1)<2q^{e_{2}(1-1/r)}
, while
(q-1)/(q_{0}-1)>q^{1-1/r}
. Hence

{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}};r)<4q^{-(1-1/r)(2e_{1}e_{2}-e_{1}-e_{2}+1)}.

For
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{U}}
, we have
r
odd, and so
q^{2}\geqslant 2^{6}
as
q\geqslant 8
by Lemma
9.3
(a). Thus, using the bounds
\frac{125}{128}\leqslant 1-3/(2q^{2})\leqslant\mathbf{k}(d,q^{u},e_{1},{\mathbf{U}})<1
, we obtain

\displaystyle{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{U}};r)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{(q^{e_{1}}+1)(q^{e_{2}}+1)}{\mathbf{k}(d,q^{2},e_{1},{\mathbf{U}})\cdot|\mathcal{U}(d,q^{2},e_{1},{\mathbf{U}}|}\cdot\frac{\mathbf{k}(d,q_{0}^{2},e_{1},{\mathbf{U}})\cdot|\mathcal{U}(d,q_{0}^{2},e_{1},{\mathbf{U}})|}{(q+1)/(q_{0}+1)\cdot(q_{0}^{e_{1}}+1)(q_{0}^{e_{2}}+1)}

\displaystyle<\frac{|\mathcal{U}(d,q_{0}^{2},e_{1},{\mathbf{U}})|}{(125/128)\cdot|\mathcal{U}(d,q^{2},e_{1},{\mathbf{U}})|}\cdot\frac{(q^{e_{1}}+1)(q^{e_{2}}+1)}{(q+1)/(q_{0}+1)\cdot(q_{0}^{e_{1}}+1)(q_{0}^{e_{2}}+1)}

\displaystyle<\frac{{\bf b}(q_{0}^{2},{\mathbf{U}})\,q_{0}^{2e_{1}e_{2}}}{(125/128)\cdot{\bf a}(q^{2},{\mathbf{U}})\,q^{2e_{1}e_{2}}}\cdot\frac{(q^{e_{1}}+1)(q^{e_{2}}+1)}{(q+1)/(q_{0}+1)\cdot(q_{0}^{e_{1}}+1)(q_{0}^{e_{2}}+1)}.

Next, we note since
r
is odd that, for
e\in\{1,e_{1},e_{2}\}
,

(32)

\frac{q^{e}+1}{q_{0}^{e}+1}=q^{e(1-1/r)}\frac{1+q_{0}^{-er}}{1+q_{0}^{-e}}<q^{e(1-1/r)}.

Also, taking
e=1
, we see that

\frac{q+1}{q_{0}+1}>q^{1-1/r}\frac{1}{1+q_{0}^{-1}}\geqslant\frac{2}{3}q^{1-1/r}.

Thus the second term in the upper bound for
{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{U}};r)
is less than

\frac{q^{e_{1}(1-1/r)}\cdot q^{e_{2}(1-1/r)}}{(2/3)q^{1-1/r}}=\frac{3}{2}q^{(1-1/r)(e_{1}+e_{2}-1)}.

Then, using the upper and lower bounds in Proposition
4.4
with
q^{2}>q_{0}^{2}\geqslant 4
, we have

\displaystyle{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{U}};r)

\displaystyle<\frac{(q^{1/r})^{2e_{1}e_{2}}}{(125/128)\cdot(20/27)\cdot q^{2e_{1}e_{2}}}\cdot(3/2)\cdot q^{(1-1/r)(e_{1}+e_{2}-1)}

\displaystyle<2.08\cdot q^{-(1-1/r)(2e_{1}e_{2}-e_{1}-e_{2}+1)}.

In the last two cases
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
or
\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}
,
r
is odd, and so
q=q_{0}^{r}\geqslant 8
. Thus, using the bounds

13/16\leqslant 1-3/(2q)\leqslant\mathbf{k}(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})<1
and
1\leqslant x({\mathbf{X}})\leqslant 2
, and setting
\nu=1
if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
and
\nu=\varepsilon\cdot 1
if
{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}
we obtain, in (
31
),

\displaystyle{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};r)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{(q^{e_{1}/2}+1)(q^{e_{2}/2}+\nu)}{\mathbf{k}(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})\cdot|\mathcal{U}(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}\cdot\frac{\mathbf{k}(d,q_{0},e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})\cdot|\mathcal{U}(d,q_{0},e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}{x({\mathbf{X}})\cdot(q_{0}^{e_{1}/2}+1)(q_{0}^{e_{2}/2}+\nu)}

\displaystyle<\frac{|\mathcal{U}(d,q_{0},e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}{(13/16)\cdot|\mathcal{U}(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}\cdot\frac{(q^{e_{1}/2}+1)(q^{e_{2}/2}+\nu)}{(q_{0}^{e_{1}/2}+1)(q_{0}^{e_{2}/2}+\nu)}.

Replacing
q,q_{0}
in (
32
) with
q^{1/2},q_{0}^{1/2}
gives
(q^{e/2}+1)/(q_{0}^{e/2}+1)<q^{(e/2)(1-1/r)}
, for
e\in\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
,
while
(q^{e_{2}/2}-1)/(q_{0}^{e_{2}/2}-1)<2q^{(e_{2}/2)(1-1/r)}
. Then, setting
y({\mathbf{X}})=1
if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
or
\mathbf{O^{+}}
(the cases where
\nu=1
) and
y({\mathbf{X}})=2
if
{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{-}}
(the case where
\nu=-1
), the second term in the above expression is less than
y({\mathbf{X}})\cdot q^{(e_{i}+e_{2})(1-1/r)/2}
. For the first term in the above expression we use the
inequalities
in Proposition
4.4
, and find

\displaystyle{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};r)

\displaystyle<\frac{{\mathbf{b}}(q_{0},{\mathbf{X}})\cdot q_{0}^{e_{1}e_{2}}\cdot y({\mathbf{X}})\cdot q^{(e_{1}+e_{2})(1-1/r)/2}}{(13/16)\cdot{\mathbf{a}}(q,{\mathbf{X}})\cdot q^{e_{1}e_{2}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(16/13)\cdot y({\mathbf{X}})\cdot{\mathbf{b}}(q_{0},{\mathbf{X}})}{{\mathbf{a}}(q,{\mathbf{X}})}\cdot q^{-(2e_{1}e_{2}-e_{1}-e_{2})(1-1/r)/2}

and from Table
7
with
q\geqslant q_{0}^{2}\geqslant 4
, we have
(16/13)\cdot y({\mathbf{X}})\cdot{\mathbf{b}}(q_{0},{\mathbf{X}})/{\mathbf{a}}(q,{\mathbf{X}})
is
\frac{16}{13}\cdot\frac{16}{11}/1=256/143<1.8
if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
, and is
\frac{16}{13}\cdot\frac{8}{11}/\frac{47}{128}<2.44
if
{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{+}}
, and is
\frac{16}{13}\cdot 2\cdot\frac{12}{11}/\frac{1}{2}<5.4
if
{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{-}}
.
∎

Finally we prove Proposition
9.1
.

Proof of Proposition
9.1
.

Recall that
q=p^{a}
. By Lemma
9.3
,
{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=0
if
a=1
, or if each prime divisor of
a
divides
\lcm(e_{1},e_{2})
, or if
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{L}}
and
a=2
or
q<8
; and in general we have
{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant\sum_{r}{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};r)
as in (
29
), where the sum is over all primes
r
dividing
a
such that
r
does not divide
\lcm(e_{1},e_{2})
. An upper bound for
{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};r)
is given by Lemma
9.4
, and in all cases the upper bound can be expressed as
Y_{\mathbf{X}}Q_{\mathbf{X}}^{a/r}
, with both
Y_{\mathbf{X}},Q_{\mathbf{X}}
positive quantities depending on
p
and
a
, but not
r
. In particular we take
Q_{\mathbf{L}}=Q_{\mathbf{U}}=p^{2e_{1}e_{2}-d+1}
and
Q_{\mathbf{Sp}}=Q_{\mathbf{O}^{\varepsilon}}=p^{e_{1}e_{2}-d/2}
. Then, for all
{\mathbf{X}}
, we have, since
d\geqslant 9
,

Q_{\mathbf{X}}\geqslant p^{e_{1}e_{2}-d/2}\geqslant 2^{(d-2)2-d/2}=2^{3d/2-4}>2^{9},\ \mbox{and}\ \sum_{i=0}^{\infty}Q_{\mathbf{X}}^{-i}=1/(1-Q_{\mathbf{X}}^{-1})<1.01.

Consider first
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
. Here by Lemma
9.4
,
Y_{\mathbf{L}}=4Q_{\mathbf{L}}^{-a}
. Let
r_{0}
be the smallest prime dividing
a
, so
a/r\leqslant a/2
, and we have

\displaystyle{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})

\displaystyle<Y_{\mathbf{L}}\sum_{r|a}Q_{\mathbf{L}}^{a/r}\leqslant Y_{\mathbf{L}}Q_{\mathbf{L}}^{a/r_{0}}\sum_{i=0}^{a/r_{0}}Q_{\mathbf{L}}^{-i}

\displaystyle<Y_{\mathbf{L}}Q_{\mathbf{L}}^{a/2}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}Q_{\mathbf{L}}^{-i}<1.01\cdot Y_{\mathbf{L}}Q_{\mathbf{L}}^{a/2}=4.04\cdot Q_{\mathbf{L}}^{-a/2}.

This gives the upper bound
{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})<4.04\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}+(d-1)/2}
in Proposition
9.1
. For all other types
{\mathbf{X}}
,
r
is odd by Table
11
, so
a/r\leqslant a/3
, and hence

(33)

{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})<Y_{\mathbf{X}}\sum_{r|a}Q_{\mathbf{X}}^{a/r}<Y_{\mathbf{X}}Q_{\mathbf{X}}^{a/3}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}Q_{\mathbf{X}}^{-i}<1.01\cdot Y_{\mathbf{X}}Q_{\mathbf{X}}^{a/3}.

If
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{U}}
then, by Lemma
9.4
,
Q_{\mathbf{U}}=Q_{\mathbf{L}}
and
Y_{\mathbf{U}}=2.08\cdot Q_{\mathbf{L}}^{-a}
. Hence, by (
33
),
{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{U}})<2.11\cdot Q_{\mathbf{L}}^{-2a/3}=2.11\cdot q^{-(4/3)e_{1}e_{2}+(2/3)(d-1)}
, as in Proposition
9.1
.
Finally, for
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
,
{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{+}}
, or
{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{-}}
, we have, by Lemma
9.4
,
Q_{\mathbf{X}}=p^{e_{1}e_{2}-d/2}
and
Y_{\mathbf{X}}=c_{\mathbf{X}}Q_{\mathbf{X}}^{-a}
, where
c_{\mathbf{X}}=1.8,2.44
, or
5.4
, respectively. Hence, by (
33
),
{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})<1.01\cdot c_{\mathbf{X}}\cdot Q_{\mathbf{X}}^{-2a/3}=1.01\cdot c_{\mathbf{X}}\cdot q^{-(2/3)e_{1}e_{2}+d/3}
, and the bounds in Proposition
9.1
follow.
∎

10.
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{6}:
normalisers of symplectic type
s
-groups

We use the notation and assumptions from Hypothesis
3.1
, and deal with the Aschbacher category
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{6}
, where the dimension
d=s^{n}
for some prime divisor
s
of
q^{u}-1
and
n\geqslant 1
.
In this section we estimate the proportion
{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
of stingray duos
(g,g^{\prime})\in g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}
for which
\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle
lies in the normaliser of an extraspecial group of order
s^{1+2n}
, or a symplectic type group of order
2^{2+2n}
with
s=2
, and exponent
s\cdot\gcd(2,s)
(see
[
21
, Chapter 4.6]
or
[
3
, Definition 2.2.13]
). In particular, we shall see that for a non-zero contribution to
{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
, the prime
s
must be
2
. Our main result is Proposition
10.2
, and a formal proof is given after proving a crucial technical Lemma
10.4
.

Remark 10.1
.

Our approach to analysing this case has been influenced by the approach in
[
33
, Section 10]
. However, in working through our more general estimation problem for
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{6}
groups, we discovered an error in the proof of
[
33
, Lemma 10.1]
. A valid upper bound for the proportion in this case in the situation in
[
33
, Section 10]
is given by our Proposition
10.2
(a) (since
s=2
and
e_{1}=e_{2}
in
[
33
]
). This error does not affect the main result of
[
33
]
. (The problem in
[
33
, Lemma 10.1]
, where
\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle
normalises a 2-group
R
of type
2^{1+2n}
or
4\circ 2^{1+2n}
, is that the proof implicitly assumed that the stingray elements left invariant each nontrivial
R
-orbit in
V
, and this was not justified.)

Proposition 10.2
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.1
holds. Then

(a)

{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=0
if any one of the following holds:

(i)

d\neq 2^{n}
for some
n\geqslant 4
, or
q
is not an odd prime;

(ii)

(e_{1},e_{2})\neq(d/2,d/2)
or
(d-2,2)
;

(iii)

{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{U}}
or
{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{-}}
;

(b)

and otherwise
d=2^{n}
for some
n\geqslant 4
,
q
is an odd prime,
{\mathbf{X}}\in\{{\mathbf{L}},{\mathbf{Sp}},\mathbf{O^{+}}\}
, and if
{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})>0
then either

(i)

(e_{1},e_{2})=(d/2,d/2)
,
r_{1}=r_{2}
is
e_{1}+1=2^{n-1}+1
or
2e_{1}+1=2^{n}+1
, and

{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})<\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{45}{1024}\cdot q^{-d^{2}/4}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}$}\\
\frac{45}{1024}\cdot q^{-d^{2}/4+23d/10}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}$ or $\mathbf{O^{+}}$;}\\
\end{array}\right.

(ii)

or
(e_{1},e_{2})=(d-2,2)
, with
d=2^{n}\geqslant 32
,
q\geqslant 5
, and
n,q
both odd primes as in Lemma
10.4
(b)(iii)
,
r_{1}=e_{1}+1=2^{n}-1
,
r_{2}
is an odd prime divisor of
q+1
(in particular if
n=5
then
q\geqslant 11
), and

{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})<\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\frac{45}{640}\cdot q^{-\frac{59}{25}d+8}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}$}\\
5.3\cdot 10^{-3}\cdot q^{-d+3}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}$ or $\mathbf{O^{+}}$ and either $d\geqslant 128$ or $q\geqslant 19$}\\
6.9\cdot q^{-d+3}&\mbox{if ${\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}$ or $\mathbf{O^{+}}$ and $d=32$ with $q\in\{11,13,17\}$.}\end{array}\right.

Moreover we take
{\mathcal{M}}_{6}({\mathbf{X}})=\emptyset
if any of the conditions of part
(a)
hold, or if
r_{1},r_{2}
are not as in part
(b)
, and otherwise

{\mathcal{M}}_{6}({\mathbf{X}})
is the

-conjugacy class of subgroups

occurring in Table

.

To prove Proposition

, we work with the following assumptions.

.

First we obtain some restrictions on the parameters, and on the subgroup

.

Lemma 10.4
.

Assume that Hypothesis
10.3
holds. Then

(a)

the prime

,

,

. In particular,

.

(b)

One of the following holds for the parameters

and
r_{i}
:

(i)

e_{1}=e_{2}=d/2=2^{n-1}
and
r_{1}=r_{2}=2^{n-1}+1
with
n-1=2^{n_{0}}\geqslant 4
,

(ii)

e_{1}=e_{2}=d/2=2^{n-1}
and
r_{1}=r_{2}=2^{n}+1
with
n=2^{n_{0}}\geqslant 4
,

(iii)

e_{1}=d-2=2^{n}-2
,
r_{1}=e_{1}+1=2^{n}-1
with

an odd prime,
n\geqslant 5
,
q\geqslant 5
, and
e_{2}=2
with
r_{2}
an odd prime divisor of
q+1
, so
q\neq 3,7
.
Moreover (recalling that
q,n
are odd primes), either
n\geqslant 7
with
q\geqslant 5
, or
n=5
with
q\geqslant 11
.

(c)

For all
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{O^{-}}
, there is a single
G
-conjugacy class of subgroups
M
as in Table
12
, and for a fixed
g\in g_{1}^{G}\cap M
, the number of
G
-conjugates of

containing
g
is

Y=|C_{G}(g_{1})|\cdot e_{1}/|N_{M}(\langle g\rangle)|,

and
|g_{1}^{G}\cap M|=e_{1}\cdot|M|/|N_{M}(\langle g\rangle)|
.
Moreover,
|N_{M}(\langle g\rangle)|
is given by the following table, where
z\coloneq|Z(M)|=|Z(R)|
as in Table
12
.
