In particular Proposition
10.2
(a)
is proved, and the content of the set
{\mathcal{M}}_{6}(\mathbf{X)}
given in Proposition
10.2
is justified.

Proof.

(a) If
e_{1}>d/2
then, by
[
17
, Lemma 4.3 and Example 2.5]
, it follows that
s=2
. On the other hand, if
e_{1}=d/2
then
d=s^{n}
is even and again
s=2
. Thus in all cases
s=2
, and hence
n\geqslant 4
(since
d=2^{n}>8
) and
q
is odd (since
s
divides
q^{u}-1
). It follows from
[
21
, Chapter 4.6, Table 4.6.B]
(or see
[
3
, Table 2.9]
) that
M=N_{G}(R)
is self-normalising with
R
and
M
as in one of the lines of Table
12
, or
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{U}}
,
R=(q+1)\circ 2^{1+2n}
, and
M=R.{\mathrm{Sp}}_{2n}(2)
with
q=p\equiv 3\pmod{4}
.
Moreover since
\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle
lies in no subgroup in
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\mathbf{X}})\cup\dots\cup{\mathcal{M}}_{5}({\mathbf{X}})
, it follows in particular that
M
does not preserve a subfield structure on
V
, and hence
q
is an odd prime (by
[
21
, Proposition 4.6.3(ii) and Table 4.6.B]
, since
s=2
). In particular,
{\mathbf{X}}\neq\mathbf{O^{-}}
, and we will show below that the unitary case does not arise; apart from this part (a) is proved.

(b) Now for all types
{\mathbf{X}}
,
the group
M
is contained in a subgroup of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q^{u})
of the form
\widehat{M}\coloneq(Z_{q^{u}-1}\circ 2^{1+2n})\ldotp{\mathrm{Sp}}_{2n}(2)
(see
[
21
, Section 4.6]
).
We consider first the ppd
e_{1}
-stingray element
g
of
M
. Since
|g|=r_{1}
does not divide
q^{u}-1
(as
e_{1}\geqslant 2
), it follows that
r_{1}
is odd and
g
does not lie in
the normal subgroup
\widehat{R}\coloneq Z_{q^{u}-1}\circ 2^{1+2n}
of
\widehat{M}
. Hence
\widehat{R}g
is a semisimple element of
\widehat{M}/\widehat{R}\cong{\mathrm{Sp}}_{2n}(2)
, so
r_{1}
divides
2^{j}\pm 1
for some least positive integer
j\leqslant n
. Since
r_{1}
is a primitive prime divisor of
(q^{u})^{e_{1}}-1
, also
r_{1}=ke_{1}+1
for some positive integer
k
, and so
2^{j}+1\geqslant r_{1}=ke_{1}+1\geqslant kd/2+1\geqslant 2^{n-1}+1
. In particular
k\leqslant 2
. Suppose first that
k=2
. Then
r_{1}=2^{n}+1
and
e_{1}=d/2
, so also
e_{2}=d/2
. Since
r_{1}=2^{n}+1
is a (Fermat) prime, the exponent
n
must be a
2
-power
n=2^{n_{0}}\geqslant 4
. Exactly the same argument applied to
(g^{\prime},r_{2})
yields that either
r_{2}=r_{1}
, so that part (b)(ii) holds, or
r_{2}=e_{2}+1=2^{n-1}+1
. However, in the latter case, for
r_{2}
to be prime, we would also require
n-1=2^{n_{0}}-1
to be a
2
-power, which is a contradiction since
n\geqslant 4
.

Thus we now assume that
k=1
, so
2^{j}+1\geqslant r_{1}=e_{1}+1\geqslant 2^{n-1}+1
. Suppose next that
j<n
. Then
r_{1}=2^{n-1}+1
and
e_{1}=d/2
, so also
e_{2}=d/2
. As before, since
r_{1}=2^{n-1}+1
is a (Fermat) prime, the exponent
n-1
must be a
2
-power, so
n-1=2^{n_{0}}\geqslant 3
and this must be at least 4 for
r_{1}
to be prime. Applying this argument to
(g^{\prime},r_{2})
yields that either
r_{2}=r_{1}
, so that part (b)(i) holds, or
r_{2}=2e_{2}+1=2^{n}+1
. However in the latter case, for
r_{2}
to be prime we would also require
n+1=2^{n_{0}}+1
to be a
2
-power, which is a contradiction. Thus we may assume that
j=n
so
r_{1}
divides
2^{n}\pm 1
. We also have
r_{1}=e_{1}+1\leqslant 2^{n}-1
since
e_{1}\leqslant d-2
. Thus
2^{n-1}+1\leqslant r_{1}\leqslant 2^{n}-1
and
r_{1}
divides
2^{n}\pm 1
. The only possibility is
r_{1}=2^{n}-1
with
e_{1}=d-2
. For
r_{1}=2^{n}-1
to be prime we require that
n
is an odd prime and
n\geqslant 5
(since
n\geqslant 4
). Also
e_{2}=2
so
r_{2}
divides
q^{u}+1
. We note at this point that in all cases the
e_{i}
are even, and hence
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{U}}
, by Table
3
, completing the proof of part (a). This implies that
u=1
, and hence in the case covered by (b)(iii),
r_{2}
is an odd prime divisor of
q+1
, so
q\neq 3,7
, that is, either
q=5
or
q\geqslant 11
(as
q
is an odd prime). To complete the proof it remains to show that, in case (b)(iii), the case
q=n=5
is not possible: we note in this exceptional case that

e_{1}=d-2=30
, and
r_{1}=31
with
r_{1}
a ppd of
q^{30}-1
. However, the
prime
31
already divides
5^{3}-1
, so the ppd condition on
r_{1}
fails. This completes the proof of part (b).

(c) In type
{\mathbf{L}}
, all subgroups
M
as in Line 1 of Table
12
are
G
-conjugate by
[
21
, Proposition 4.6.3(ii) and Lemma 2.10.14]
, while the same is true for types
{\mathbf{Sp}}
and
\mathbf{O^{+}}
by
[
21
, Proposition 4.6.3(i)]
(since
Z(R)=Z_{2}
).

Now we consider
g_{1}^{G}\cap M
. By Lemma
2.5
(c), each
h\in g_{1}^{G}
lies in a unique cyclic torus
T
and each such
T
contains exactly
e_{1}
elements of
g_{1}^{G}
. Moreover these
e_{1}
elements all lie in the unique subgroup of
T
of order
r_{1}
. Now fix
g\in g_{1}^{G}\cap M
.
By part (b) it follows that the only factor
2^{2j}-1
of
|{\mathrm{Sp}}_{2n}(2)|
which is divisible by
r_{1}
has
j=n-1,n,n
in parts (i), (ii), (iii), respectively; and it follows that in all three cases
\langle g\rangle\cong Z_{r_{1}}
is a Sylow
r_{1}
-subgroup of
M
. Thus
|g_{1}^{G}\cap M|
is equal to
|M:N_{M}(\langle g\rangle)|
(the number of Sylow
r_{1}
-subgroups of
M
) times
e_{1}
(the number of
g_{1}
-conjugates contained in each of these Sylow subgroups), that is to say (since
M=N_{G}(M)
),

|g_{1}^{G}\cap M|=e_{1}\cdot|M|/|N_{M}(\langle g\rangle)|.

Let
Y
be the number of
M
-conjugates containing
g
. Then, the number of pairs
(h,L)
such that
h\in g_{1}^{G},L\in M^{G}
, and
h\in L
, satisfies

\frac{|G|}{|C_{G}(g_{1})|}\cdot Y=\frac{|G|}{|M|}\cdot|g_{1}^{G}\cap M|=\frac{|G|\cdot e_{1}}{|N_{M}(\langle g\rangle)|},

and hence
Y=|C_{G}(g_{1})|\cdot e_{1}/|N_{M}(\langle g\rangle)|
. Finally we determine
|N_{M}(\langle g\rangle)|
.

We claim that
|N_{M}(\langle g\rangle):C_{M}(g)|=2j
(with
j=n-1,n,n
as above). To see this we note that
Rg
generates a cyclic torus, say
T_{0}
, of
M/R
(which is
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{2n}(2)
or
{\mathrm{SO}}_{2n}^{\pm}(2)
) of order
r_{1}=2^{n-1}+1,2^{n}+1,2^{n}-1
, respectively. The normaliser of
T_{0}
modulo its centraliser is cyclic of order
2j=2(n-1),2n,2n
, respectively, and the claim follows. This means that
|N_{M}(\langle g\rangle)|=2j|C|
, where
C\coloneq C_{M}(g)
. To determine
C
we note first that
C
contains
Z(M)
, and we have
C/Z(M)\leqslant M/Z(M)=2^{2n}\ldotp M_{0}
where
M_{0}={\mathrm{Sp}}_{2n}(2)
or
{\mathrm{SO}}^{\pm}_{2n}(2)
acting naturally on the normal subgroup
2^{2n}
of
M/Z(M)
. In case (b)(i), (ii), (iii), the element
Z(M)g
centralises a subgroup of
2^{2n}
of order
2^{2},1,1
respectively, and the centraliser of
Rg
in
M/R\leqslant{\mathrm{Sp}}_{2n}(2)
has order
r_{1}|S_{3}|,r_{1},r_{1}
, respectively. Thus
|C|
is
24r_{1}|Z(M)|,r_{1}|Z(M)|,r_{1}|Z(M)|
respectively.

We note that Proposition
10.2
(a) and the validity of
{\mathcal{M}}_{6}(\mathbf{X)}
follow immediately from parts (a)–(c).
∎

Next we derive upper bounds
|M|
for the groups
M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{6}(\mathbf{X)}
. The proof is a simple modification of the argument of
[
32
, Lemma 4.1]
.

Lemma 10.5
.

For
{\mathbf{X}}
and
M
as in Table
12
, with
d=2^{n}\geqslant 16
and
q
an odd prime,

|M|<\begin{cases}\frac{45}{64}\cdot q^{\frac{9}{5}d}&\textrm{for all }{\mathbf{X}}\textrm{ and }n\geqslant 4,q\geqslant 3,\\
\frac{45}{64}\cdot q^{\frac{16}{25}d}&\textrm{for all }{\mathbf{X}}\textrm{ and }n\geqslant 5,q\geqslant 11,\textrm{ or }n\geqslant 7,q\geqslant 5.\\
\end{cases}

Moreover, if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
or
\mathbf{O^{+}}
and the parameters are as in Lemma
10.4
(b)(iii)
, so either
n=5
with
q\geqslant 11
, or
n\geqslant 7
with
q\geqslant 5
, we have

|M|<\begin{cases}5.81\cdot q^{\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{3}{125}\right)d}&\textrm{for }n=5\textrm{ and }11\leqslant q\leqslant 17,\\
5.81\cdot q^{\frac{2}{5}d}&\textrm{for }n\geqslant 7,q\geqslant 5,\textrm{ or }n=5,q\geqslant 19.\\
\end{cases}

Proof.

Note first that
n\geqslant 4
as
d=2^{n}\geqslant 16
. Let
z=|Z(M)|
.
It follows from Table
12
that
|M|<z\cdot 2^{2n}\cdot|{\mathrm{Sp}}_{2n}(2)|
. Since
|{\mathrm{Sp}}_{2n}(2)|<\frac{45}{64}\cdot 2^{2n^{2}+n}
(see
[
32
, Table 3]
where, as
n\geqslant 4
, we use the improved bound
\Omega(1,n,2^{2})<(1-\frac{1}{2^{2}})(1-\frac{1}{2^{4}})=\frac{45}{64}
),
and since in all cases
z<q
, it follows, that

|M|<z\cdot\frac{45}{64}\cdot 2^{2n^{2}+3n}<\frac{45}{64}\cdot q^{(2n^{2}+3n)/\log_{2}(q)+1}.

By induction (on
n
) it is
easy to show that, for
n\geqslant 4

and
q\geqslant 3
,

(2n^{2}+3n)/\log_{2}(q)+1\leqslant(2n^{2}+3n)/\log_{2}(3)+1<\frac{9}{5}2^{n}=\frac{9}{5}d,

while for either
n\geqslant 5
with
q\geqslant 11
, or
n\geqslant 7
with
q\geqslant 5
, we have

(2n^{2}+3n)/\log_{2}(q)+1<\frac{16}{25}2^{n}=\frac{16}{25}d.

This proves the first bounds in the lemma.

Now we assume that
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
or
{\mathbf{O}}^{+}
and either
n=5
with
q\geqslant 11
, or
n\geqslant 7
with
q\geqslant 5
. In this case
z=2
and
it follows from Table
12
that
|M|<2^{2n+1}\cdot|{\mathrm{SO}}^{-}_{2n}(2)|
.
Since

|{\mathrm{SO}}^{-}_{2n}(2)|<\frac{45}{64}\cdot(1+2^{-n})\cdot 2^{2n^{2}-n+1}\leqslant\frac{45}{32}\cdot\left(1+\frac{1}{32}\right)\cdot 2^{2n^{2}-n}<1.451\cdot 2^{2n^{2}-n}

(see
[
32
, Table 3]
, again using the improved bound
\Omega(1,n-1,2^{2})<(1-\frac{1}{2^{2}})(1-\frac{1}{2^{4}})=\frac{45}{64}
)
it follows that

|M|<1.451\cdot 2^{2n^{2}+n+1}=1.451\cdot 4\cdot 2^{2n^{2}+n-1}<5.81\cdot q^{(2n^{2}+n-1)/\log_{2}(q)}.

For
q\geqslant 11
and
n=5
we have

(2n^{2}+n-1)/\log_{2}(q)\leqslant(2n^{2}+n-1)/\log_{2}(11)<\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{3}{125}\right)2^{n}=\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{3}{125}\right)d,

giving the bound asserted for
n=5
with
11\leqslant q\leqslant 17
. Further, if either
n\geqslant 7
with
q\geqslant 5
, or
n=5
with
q\geqslant 19
, we find

(2n^{2}+n-1)/\log_{2}(q)<\frac{2}{5}2^{n}=\frac{2}{5}d

which yields the asserted bounds.
∎

Finally we prove Proposition
10.2
.

Proof of Proposition
10.2
.
Part (a) of Proposition
10.2
, and the form of
{\mathcal{M}}_{6}({\mathbf{X}})
follow from Lemma
10.4
.

Suppose then that Hypothesis
10.3
holds, in particular that
{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})>0
. The assertions in Proposition
10.2
(b) about
d,q
, the
e_{i}
and
r_{i}
are proved in Lemma
10.4
, and it remains to estimate
{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
for the cases in Lemma
10.4
(b)(i), (b)(ii) and (b)(iii). In the first two cases

e_{1}=e_{2}=d/2=2^{n-1}
, while in the third case
e_{1}=d-2
and
e_{2}=2
. Let
g\in g_{1}^{G}
and let
M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{6}({\mathbf{X}})
such that
g\in M
. By Lemma
10.4
(c),
{\mathcal{M}}_{6}({\mathbf{X}})
is a single
G
-conjugacy class, the number of
G
-conjugates
L\in{\mathcal{M}}_{6}({\mathbf{X}})
such that
g\in L
is
|C_{G}(g_{1})|\cdot e_{1}/|N_{M}(\langle g\rangle)|
, and
|N_{M}(\langle g\rangle)|=cr_{1}z
with
z=|Z(M)|
, and
c=48(n-1),2n,2n
in case (b)(i), (b)(ii), or (b)(iii), respectively.
Thus, by (
11
),

(34)

{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant\frac{|C_{G}(g_{1})|\cdot e_{1}}{cr_{1}z}\cdot\frac{|\mathcal{S}_{6}(g,M)|}{|N(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}=\frac{|C_{G}(g_{1})|}{|N(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}\cdot\frac{e_{1}\cdot|\mathcal{S}_{6}(g,M)|}{cr_{1}z},

with
|N(d,q^{u},e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|
as in (
12
), and
\mathcal{S}_{6}(g,M)
as in (
9
), namely the set of all
g^{\prime}\in g_{2}^{G}\cap M
such that
(g,g^{\prime})
is a stingray duo and
\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle
is not contained in a subgroup of
{\mathcal{M}}_{j}({\mathbf{X}})
for any
j\leqslant 5
.
The first factor in the last expression in (
34
) is determined in Propositions
4.2
and
4.4
.
As we do not have an easy description of the actions of
M
on
V
, we over-estimate
|\mathcal{S}_{6}(g,M)|
by
|g_{2}^{G}\cap M|
for the first two cases and by
|M|
in the third case.

Cases
(b)(i)
and
(b)(ii)
of Lemma
10.4
:
Here
e_{2}=e_{1}
, and hence, by Lemma
10.4
(c),
|g_{2}^{G}\cap M|
is equal to
|g_{1}^{G}\cap M|
, which is equal to
e_{1}\cdot|M|/|N_{M}(\langle g\rangle)|=e_{1}\cdot|M|/cr_{1}z
. Thus, since
e_{1}<r_{1}
,

(35)

{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant\frac{|C_{G}(g_{1})|}{|N(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}\cdot\frac{e_{1}^{2}\cdot|M|}{(cr_{1}z)^{2}}<\frac{|C_{G}(g_{1})|}{|N(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}\cdot\frac{|M|}{4n^{2}z^{2}}

where we have used the common upper bound
1/c\leqslant 1/2n
for the cases (b)(i) and (b)(ii).
Note that, by Lemma
10.5
,
|M|<\frac{45}{64}q^{2d}
.
We now apply Propositions
4.2
and
4.4

separately for each type.
Note that
(q^{e_{i}}-1)/(q-1)<2q^{e_{i}-1}.

If
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
, noting that
(q^{e_{i}}-1)/(q-1)<2q^{e_{i}}
, we have

{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})\leqslant\frac{(q^{e_{1}}-1)(q^{e_{2}}-1)}{q^{2e_{1}e_{2}}}\cdot\frac{\frac{45}{64}q^{\frac{9}{5}d}}{4n^{2}(q-1)^{2}}<\frac{45}{64\,n^{2}}\cdot\frac{q^{e_{1}+e_{2}-2}\cdot q^{\frac{9}{5}d}}{q^{2e_{1}e_{2}}},

noting that
(q^{e_{i}}-1)/(q-1)<2q^{e_{i}-1}.

Since
e_{1}=e_{2}=d/2
, this upper bound is
\frac{45}{64n^{2}}\cdot q^{-b}
where
b=d^{2}/2-\frac{14}{5}d+2
, and for all
d=2^{n}\geqslant 16
we have
b>d^{2}/4
, and hence
{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})\leqslant\frac{45}{1024}q^{-d^{2}/4}
as in Proposition
10.2
(b)(i). In the other lines of Table
12
,
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
or
\mathbf{O^{+}}
, and we have

q
odd, and the bounds
1/2\leqslant 1-3/(2q)\leqslant\mathbf{k}(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})<1
. Note that
(q^{e_{1}/2}+1)(q^{e_{2}/2}+1)<2q^{(e_{1}+e_{2})/2}
. Thus using Propositions
4.4
, (
35
) yields

\displaystyle{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant

\displaystyle\frac{(q^{e_{1}/2}+1)(q^{e_{2}/2}+1)}{\mathbf{k}(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})\cdot|\mathcal{U}(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}\cdot\frac{|M|}{16n^{2}}<\frac{q^{(e_{1}+e_{2})/2}\cdot|M|}{{\mathbf{a}}(q,{\mathbf{X}})\cdot q^{e_{1}e_{2}}\cdot 4n^{2}}.

For both types, we have
4{\mathbf{a}}(q,{\mathbf{X}})>1
, by Table
7
(since
q
is odd), so using
e_{1}=e_{2}=d/2
, we have

{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})<\frac{1}{n^{2}}\cdot q^{-d^{2}/4+d/2}\cdot|M|
. By
Lemma
10.5
,
|M|<\frac{45}{64}q^{\frac{9}{5}d}
and
thus

{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})<\frac{45}{1024}\cdot q^{-d^{2}/4+23d/10}.

Cases
(b)(iii)
of Lemma
10.4
:
Here
e_{1}=d-2
and
e_{2}=2
, the dimension
d=2^{n}\geqslant 32
with
n
and
q
odd primes,
and either
n\geqslant 7
with
q\geqslant 5
, or
n=5
with
q\geqslant 11
.

We use
|\mathcal{S}_{6}(g,M)|<|M|
, and the upper bound for
|M|

depending on
{\mathbf{X}}
given by Lemma
10.5
. Thus, since
e_{1}<r_{1}
and
c=2n
, (
34
) becomes

{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant\frac{|C_{G}(g_{1})|}{|N(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}\cdot\frac{e_{1}\cdot|M|}{cr_{1}z}<\frac{|C_{G}(g_{1})|}{|N(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}\cdot\frac{|M|/z}{2n}.

Again we apply Propositions
4.2
and
4.4
.
If
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
,
then by Lemma
10.5

|M|<\frac{45}{64}q^{\frac{16}{25}d}
, thus

{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})\leqslant\frac{(q^{d-2}-1)(q^{2}-1)}{q^{4d-8}}\cdot\frac{\frac{45}{64}q^{\frac{16}{25}d}}{2n}<\frac{45}{640}q^{-\frac{59}{25}d+8}.

If
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
or
\mathbf{O^{+}}
then

{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant\frac{(q^{(d-2)/2}+1)(q+1)}{\mathbf{k}(d,q,d-2,{\mathbf{X}})\cdot|\mathcal{U}(d,q,d-2,{\mathbf{X}})|}\cdot\frac{|M|}{2n}.

By Proposition
4.4
,
|\mathcal{U}(d,q,d-2,{\mathbf{X}})|>(47/128)\cdot q^{2d-4}
.
Also
(q^{(d-2)/2}+1)(q+1)<2q^{d/2}
and
1-3/(2q)\leqslant\mathbf{k}(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}}).

We consider first the cases

n\geqslant 7
with
q\geqslant 5
, and
n=5
with
q\geqslant 19,
(recall
n,q
are odd primes).
Here by
Lemma
10.5
,
|M|<5.81\cdot q^{\frac{2}{5}d}

and hence

{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})<\frac{2q^{d/2}\cdot 5.81\cdot q^{\frac{2}{5}d}}{(1-3/(2q))\cdot(47/128)\cdot q^{2d-4}\cdot 2n}<\frac{15.83}{n(1-3/(2q))\cdot q^{2^{n}/10-1}}\cdot q^{-d+3}.

In all of these cases

\frac{15.83}{n(1-3/(2q))\cdot q^{2^{n}/10-1}}\cdot q^{-d+3}<5.3\cdot 10^{-3}\cdot q^{-d+3}.

This leaves the case
n=5
with

q\in\{11,13,17\}
; here
2n=10
and

|M|\leqslant 2^{2n+1}\cdot|{\mathrm{SO}}^{-}_{2n}(2)|.

As in this case
(q^{(d-2)/2}+1)(q+1)<1.1\cdot q^{d/2},
and
|\mathcal{U}(d,q,d-2,{\mathbf{X}})|>(227/500)\cdot q^{2d-4}
by using
q\geqslant 11
in the proof of Proposition
4.4
,

\displaystyle{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})

\displaystyle<\frac{1.1\cdot q^{d/2}\cdot 2^{2n+1}|{\mathrm{SO}}^{-}_{2n}(2)|}{(1-3/(2q))\cdot(227/500)\cdot q^{2d-4}\cdot 10}=\frac{1.1\cdot 50\cdot 2^{2n+1}\cdot|{\mathrm{SO}}^{-}_{2n}(2)|}{227\cdot(1-3/(2q))\cdot q^{\frac{3}{2}d-4}}

\displaystyle<\frac{55\cdot 2048\cdot|{\mathrm{SO}}^{-}_{2n}(2)|}{227\cdot(1-3/(2q))\cdot q^{15}}\cdot q^{-d+3}<6.9\cdot q^{-d+3}.

This completes the proof of Proposition
10.2
.

11.
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{8}:
classical groups

We use the notation and assumptions from Hypothesis
3.1
, and in this section we estimate the proportion
{\rm Prob}_{8}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
of stingray duos
(g,g^{\prime})\in g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}
for which
\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle
lies in a classical subgroup
M
of
G
lying in
the Aschbacher category
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{8}
, that is,
M
preserves a non-degenerate form on
V
other than the form defining
G
of type
{\mathbf{X}}
. For a non-zero contribution to
{\rm Prob}_{8}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
, we do not have
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
with
q
even, by Remark
3.2
(b), and taking this into account we record in Table
13
the possibilities for
M
(see
[
21
, Table 4.8.A]
).
In particular, such subgroups
M
exist only if the type
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
, and we take
the family
{\mathcal{M}}_{8}({\mathbf{X}})
as in the main result of this section, Proposition
11.1
.

Proposition 11.1
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.1
holds, and
d>8
. Then
{\rm Prob}_{8}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=0
if
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{L}}
or if
d
is odd, and otherwise

{\rm Prob}_{8}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})\leqslant\begin{cases}1.46\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}+d/2}&\mbox{if $q=2$}\\
3.18\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}+d/2}&\mbox{if $q\geqslant 3$.}\end{cases}

Moreover we take
{\mathcal{M}}_{8}({\mathbf{X}})=\emptyset
if
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{L}}
or if
d
is odd, and for
d
even we take
{\mathcal{M}}_{8}({\mathbf{L}})
to consist the classical groups
M
of type
{\mathbf{Y}}
as in Table
13
.

Table 13.
Maximal subgroups
M

in
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{8}
containing
e_{i}
-stingray

elements in Lemma
11.3

{\mathbf{X}}

M

Conditions

{\mathbf{Y}}

{\mathbf{L}}

{\mathrm{GSp}}_{d}(q)

d,e_{i}
even

{\mathbf{Sp}}

{\mathbf{L}}

{\mathrm{GU}}_{d}(q_{0})

e_{i}
odd,
q=q_{0}^{2}

{\mathbf{U}}

{\mathbf{L}}

{\mathrm{GO}}^{\varepsilon}_{d}(q)

d,e_{i}
even,
q
odd

\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}

To prove Proposition
11.1
, we work with the following assumptions.

Hypothesis 11.2

(for
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{8}
)
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.1
holds with
d>8
, and also that
{\rm Prob}_{8}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\neq 0
, so
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
(by our discussion above). Let
(g,g^{\prime})
be a stingray duo in
g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}
such that
H\coloneq\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle\leqslant M
for
some
M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{8}({\mathbf{L}})
, while
H\not\leqslant L
for any
L\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{7}{\mathcal{M}}_{i}({\mathbf{L}})
.

We begin with a lemma which determines the
G
-conjugates of a stingray element lying in a subgroup

M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{8}({\mathbf{L}})
.

Lemma 11.3
.

Assume that Hypothesis
11.2
holds, and let
i\in\{1,2\}
,
e=e_{i},r=r_{i}
and
h\in g_{i}^{G}\cap M
.
Then the ‘Conditions’ in Table
13
hold,
M
is self-normalising and
g_{i}^{G}\cap M=h^{M}.

Proof.

The subgroup
M
is
self-normalising as
M
is maximal in
G
. Since

h^{M}\subseteq g_{i}^{G}\cap M
, it remains in each case
to show that
g_{i}^{G}\cap M\subseteq h^{M}.

Recall that
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
,
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q)
, and that, by Table
13
,

M
is isomorphic to
{\mathrm{GSp}}_{d}(q)
,
{\mathrm{GU}}_{d}(q)
, or
{\mathrm{GO}}^{\varepsilon}_{d}(q)
. The ‘Conditions’ in Table
13
follow from Table
5
.
Two elements in
g_{i}^{G}\cap M
are conjugate in
M
if and only if they are conjugate in
G,
by

[
7
, Theorem 6.1.1]
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
11.1
.
Let
g\in g_{1}^{G}
. We need to consider each
M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{8}({\mathbf{L}})
containing
g
. Now each such
M
is a classical group of type
{\mathbf{Y}}
satisfying the
‘Conditions’ in some (unique) line of Table
13
.
If we denote by
{\mathcal{M}}_{8}(G,{\mathbf{Y}})
the set of maximal subgroups of
G
of type
{\mathbf{Y}}
, then we can write (
11
) as

(36)

\displaystyle{\rm Prob}_{8}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{{\mathbf{Y}}\in\{{\mathbf{Sp}},{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}\}}\left(\sum_{M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{8}(G,{\mathbf{Y}}),g\in M}\frac{\left|\mathcal{S}_{8}(g,M)\right|}{\left|N(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{L}})\right|}\right),

with
\mathcal{S}_{8}(g,M)
as in (
9
). By Lemma
11.3
,

g_{2}^{G}\cap M
is a single
M
-conjugacy class, and hence
\mathcal{S}_{8}(g,M)
is a subset of

\{g^{\prime}\in g_{2}^{M}\mid\mbox{$(g,g^{\prime})$ is a stingray duo in }M\}.

Since
M\cong{\mathrm{G{\bf Y}}}_{d}(q)
, it follows from (
12
) that, if
M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{8}(G,{\mathbf{Y}})
contains
g
, then
|\mathcal{S}_{8}(g,M)|
is at most
|N(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{Y}})|
, (noting that, if
{\mathbf{Y}}={\mathbf{U}}
, then
M={\mathrm{GU}}_{d}(q_{0})
and the second entry
q=q_{0}^{2}
). Moreover, since by Lemma
11.3
,
N_{G}(M)=M
and
g_{1}^{G}\cap M
is a single
M
-conjugacy class, it follows from Lemma
2.7
that the number of
M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{8}(G,{\mathbf{Y}})
containing
g
is
|C_{G}(g_{1})|/|C_{M}(g)|
; and both
C_{G}(g_{1})
and
C_{M}(g)
are given by Lemma
2.5
(c) (for types
{\mathbf{L}}
and
{\mathbf{Y}}
respectively). Thus by (
36
),

\displaystyle{\rm Prob}_{8}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{{\mathbf{Y}}\in\{{\mathbf{Sp}},{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}\}}\frac{|C_{G}(g_{1})|}{|C_{M}(g)|}\cdot\frac{|N(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{Y}})|}{|N(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{L}})|}

\displaystyle=\frac{|C_{G}(g_{1})|}{|N(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{L}})|}\cdot\sum_{{\mathbf{Y}}\in\{{\mathbf{Sp}},{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}\}}\frac{|N(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{Y}})|}{|C_{M}(g)|}.

The first factor (and also each of the summands) is determined in Proposition
4.2
.
In particular,

(37)

\frac{|C_{G}(g_{1})|}{|N(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{L}})|}=\frac{(q^{e_{1}}-1)(q^{e_{2}}-1)}{q^{2e_{1}e_{2}}}<q^{-2e_{1}e_{2}+d}.

For
{\mathbf{Y}}={\mathbf{U}}
, we have zero contribution unless
q=q_{0}^{2}
is a square and the
e_{i}
are both odd, and in this case it follows from Proposition
4.4
that

\frac{|N(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{U}})|}{|C_{M}(g)|}=\frac{\mathbf{k}(d,q_{0}^{2},e_{1},{\mathbf{U}})|{\mathcal{U}}(d,q_{0}^{2},e_{1},{\mathbf{U}})|}{(q_{0}^{e_{1}}+1)(q_{0}^{e_{2}}+1)}<\frac{|{\mathcal{U}}(d,q_{0}^{2},e_{1},{\mathbf{U}})|}{(q_{0}^{e_{1}}+1)(q_{0}^{e_{2}}+1)}<\frac{q_{0}^{2e_{1}e_{2}}}{q_{0}^{d}}=q^{e_{1}e_{2}-d/2}

and the contribution to
{\rm Prob}_{8}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})
is less than
q^{-2e_{1}e_{2}+d}\cdot q^{e_{1}e_{2}-d/2}=q^{-e_{1}e_{2}+d/2}
.

For
{\mathbf{Y}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
or
\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}
, we have zero contribution unless the
e_{i}
are both even, and in this case it follows from Proposition
4.4
, (taking
\varepsilon=1
if
{\mathbf{Y}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
) that

\frac{|N(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{Y}})|}{|C_{M}(g)|}<\frac{|{\mathcal{U}}(d,q,e_{1},{\mathbf{Y}})|}{(q^{e_{1}/2}+1)(q^{e_{2}/2}+\varepsilon)}<\frac{{\mathbf{b}}(q,{\mathbf{Y}})\cdot q^{e_{1}e_{2}}}{q^{e_{1}/2}(q^{e_{2}/2}+\varepsilon)}

with
{\mathbf{b}}(q,{\mathbf{Y}})
as in Table
7
. If
{\mathbf{Y}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
then
{\mathbf{b}}(q,{\mathbf{Y}})\leqslant 16/11
if
q=2
and
{\mathbf{b}}(q,{\mathbf{Y}})\leqslant 8/7
if
q\geqslant 3
. Hence the contribution to
{\rm Prob}_{8}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})
is less than
q^{-2e_{1}e_{2}+d}\cdot(16/11)\cdot q^{e_{1}e_{2}-d/2}=(16/11)\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}+d/2}
if
q=2
, or
(8/7)\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}+d/2}
if
q\geqslant 3
. If
{\mathbf{Y}}=\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}
then
q
is odd (see Table
13
) so
{\mathbf{b}}(q,{\mathbf{Y}})\leqslant 54/71
and to estimate the contribution to
{\rm Prob}_{8}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})
we use (
37
) to see that it is less than

\frac{(q^{e_{1}}-1)(q^{e_{2}}-1)}{q^{2e_{1}e_{2}}}\cdot\frac{(54/71)\cdot q^{e_{1}e_{2}}}{q^{e_{1}/2}(q^{e_{2}/2}-1)}<q^{-e_{1}e_{2}+d/2}\cdot(54/71)\cdot(1+q^{-e_{2}/2}).

Since
q\geqslant 3
and
e_{2}\geqslant 2
, the final factor
1+q^{-e_{2}/2}\leqslant 4/3
, and the contribution is therefore less than
1.015\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}+d/2}
.

Finally we put these estimates together to obtain an upper bound for
{\rm Prob}_{8}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})
of the form
k\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}+d/2}
. If at least one of the
e_{i}
is odd, then the only non-zero contribution is from
{\mathbf{Y}}={\mathbf{U}}
so we have
k=1
, and Proposition
11.1
follows for all
q
in this case. Suppose then that both of the
e_{i}
are even. Then we can only have
{\mathbf{Y}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
or
\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}
. If
q=2
then the only non-zero contribution is from
{\mathbf{Y}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
so we have
k=16/11<1.46
. On the other hand if
q\geqslant 3
then we may have contributions from
{\mathbf{Y}}={\mathbf{Sp}},{\mathbf{O}}^{+}
, and
{\mathbf{O}}^{-}
, and we obtain
k=(8/7)+1.015+1.015<3.18
.
This completes the proof of Proposition
11.1
.

12.
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}:
almost simple groups

We use the notation and assumptions from Hypothesis
3.1
, and in this section we estimate the proportion
{\rm Prob}_{9}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
of stingray duos
(g,g^{\prime})\in g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}
for which
\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle\leqslant M
where
M
lies in
the Aschbacher category
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}
. In most cases these subgroups
M
are maximal subgroups of
G
lying in

\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}
which can conceivably contain such stingray duos, while in the exceptional case where
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
and
q
is even, these are
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}
-subgroups of orthogonal subgroups of
G
, as described in Remark
3.2
(b). Now
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}
-subgroups
M
are projectively almost simple, that is,
M\cap Z(G)<N\trianglelefteqslant M
such that
S\coloneq N/(M\cap Z(G))
is a nonabelian simple group and
M/(M\cap Z(G))\leqslant\mathrm{Aut}(S)
.
Moreover,
N
is absolutely irreducible on
V
. We make the usual assumption
d>8
. The main result of the section is Proposition
12.1
; in particular, the bounds obtained there improve on those in
[
33
, Lemma 13.1]
for the
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}
case with
e_{1}=e_{2}=d/2
, see also
[
15
, Theorem 1.4]
.

Proposition 12.1
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.1
holds with
d>8
.

(a)

If
q^{u}\neq p
, or if
{\mathbf{X}}\in\{{\mathbf{L}},{\mathbf{U}}\}
, or if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
with
q
odd, then
\textup{Prob}_{9}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=0
.

(b)

If
q^{u}=p
and
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon=\pm
, or
({{\mathbf{X}}},q)=({\mathbf{Sp}},2)
, then

\textup{Prob}_{9}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant\left\{\begin{array}[]{rc}8.24\cdot p^{-e_{1}e_{2}+d/2}&\mbox{ if ${\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}$ and $q^{u}=p\geqslant 3$}\\
28.1\cdot 2^{-e_{1}e_{2}+d/2}&\mbox{ if ${\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}$ and $q^{u}=p=2$}\\
6.38\cdot 2^{-e_{1}e_{2}+d/2}&\mbox{ if ${\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}$ and $q^{u}=p=2$.}\end{array}\right.

To prove Proposition
12.1
, we work with the following assumptions.

Hypothesis 12.2

(for
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}
)
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.1
holds, with
d>8
, and also that
{\rm Prob}_{9}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\neq 0
. Let
(g,g^{\prime})
be a stingray duo in
g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}
such that
H\coloneq\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle\leqslant M
, for some
M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{9}({\mathbf{X}})
, while
H\not\leqslant L
for any
L\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{8}{\mathcal{M}}_{i}({\mathbf{X}})
. Thus
M\cap Z(G)<N\trianglelefteqslant M
with
S\coloneq N/(M\cap Z(G))
a nonabelian simple group,
M/(M\cap Z(G))\leqslant\mathrm{Aut}(S)
, and
N
absolutely irreducible on
V
.

The situation where
S=A_{n}
and
V
is the fully deleted permutation module for the natural action of
S
is exceptional, and is considered separately in Subsection
12.1
. It arises only for
{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}
, and bounds on the contribution
\textup{Prob}_{9}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};\textup{f.d. perm mod})
from groups of this type to
\textup{Prob}_{9}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
are obtained in Proposition
12.3
. For this reason we write

\textup{Prob}_{9}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=\textup{Prob}_{9}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};\textup{f.d. perm mod})+\textup{Prob}_{9}^{*}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})

where
\textup{Prob}_{9}^{*}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
is the contribution from all other kinds of subgroups in
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}
.
We are able to show in Subsection
12.2
that
\textup{Prob}_{9}^{*}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=0
when
d>8
. The proof in the case
e_{1}>d/2
uses the classification of the
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}
-subgroups containing an
e_{1}
-ppd stingray element given in
[
17
, Main Theorem]
.
On the other hand, if
e_{1}=e_{2}=d/2
, then we use a new result obtained in
[
15
, Theorem 1.2]
which shows, for
d>8
, that no
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}
-subgroups contain a
d/2
-ppd stingray element apart from the groups
A_{n},S_{n}
acting on fully deleted permutation modules.

12.1.
Fully deleted permutation modules

We estimate
\textup{Prob}_{9}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};\textup{f.d. perm mod})
in Proposition
12.3
.

Proposition 12.3
.

Assume that Hypothesis
12.2
holds. Then:

(a)

if
q\neq p
, or if
{\mathbf{X}}\in\{{\mathbf{L}},{\mathbf{U}}\}
, or if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
with
q
odd, then

\textup{Prob}_{9}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};\textup{f.d. perm mod})=0;

(b)

if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon=\pm
with
q=p
, or if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
with
q=2
, then

\textup{Prob}_{9}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};\textup{f.d. perm mod})\leqslant\left\{\begin{array}[]{rc}8.24\cdot p^{-e_{1}e_{2}+d/2}&\mbox{ if ${\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}$ and $q=p\geqslant 3$}\\
28.1\cdot 2^{-e_{1}e_{2}+d/2}&\mbox{ if ${\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{O^{\varepsilon}}$ and $q=p=2$}\\
6.38\cdot 2^{-e_{1}e_{2}+d/2}&\mbox{ if ${\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}$ and $q=p=2$.}\end{array}\right.

Recall that
q=p^{a}
where
p
is prime and
a\geqslant 1
. Let
S_{n}
act on
Y=({\mathbb{F}}_{q^{u}})^{n}
by permuting the basis vectors
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
according to the natural representation of
S_{n}
. The
S_{n}
-module
Y
has submodules of dimensions 1 and
n-1
, namely

W\coloneq\langle y_{i}-y_{i+1}\mid 1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1\rangle=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}y_{i}\mid\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=0\right\}\quad\mbox{and}\quad D\coloneq\langle y_{1}+\cdots+y_{n}\rangle.

We call
V=(W+D)/D\cong W/(W\cap D)
the
fully deleted permutation module
. Note that
W\cap D=D
if
p\mid n
and
W\cap D=0
if
p\nmid n
. Let
\delta=1
if
p\nmid n
and
\delta=2
if
p\mid n
(see
[
21
, (5.3.6)]
). Then:

(PM1)

The following are equivalent:
p\nmid n
,
\delta=1
,
Y=W\oplus D
,
V\cong W\cong Y/D
;

(PM2)

The following are equivalent:
p\mid n
,
\delta=2
,
D\leqslant W
,
V=W/D
.

Thus
\dim(V)=n-\delta
. The
S_{n}
-module
V
is absolutely irreducible, and remains absolutely irreducible when restricted to
A_{n}
. It can be written over the prime field
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{u}}
, and since
d=n-\delta>8
, the normaliser
L\coloneq N_{{\mathrm{GL}}(V)}(A_{n})=S_{n}\times Z
, where
Z
is the subgroup of scalar matrices of
{\mathrm{GL}}(V)
, see
[
21
, p. 187]
. We collect together in the next result these and other observations concerning
N_{G}(A_{n})=G\cap L
when this group is not contained in any subgroup of
\bigcup_{i=1}^{8}\mathcal{M}_{i}({\mathbf{X}})
.

Lemma 12.4
.

Assume that Hypothesis
12.2
holds, and let
V,n,\delta,Z
,
L\coloneq N_{{\mathrm{GL}}(V)}(A_{n})
be as above. Then:

(a)

d=\dim(V)=n-\delta
and
\mathrm{(PM1)},\mathrm{(PM2)}
hold, and
L\coloneq N_{{\mathrm{GL}}(V)}(A_{n})=S_{n}\times Z
;

(b)

if
N_{G}(A_{n})=G\cap L
is not contained in any subgroup of
\bigcup_{i=1}^{8}\mathcal{M}_{i}({\mathbf{X}})
, then
q^{u}=p
, and either
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
with
p=2
, or
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
depending on
p
;

(c)

with
G,{\mathbf{X}}
as in part
(b)
, the set of all subgroups of
\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(p)
isomorphic to
A_{d+1}
either forms a single
\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(p)
-conjugacy class, or is the union of two equal sized
\textup{G{\bf X}}_{d}(p)
-conjugacy classes, and in the latter case
q=p
is odd.

Proof.

Part (a) follows from the discussion above. Assume now that
N_{G}(A_{n})=G\cap L
is not contained in any subgroup of
\bigcup_{i=1}^{8}\mathcal{M}_{i}({\mathbf{X}})
. Then in particular, since the representation of
S_{n}
can be written over the prime field
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{u}}
, and since
N_{G}(A_{n})=G\cap L
is not contained in a subgroup in
\mathcal{M}_{5}({\mathbf{X}})
, it follows that
q^{u}=p
. Also (see for example
[
21
, pp. 186–187]
), either
A_{n}
preserves a non-degenerate quadratic form on
V
defined over
\mathbf{F}_{p}
, or
n\equiv 2\pmod{4}
,
p=2
, and
A_{n}
preserves a non-degenerate symplectic form on
V
(but no such quadratic form) defined over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
. Then since
N_{G}(A_{n})=G\cap L
is not contained in a subgroup in
\mathcal{M}_{8}({\mathbf{X}})
, it follows that either
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon=\pm
, or
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
with
p=2
, and part (b) is proved.

It remains to prove part (c). By part (a),
n=d+\delta\geqslant d+1
, and so
n\geqslant 10
since
d>8
. Note that if
n=d+2
then all subgroups
A_{d+1}
of
A_{n}
are conjugate in
A_{n}
. Then since
n\geqslant 10
,
A_{n}
lies in a unique
{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(p)
-conjugacy class by
[
21
, Proposition 5.3.5]
, and hence each
A_{d+1}\leqslant A_{n}
also lies in a unique
{\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(p)
-conjugacy class of subgroups isomorphic to
A_{d+1}
.
Now
A_{d+1}\leqslant\Omega_{d}^{\varepsilon}(p)
by
[
21
, p. 187]
, and
A_{d+1}
lies in a unique
conjugacy class of the conformal orthogonal group
\textup{CO}_{d}^{\varepsilon}(p)
by
[
3
, Lemma 1.8.10(ii)]
. Since
|\textup{CO}_{d}^{\varepsilon}(p):{\mathrm{GO}}_{d}^{\varepsilon}(p)|=p-1
, there is a unique conjugacy class of subgroups
A_{d+1}
in
{\mathrm{GO}}_{d}^{\varepsilon}(p)
when
p=2
. Also, since
{\mathrm{GO}}_{d}^{\varepsilon}(2)={\mathrm{SO}}_{d}^{\varepsilon}(2)<{\mathrm{Sp}}_{d}(2)
and
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{d}(2)
has a unique conjugacy class of subgroups
{\mathrm{SO}}_{d}^{\varepsilon}(2)
, it follows that
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{d}(2)
has a unique conjugacy class of subgroups
A_{d+1}
.

Finally suppose that
p
is odd, and let
H,H^{x}
be subgroups of
{\mathrm{GO}}_{d}^{\varepsilon}(p)
isomorphic to
A_{d+1}
where
x\in\textup{CO}_{d}^{\varepsilon}(p)
. We prove that
H,H^{x^{2}}
are conjugate in
{\mathrm{GO}}_{d}^{\varepsilon}(p)
. If
B
is the Gram matrix of the symmetric form preserved by
{\mathrm{GO}}_{d}^{\varepsilon}(p)
, then
x^{T}Bx=\lambda B
for some
\lambda\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{\times}
. Hence
(x^{2})^{T}Bx^{2}=\lambda^{2}B
and
x^{\prime}\coloneq\lambda^{-1}x^{2}
satisfies
(x^{\prime})^{T}Bx^{\prime}=B
, so
x^{\prime}\in{\mathrm{GO}}_{d}^{\varepsilon}(p)
. Since
H^{x^{\prime}}=H^{x^{2}}
, and
\textup{CO}_{d}^{\varepsilon}(p)/{\mathrm{GO}}_{d}^{\varepsilon}(p)\cong C_{p-1}
, it follows that there are at most two conjugacy classes of subgroups of
{\mathrm{GO}}_{d}^{\varepsilon}(p)
isomorphic to
A_{d+1}
, and if there are two classes then they are interchanged by
\textup{CO}_{d}^{\varepsilon}(p)
and so have equal size.
∎

Next we restrict the cycle types of stingray elements in these subgroups
S_{n}\times Z
.

Lemma 12.5
.

Using the assumptions of Proposition
12.3
, suppose also that
V
is the fully deleted permutation module for
S_{n}
as defined above, that
M\coloneq N_{G}(A_{n})
is an
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}
-subgroup of
\textup{G{\bf X}}(V)
such that
M\not\leqslant L
for any
L\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{8}\mathcal{M}_{i}({\mathbf{X}})
, and that
(g,g^{\prime})
is a stingray duo in
g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}
with
H\coloneq\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle\leqslant M
and
H
irreducible on
V
. Then the following all hold.

(a)

q^{u}=p
,
d=\dim(V)=n-\delta
, and
{\mathbf{X}}\in\{{\mathbf{O}},{\mathbf{Sp}}\}
, as in Lemma
12.4
;

(b)

If
p=2
, then
H\leqslant A_{n^{\prime}}<T
, where either
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}
and
T=\Omega_{d}^{\varepsilon}(2)\unlhd G
, or
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
and
T\cong\Omega_{d}^{\varepsilon}(2)
with
T.2
a maximal subgroup of
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{d}(2)
, for some
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
; in either case the inclusions
A_{n^{\prime}}<{\mathrm{GO}}_{d}^{\varepsilon}(2)
are as in the relevant line of Table
14
.

(c)

r_{i}=e_{i}+1
for each
i
, so
d,e_{1},e_{2}
are all even; also
g
is an
r_{1}
-cycle and
g^{\prime}
is an
r_{2}
-cycle.

(d)

Let
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}
denote the subset of
\Omega=\{1,\dots,n\}
of points permuted nontrivially by
g,g^{\prime}
, respectively. Then
|\Delta\cap\Delta^{\prime}|=1
, and
H=A_{d+1}
, fixing
\delta-1
points of
\Omega
.

Table 14.
\varepsilon,d,n^{\prime}
for Lemma
12.5
(b) when
q^{u}=p=2

\varepsilon

d

n^{\prime}

Inclusions of alternating groups

+

d+1

A_{d+1}<{\mathrm{GO}}^{+}_{d}(2)

Proof.

(a) This part comes from Lemma
12.4
.
Since
|g|,|g^{\prime}|
, are odd and do not divide
q^{u}-1=p-1
, it follows that

H\leqslant A_{n}
. Moreover, since
{\mathbf{X}}\in\{{\mathbf{O}},{\mathbf{Sp}}\}
, the parameters
e_{1},e_{2}
are even, by Table
5
, and hence also
d
is even.

(b) We assume that
p=2
and we refer to
[
21
, pp. 186–187]
, or see
[
23
, Section 4]
.
If
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
, then by Lemma
2.8
(and Lemma
2.9
),
H\leqslant T\cong\Omega_{d}^{\varepsilon}(2)
with
T.2={\mathrm{SO}}_{d}^{\varepsilon}(2)
a maximal subgroup of
G
(for some
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
), so
H\leqslant T\cap N_{G}(S_{n})
. Thus, whether
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{O}}
or
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
, we have, by
[
21
, pp. 186–187]
, that either one of the inclusions given in Table
14
holds with
n^{\prime}=n
, or

(38)

{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}},\quad d=n-2\equiv 0\pmod{4},\mbox{and\quad$H\leqslant A_{d+2}<{\mathrm{Sp}}_{d}(2)$.}

However, if (
38
) holds, then
A_{d+2}
preserves no quadratic form on
V
(see comment after
[
21
, (5.3.9) on p. 187]
), and so
A_{d+2}
does not lie in any orthogonal subgroup of
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{d}(2)
. Thus
H\cap A_{d+2}
is a proper subgroup of
A_{d+2}
; in fact
H\cap A_{d+2}=A_{n^{\prime}}
with
n^{\prime}=d+1
and Line 1 or 3 of Table
14
holds. To clarify what is happening in this case, let
A\cong A_{d+1}
be the stabiliser in
A_{d+2}
of the point
d+2
, and let
Y^{\prime}=\langle y_{1}^{\prime},\dots,y_{d+1}^{\prime}\rangle={\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{d+1}
denote
the permutation module for
A
with corresponding fully deleted
A
-permutation module
V^{\prime}=\langle y_{i}^{\prime}-y_{i+1}^{\prime}\mid 1\leqslant i\leqslant d\rangle
, (recall that
p=2
and
d+1
is odd). The map
\varphi:Y^{\prime}\to Y
given by

y_{i}^{\prime}\to y_{i}-y_{d+2}
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant d+1
, is an
A
-module monomorphism, and
\varphi(V^{\prime})\leqslant W
with
\varphi(V^{\prime})\cap D=0
. Thus
W=\varphi(V^{\prime})\oplus D
and
\varphi
induces an
A
-module isomorphism from
V^{\prime}

to
V=W/D
. By
[
21
, (5.3.9) on p. 187]
(or see Tab;e
14
),
A
preserves a quadratic form on
V
, and hence
A<\Omega_{d}^{\varepsilon}(2)<{\mathrm{Sp}}_{d}(2)=G
, with
\varepsilon=+
if
d\equiv 0\pmod{8}
, and
\varepsilon=-
if
d\equiv 4\pmod{8}
. Therefore, if (
38
) holds, then
T\cong\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{d}(2)

for this value of
\varepsilon
, and up to conjugacy
A_{d+2}\cap T=A
. Also
H\leqslant A\leqslant N_{T}(A)
, and by Remark
3.2
,
N_{T}(A)
is an
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}
-subgroup for
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
, and the inclusion on Line 1 or 3 of Table
14
holds. Thus part (b) is proved.

(c) Here we apply results from
[
33
]
. For
i=1,2
, let
h=g
if
i=1
, and let
h=g^{\prime}
if
i=2
; also let
r=r_{i},e=e_{i}
and
e^{\prime}=e_{3-i}=d-e
, so that
h
is an
e
-ppd stingray element of
G
of odd prime order
r
. As noted in Section
2
,
r=\ell e+1
for some positive integer
\ell
. Moreover, as a permutation of
\Omega=\{1,\dots,n\}
under the natural action of
A_{n}
,
h
therefore has
c
cycles of length
r
and
f
fixed points, for some
c,f
such that
n=cr+f
, and hence
h
has exactly
s\coloneq c+f
cycles. Note that
s=c+f=n-c(r-1)=n-c\ell e
. By
[
33
, Lemma 4.3, and Table 4]
if
p
divides
n
(so
d=n-2
), or by
[
33
, Lemma 4.4]
if
p
does not divide
n
(so
d=n-1
), the fixed point subspace
F_{h}
of
h
in
V
has dimension
s-\tau
for some
\tau\in\{0,1,2\}
. Since
\dim(F_{h})=e^{\prime}=d-e
, and since
d=n-\delta
by part (a), it follows that
n-\delta-e=d-e=e^{\prime}=s-\tau=n-c\ell e-\tau
, and hence

(c\ell-1)e=\delta-\tau\leqslant\delta\leqslant 2.

Suppose that
c\ell\geqslant 2
. The above equation then implies that
e\leqslant 2
, and since
e\geqslant 2
, we conclude that
e=2,c\ell=2
,
\delta=2
, and
\tau=0
. Thus
d=n-\delta=n-2
and so
p
divides
n
by (PM2). The more detailed information in
[
33
, Lemma 4.3, and Table 4]
now implies, since
\dim(F_{h})=s-\tau=s
, that
p
is odd and
p
divides each cycle length of
h
. Thus
p=r
, which is a contradiction since
r
divides
p^{e}-1
. Therefore
c\ell=1
so
c=\ell=1
, and part (c) is proved.

(d) Let
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}
be as in the statement. By part (c),
d=e_{1}+e_{2}=r_{1}+r_{2}-2
, so
n=r_{1}+r_{2}-2+\delta
. We claim that, if
|\Delta\cap\Delta^{\prime}|=1
, then
H=A_{d+1}
and fixes
\delta-1
points of
\Omega
.
Assume that
|\Delta\cap\Delta^{\prime}|=1
. Then
\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}
is an
H
-orbit in
\Omega
of size
|\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}|=r_{1}+r_{2}-1=d+1
, and
H
fixes the remaining
|\Omega\setminus(\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime})|=n-(d+1)=\delta-1
points. In particular
|\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}|\leqslant\min\{r_{1}+r_{2}-1,n\}
. Note that
r_{1}=e_{1}+1\geqslant d/2+1>5
(since
d>8
), and hence
|\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}|\geqslant\max\{r_{1},r_{2}+3\}
. The claim therefore follows from Lemma
1.4
, and it remains to prove that
|\Delta\cap\Delta^{\prime}|=1
.

Let
\nu\coloneq|\Delta\cap\Delta^{\prime}|
. Then
n\geqslant|\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}|=r_{1}+r_{2}-\nu
.
If
|\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}|\leqslant n-2
, then
H
fixes at least two distinct points of
\Omega
, say
i
and
j
, and hence
H
fixes
the
1
-subspace
(\langle y_{i}-y_{j}\rangle+D)/D
of
V
, contradicting the assumption that
H
is irreducible on
V
.
Thus
|\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}|\geqslant n-1
, and we have

r_{1}+r_{2}-3+\delta=n-1\leqslant|\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}|=r_{1}+r_{2}-\nu\leqslant n=r_{1}+r_{2}-2+\delta

so
2\leqslant\delta+\nu\leqslant 3
. Suppose first that
\delta=2
. Then
p
divides
n
by (PM2),
n=r_{1}+r_{2}
, and
\nu\leqslant 1
. If
\nu=0
, then
H=\langle g\rangle\times\langle g^{\prime}\rangle
and
H
leaves invariant the
e_{1}
-subspace
(W_{0}+D)/D
, where
W_{0}=\langle y_{i}-y_{j}\mid i,j\in\Delta\rangle
. This is a contradiction, and hence
\nu=1
, and part (d) is proved in this case. Thus we may assume that
\delta=1
. Then
p
does not divide
n
,
n=r_{1}+r_{2}-1
, and
1\leqslant\nu\leqslant 2
. Also
W\cap D=0
and the permutation module
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{n}=W\oplus D
. If
\nu=2
then
\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}
is an
H
-invariant
(n-1)
-subset of
\Omega
, and hence
H
fixes some point, say
j
, of
\Omega
. This means that, in its action on
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{n}
,
H
fixes the vector
w\coloneq\sum_{i\neq j}(y_{i}-y_{j})
of
W
. Now
w=v-ny_{j}
, where
v=\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}
spans the subspace
D
. Note that
ny_{j}
is non-zero since
p
does not divide
n
, and hence
ny_{j}\not\in D
. This implies that
w\neq 0
,
for if
w=0

then we would have
ny_{j}=v\in D
. Thus
H
leaves invariant the
1
-subspace
(\langle w\rangle+D)/D
of
V
, which is a contradiction. Hence
\nu=1
and the proof of part (d), and the lemma, is complete.
∎

We now have all the information needed to prove Proposition
12.3
.

Proof of Proposition
12.3
.
Suppose that
\textup{Prob}_{9}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};\textup{f.d. perm mod})>0
. Then, by Lemma
12.5
(a),

q^{u}=p
, and the type
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon=\pm
, or
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
with
q^{u}=2
.

If
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
with
q=2
, then
G={\mathrm{Sp}}_{d}(2)
and the maximal
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}
-subgroups we are concerned with are of the form
N_{G}(S_{d+2})
. By Lemma
12.4
(c),
G
contains a unique conjugacy class of subgroups
A_{d+1}
, and by Lemma
12.5
(b), each such subgroup is contained in a maximal subgroup
{\mathrm{SO}}_{d}^{\varepsilon}(2)
of
G
, for some
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
(depending on the congruence of
d
modulo
8
). Further, by Lemma
12.5
(d), for each stingray duo
(g,g^{\prime})
in
N_{G}(S_{d+2})
, the subgroup
H=\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle=A_{d+1}
(and so
H
is contained in a subgroup
{\mathrm{SO}}_{d}^{\varepsilon}(2)
). Note that we treat the subgroups
N_{G}(H)=N_{G}(A_{d+1})
as possible
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}
-subgroups (recall Definition
1.2
and Lemma
2.8
), and we note that there is exactly
x(p)=1
(a unique)
G
-conjugacy class of them.

On the other hand if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
then again, by Lemma
12.5
(d), contributions to the probability come from stingray duos
(g,g^{\prime})

such that
\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle=A_{d+1}
and
V
is the fully deleted permutation module for
A_{d+1}
. Moreover, by Lemma
12.4
(c), these subgroups form
x(p)
equal-sized
G
-conjugacy classes, where
x(p)=1
if
p=2
, and
x(p)\leqslant 2
if
p
is odd.

From now on we treat these cases together; and note that the number of
G
-conjugacy classes of these
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}
-subgroups is
x(p)
in both cases.
To estimate the probability, choose a subgroup
M=A_{d+1}
and an element
g\in g_{1}^{G}\cap M
. Note that
N_{G}(M)=S_{d+1}\times Z^{\prime}
where
Z^{\prime}
is the subgroup of scalars in
G
, so
|Z^{\prime}|=\gcd(p-1,2)
. Note also that
N_{G}(M)
is self-normalising in
G
as
S_{n}
is complete. By Lemma
12.5
(c), each element of
g_{1}^{G}\cap M=g_{1}^{G}\cap N_{G}(M)
is an
r_{1}
-cycle in
M=A_{d+1}
and hence
g_{1}^{G}\cap N_{G}(M)=g^{N_{G}(M)}
. Thus we may apply Lemma
2.7
to the
G
-conjugacy classes
N_{G}(M)^{G}
and
g_{1}^{G}
. Therefore, the number of subgroups in
N_{G}(M)^{G}
that contain
g
is equal to
|C_{G}(g_{1})|/|C_{N_{G}(M)}(g)|
, and the number of subgroups
M
of this kind that contain
g
is
x(p)\cdot|C_{G}(g_{1})|/|C_{N_{G}(M)}(g)|
. Thus, by (
11
),

(39)

\displaystyle\textup{Prob}_{9}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};\textup{f.d. perm mod})

\displaystyle\leqslant x(p)\cdot\frac{|C_{G}(g_{1})|}{|C_{N_{G}(M)}(g)|}\cdot\frac{|\mathcal{S}_{9}(g,M)|}{|N(d,p,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}

\displaystyle=x(p)\cdot\frac{|C_{G}(g_{1})|}{|N(d,p,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}\cdot\frac{|\mathcal{S}_{9}(g,M)|}{|C_{N_{G}(M)}(g)|},

with
|N(d,p,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|
as in (
12
), and
\mathcal{S}_{9}(g,M)
as in (
9
), the set of all
g^{\prime}\in g_{2}^{G}\cap M
such that
(g,g^{\prime})
is a stingray duo and
\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle
is not contained in a subgroup of
{\mathcal{M}}_{j}({\mathbf{X}})
for any
j\leqslant 8
.
The first fraction in the last expression in (
39
) is determined by Proposition
4.4
, namely

\frac{|C_{G}(g_{1})|}{|N(d,p,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}\leqslant\frac{(p^{e_{1}/2}+1)(p^{e_{2}/2}+1)}{\mathbf{k}(d,p,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})\cdot|{\mathcal{U}}(d,p,e_{1},{\mathbf{X}})|}\leqslant\frac{(p^{e_{1}/2}+1)(p^{e_{2}/2}+1)}{(1-3/(2p))\cdot p^{e_{1}e_{2}}\cdot{\mathbf{a}}(p,{\mathbf{X}})}

with
{\mathbf{a}}(p,{\mathbf{X}})
as in Table
7
, which we write also in Table
15
.

To estimate the second fraction in (
39
) we note first that

|C_{N_{G}(M)}(g)|=|Z^{\prime}|\,|C_{S_{d+1}}(g)|=\gcd(p-1,2)\cdot r_{1}\cdot(d+1-r_{1})!\geqslant x(p)\cdot r_{1}\cdot e_{2}!.

We estimate
|\mathcal{S}_{9}(g,M)|
as follows. As in Lemma
12.5
(d),
g
has support
\Delta
, an
r_{1}
-subset of
\Omega^{\prime}=\{1,\dots,d+1\}
. Each of the elements
g^{\prime}
has support

\Delta^{\prime}=(\Omega^{\prime}\setminus\Delta)\cup\{j\}
for some
j\in\Delta
. There are
r_{1}
choices for
j
and, given
j
, there are
(r_{2}-1)!=e_{2}!
choices for the
r_{2}
-cycle
g^{\prime}
with support
\Delta^{\prime}
. Thus
|\mathcal{S}_{9}(g,M)|\leqslant r_{1}\cdot e_{2}!
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\textup{Prob}_{9}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};\textup{f.d. perm mod})

\displaystyle\leqslant x(p)\cdot\frac{(p^{e_{1}/2}+1)(p^{e_{2}/2}+1)}{(1-3/(2p))\cdot p^{e_{1}e_{2}}\cdot{\mathbf{a}}(p,{\mathbf{X}})}\cdot\frac{r_{1}\cdot e_{2}!}{x(p)\cdot r_{1}\cdot e_{2}!}

\displaystyle<\frac{y(p)}{(1-3/(2p))\cdot{\mathbf{a}}(p,{\mathbf{X}})}\cdot p^{-e_{1}e_{2}+d/2}

where, noting that
e_{1}+e_{2}=d
and
2\leqslant e_{2}=d-e_{1}\leqslant d/2
,
y(p)
is the maximum of
f(x)=(1+p^{-(d-x)/2})(1+p^{-x/2})
over all
x=e_{2}\in[2,d/2]
and
d\geqslant 10
. Since
f
has a unique maximum of
(1+p^{-(d-2)/2})(1+p^{-1})
in this interval, at
x=2
, we have
y(p)=(1+p^{-(d-2)/2})(1+p^{-1})
which is at most
y(2)\leqslant 51/32<1.6
(with
d=10
), and for
p
odd is at most
y(3)<1.35
The upper bounds in Proposition
12.3
now follow from these estimates, as recorded in Table
15
.

12.2.
Estimation of
\textup{Prob}_{9}^{*}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
.

In this subsection, we prove that the probability
\textup{Prob}_{9}^{*}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=0
. Since
d>8
, this follows immediately from a theorem
[
15
, Theorem 1.2, Table 1]
of Zalesski and the authors for the case
e_{1}=e_{2}=d/2
, and so we assume that
e_{2}<e_{1}
and hence that
e_{2}<d/2<e_{1}
. Thus
M
is an
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}
-group containing an
e_{1}
-ppd element
g
with
e_{1}>d/2
, and hence
M
is restricted by the classification given by
[
17
, Main Theorem]
and must be one of the groups in
[
17
, Example 2.6–2.9]
.
Recall that, for the contribution to
\textup{Prob}_{9}^{*}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
, the simple group
S
involved in
M
is not
A_{n}
acting on its fully deleted permutation module. We prove in Proposition
12.7

that none of the remaining groups in
[
17
, Example 2.6–2.9]
contains a stingray duo
(g,g^{\prime})
, and hence that
\textup{Prob}_{9}^{*}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=0
.
Our method of proof is to apply the classification in
[
17
, Main Theorem]
to deduce first that, in all cases,
e_{1}=d-3
or
d-2
, and hence
e_{2}=2
or
3
. We then apply a representation theoretic result
[
5
, Theorem 2.11]
, which restricts further the possibilities for
M
. We make an important remark about our application of the latter result.

Remark 12.6
.

The result
[
5
, Theorem 2.11]
involves a quantity
\nu
defined on cosets of the scalar subgroup
Z=Z({\mathrm{GL}}_{d}(q^{u}))
, see
[
5
, (7)]
. We use this quantity for the coset
g^{\prime}Z
with
g^{\prime}
our
e_{2}
-ppd stingray element, and for this element
\nu(g^{\prime}Z)
is equal to
e_{2}
.
Since
d\geqslant 9
,
[
5
, Theorem 2.11]
implies that either the simple group
S
involved in
M
belongs to one of two lists of groups, called List
\mathcal{A}
and List
\mathcal{B}
in
[
5
, Tables 1, 2]
, or

e_{2}>\max\{2,\sqrt{d}/2\}
.
Moreover, it can easily be checked that the latter inequality fails if either (i)
e_{2}=2
, or (ii)
e_{2}=3
and
d\geqslant 36
. Note that the simple groups
S
in List
\mathcal{A}
are all alternating groups.

Proposition 12.7
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.1
holds with
d>8
.
Then no maximal
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}
-group contains a stingray duo in
g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}
, apart from those with a normal subgroup
A_{n}
acting on its fully deleted permutation module. Consequently
\textup{Prob}_{9}^{*}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=0
.

Proof.

As mentioned above, if
e_{1}=e_{2}=d/2
, then the result follows from
[
15
, Theorem 1.2, Table 1]
, which shows that no
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}
-subgroup contains a
(d/2)
-stingray element when
d>8
, apart from the case of
S=A_{n}
on its fully deleted permutation module. Thus we assume that
e_{1}>d/2>e_{2}\geqslant 2
. Suppose that
M
is a maximal
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}
-group such that
M
contains a stingray duo
(g,g^{\prime})
from
g_{1}^{G}\times g_{2}^{G}
, and
M
does not have a normal subgroup
A_{n}
acting on its fully deleted permutation module.
As discussed above,
M
must be one of the groups in
[
17
, Example 2.6–2.9]
.

Groups from
[
17
, Example 2.6]
.
Here
S=A_{n}
and
n,d,p,r_{1}
must be as in one of the lines of
[
17
, Tables 2, 3, or 4]
. However, since
M
is not acting on the fully deleted permutation module for
S
, there are no possibilities with
d\geqslant 9
.

Groups from
[
17
, Example 2.7]
.
Here the simple group
S
is a sporadic group, and the derived subgroup
M^{\prime},d,p,e_{1},r_{1}
must be as in one of the lines of
[
17
, Table 5]
. For convenience we list in Table
16
the five triples
d,e_{1},M^{\prime}
from this table which satisfy
d\geqslant 9
and
d/2<e_{1}\leqslant d-2
. Observe that in each case
e_{1}=d-2
and so
e_{2}=2
. Thus by
[
5
, Theorem 2.11]
and our discussion in Remark
12.6
, the
group
S
must appear in List
\mathcal{B}
in
[
5
, Table 2]
. The only sporadic group
S
occurring in
[
5
, Table 2]
and also in Table
16
is the group
S={\rm M}_{12}
. However, in
[
5
, Table 2]
,
\dim(V)=6
, while in Table
16
,
d=\dim(V)=12
. Thus there are no examples from
[
17
, Example 2.7]
.

Table 16.

,

,

from

Groups from
[
17
, Example 2.8]
.
Here
S
is a simple group of Lie type in characteristic
p
. In this case the subgroup
M^{(\infty)}
together with
d
and
e_{1}
must be as in one of the lines of
[
17
, Table 6]
. There are exactly two lines with
d>8
; each has
d=9
and
e_{1}=6
and hence
e_{2}=3
, and the subgroup
M^{(\infty)}
is either
{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}(q_{0}^{2})
or
{\mathrm{PSL}}_{3}(q_{0}^{2})
, where
q=q_{0}^{k}
for some
k\geqslant 1
. We argue that such a group
M
does not contain a stingray duo
(g,g^{\prime})
. By assumption
V
is an absolutely irreducible
\mathbb{F}_{q}M^{(\infty)}
-module realised over no proper subfield of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Applying
[
21
, Proposition 5.4.6(i)]
with
q=p^{f}
and
q_{0}^{2}=p^{e}
we find that either (a)
f=e
and
q=q_{0}^{2}
, or (b)
f=e/2
and
q=q_{0}
. Since
g\in M^{(\infty)}
, it follows that
r_{1}=|g|
divides
q_{0}^{2i}-1
for some
i\leqslant 3
. However,
r_{1}
is a primitive prime divisor of
q^{e_{1}}-1=q^{6}-1
, and so (a) cannot hold (as
r_{1}
would then divide
q^{i}-1
with
i\leqslant 3
), and we conclude that
q=q_{0}
. Then
[
21
, Proposition 5.4.6(i)]
implies further that
V
is a twisted tensor product
U\otimes U^{(q)}
, where
U
is the natural module
U=(\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}})^{3}
of
M^{(\infty)}
, and an element
x\in M^{(\infty)}
acts on
V
as
x\otimes x^{\sigma}
, where
\sigma
is a field automorphism of order
2
(coming from the map
\lambda\mapsto\lambda^{q}
on
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}
).
The element
g^{\prime}
lies in
M^{(\infty)}
and
r_{2}=|g^{\prime}|
is a primitive prime divisor of
(q^{2})^{3}-1
, since
r_{2}
is a primitive prime divisor of
q^{3}-1
and so does not divide
q^{2}-1
or
(q^{2})^{2}-1
. Thus
g^{\prime}
is an irreducible element, and hence, in particular, a regular semisimple element of
{\mathrm{GL}}(U)
. Therefore the eigenspaces of
g^{\prime}
on
U
(working over an algebraic closure of
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}
) are all
1
-dimensional. The same is true for the action of
(g^{\prime})^{\sigma}
on
U^{(q)}
. It follows from (the semisimple case of)
[
27
, Lemma 3.7]
(or see
[
4
, Lemma 5.4.2]
) that the maximum dimension of an eigenspace for
g^{\prime}\otimes(g^{\prime})^{(q)}
acting on
V
is
3
. This a contradiction since, in its action on
V
,
g^{\prime}
has fixed point space of dimension
e_{1}=6
. Thus there are no examples from
[
17
, Example 2.8]
.

Groups from
[
17
, Example 2.9]
.
Finally we consider simple groups
S
of Lie type in characteristic different from
p
.
In this case either
M^{\prime}
,
d
and
e_{1}
are as in one of the lines of
[
17
, Table 7]
(examples from individual groups), or
S,d,e_{1},r_{1}
are as in one of the lines of
[
17
, Table 8]
(examples from infinite families of simple groups).
From
[
17
, Table 7]
we find exactly three possibilities with
d\geqslant 9
and
e_{1}\leqslant d-2
, namely
M^{\prime}
is
{}^{2}{\rm B}_{2}(8)
,
{\rm G}_{2}(3)
, or
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{4}(4)
with
d=14,14
, or
18
, respectively, and in each case
e_{1}=d-2
so
e_{2}=2
. Thus by
[
5
, Theorem 2.11]
and our discussion in Remark
12.6
, the
group
S
must appear in List
\mathcal{B}
in
[
5
, Table 2]
. However, the entries in
[
5
, Table 2]
all have
d\leqslant 10
, so we have no examples from
[
17
, Table 7]
.

Finally, we consider the infinite families
S
and possibilities for
d,e_{1},r_{1}
from
[
17
, Table 8]
with
d\geqslant 9
and
e_{1}\leqslant d-2
. This gives rise to the values of
S,d,e_{1}
listed in Table
17
. In each case
r_{1}=e_{1}+1
and
e_{1}=d-2
or
d-3
, so
e_{2}=2
or
3
. Thus by
[
5
, Theorem 2.11]
and our discussion in Remark
12.6
, if
e_{2}=2
, or if
e_{2}=3
and
d\geqslant 36
, then the group
S
must appear in List
\mathcal{B}
in
[
5
, Table 2]
. However, the only entry in
[
5
, Table 2]
for a group with
d\geqslant 9
, occurs in the first row of
[
5
, Table 2]
with
d=10
and
S={\mathrm{PSU}}_{5}(2)
, and this group
S
does not occur in Table
17
. Thus we have
e_{2}=3
,
e_{1}=d-3
, and
9\leqslant d\leqslant 35
. The only entry in Table
17
with
e_{1}=d-3
is for
S={\mathrm{PSL}}_{2}(s)
with
d=s+1
and
s=2^{c}\geqslant 7
with
c
a prime. Since
r_{1}=\ell e_{1}+1\geqslant e_{1}+1=d-2=s-1
and
r_{i}
divides
|S|
, we must have
r_{1}=e_{1}=d-2=s-1
, and since
9\leqslant d\leqslant 35
it follows that
s=8
or
32
. Now
e_{2}=3
and so
r_{2}=|g^{\prime}|
is a primitive prime divisor of
(q^{u})^{3}-1
, and
r_{2}=|g^{\prime}|=3k+1
, for some
k
. In particular,
g^{\prime}
is not a scalar, and
g^{\prime}\in M^{(\infty)}
, so
r_{2}
divides
|S|
. Also since
r_{1}=s-1
is a primitive prime divisor of
(q^{u})^{e_{1}}-1
and
e_{1}=d-3>3=e_{2}
, it follows that
r_{1}\neq r_{2}
. Thus
r_{2}
is a prime divisor of
|S|
, distinct from
s-1
, and
r_{2}\equiv 1\pmod{3}
. However,
|{\mathrm{PSL}}_{2}(s)|=2^{3}\cdot 3^{2}\cdot 7
or
2^{5}\cdot 3\cdot 11\cdot 31
for
s=8
or
32
, respectively, and there is no such prime
r_{2}
. Thus there are no examples from
[
17
, Example 2.9]
.

We have therefore proved that there are no possibilities for
M
, and hence we have
\textup{Prob}_{9}^{*}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=0
, completing the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
12.1
.
By Proposition
12.7
,

\textup{Prob}_{9}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=\textup{Prob}_{9}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}};\textup{f.d. perm mod}).

Now all the assertions of Proposition
12.1
follow immediately from Proposition
12.3
.

\rho_{\mathrm{nongen}}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=\sum_{i=1}^{9}{\rm Prob}_{i}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})

Remark 13.1
.

In Table

if
