Proposition 13.2
.

Suppose that
G
is a classical group of type
({\mathbf{X}},d,|{\mathbb{F}}|)
on
V
as in Table
1
, and that
e_{1},e_{2}
are integers such that
2\leqslant e_{2}\leqslant e_{1}
and
d=e_{1}+e_{2}>8
. If, for
j=1,2
,
g_{j}
is an
e_{j}
-ppd stingray element in
G
, and if
i\in\{1,2,\dots,9\}
, then,

{\rm Prob}_{i}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant\lambda_{\mathbf{X}}\cdot(q^{-1}+q^{-2})+p(i,{\mathbf{X}})\cdot q^{-d+3}

where
\lambda_{\mathbf{X}}=1
if
(i,{\mathbf{X}})=(1,{\mathbf{L}})
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{X}}=0
otherwise, and where
p(i,{\mathbf{X}})=0
if
i\in\{4,7\}
, and otherwise
p(i,{\mathbf{X}})
is as in line
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{i}
of Table
18
for type
{\mathbf{X}}
.

The proof of Proposition
13.2
uses Remark
13.3
a number of times.

Remark 13.3
.

In Propositions
6.1
to
12.1
, many of
the expressions involve terms of the form
q^{-e_{1}e_{2}}
. Finding upper bounds requires
setting

e_{2}=d-e_{1}
to be the integer maximising
a function
f_{Q}(x)
over
x\in[c,d/2]
, where
2\leqslant c\leqslant d/2
,
d>8
, and
Q\geqslant 2
is a fixed integer. We note that for a fixed
Q
, the functions

f_{Q}(x)=x(d-x)Q^{-x(d-x)}
,
and
g_{Q}(x)=Q^{-x(d-x)}

are decreasing
for
x\in[c,d/2]
.
Thus their maximum value is at
x=c
.

Proof.

We prove the result separately for each Aschbacher class
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{i}
, and each type
{\mathbf{X}}
. Observe that, for type
{\mathbf{L}}
we have
d\geqslant 9
and
e_{2}\geqslant 2
;
for type
{\mathbf{U}}
we have
d\geqslant 10
and
e_{2}\geqslant 3
;
and for all other types

d\geqslant 10
and
e_{2}\geqslant 2,
see Table
3
. For the cases
i\in\{4,7\}
,
{\rm Prob}_{i}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=0
by Proposition
8.1
, and so the result holds with
p(i,{\mathbf{X}})=0
. We assume from now on that
i\not\in\{4,7\}
.

We pursue the following strategy, which applies to all cases except
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{1}
with
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}.
For each type
{\mathbf{X}}

and each Aschbacher class
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{i}
, we first determine an upper bound for

{\rm Prob}_{i}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})
of the form
c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot q^{-f(d)}\cdot q^{-d+3},
where

c_{{\mathbf{X}}}

is a constant depending on
{\mathbf{X}}
and
f(x)
is a polynomial. We then bound
q^{-f(d)}

by choosing a lower bound for
q,
usually
2
or
3
, and an upper bound for
f(d)
for
d\geqslant 9
, usually
f(9)
or
f(10).

Case
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{1}
.
For
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
we have

{\rm Prob}_{1}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})\leqslant q^{-1}+q^{-2}
by Proposition
5.1
, so the result holds with
a_{1}=1
and
p(1,{\mathbf{L}})=0
.
For
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{L}}
,
upper bounds
for
{\rm Prob}_{1}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})

are given in Proposition
5.3
, namely
{\rm Prob}_{1}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{O}}^{-})=0
, and otherwise the bounds involve a constant
c_{{\mathbf{X}}}
depending on
{\mathbf{X}}
and
q
.
For
q=2
we have

c_{{\mathbf{U}}}=2.56
,

c_{{\mathbf{Sp}}}=4
, and

c_{{\mathbf{O}}^{+}}=17.6
,
and for
q\geqslant 3
we have

c_{{\mathbf{U}}}=81/50
,

c_{{\mathbf{Sp}}}=2
, and

c_{{\mathbf{O}}^{+}}=6.1.

For

{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{U}}

{\rm Prob}_{1}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{U}})\leqslant c_{{\mathbf{U}}}\cdot q^{-2e_{1}e_{2}}\leqslant c_{{\mathbf{U}}}\cdot q^{-6(d-3)}=c_{{\mathbf{U}}}\cdot q^{-5d+15}q^{-d+3}.

For
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}},{\mathbf{O}}^{+}

{\rm Prob}_{1}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}}\leqslant c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot q^{-2(d-2)}=c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot q^{-d+1}q^{-d+3}.

The constant
p(i,{\mathbf{X}})
listed
in Table
18
is an upper
bound for
c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot q^{-5d+15}
,
when
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{U}}
and an upper bound for
c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot q^{-d+1}

when
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}},{\mathbf{O}}^{+}
,
where we use
d\geqslant 10
, and we treat
q=2
and
q\geqslant 3
separately.

Case
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{2}
.
By Proposition
6.1
,

{\rm Prob}_{2}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=0

if (i)
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
, or
(ii)
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}
with
q
even, or
(iii)
q^{u}=2
, or (iv)
d
is odd. The result holds in all these cases, so we now
consider all other cases. In particular we now have
q^{u}\geqslant 3
and
d
even, so
d\geqslant 10.

Also
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
,
{\mathbf{U}}
or
{\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}
, and we set
c_{{\mathbf{L}}}=1/2
,
c_{{\mathbf{U}}}=1.46
and
c_{{\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}}=4.12
; and also

Q_{{\mathbf{X}}}=q^{2}
for
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
,
{\mathbf{U}}
, and
Q_{{\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}}=q
.
Then
{\rm Prob}_{2}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot e_{1}e_{2}\cdot Q_{{\mathbf{X}}}^{-e_{1}e_{2}+d/2}
,
by Proposition
6.1
.
We will apply Remark
13.3
.
For this we set
E_{{\mathbf{L}}}=2
,
E_{{\mathbf{U}}}=3
, and
E_{{\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}}=2
unless
q=3
where we take
E_{{\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}}=3
(see Remark
2.3
(a)).
Then by Remark
13.3
,

f_{Q_{{\mathbf{X}}}}(x)=x(d-x){Q_{{\mathbf{X}}}}^{-x(d-x)}
is decreasing for
x\in[E_{{\mathbf{X}}},d/2]
. This yields

\displaystyle{\rm Prob}_{2}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})

\displaystyle\leqslant c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot e_{1}e_{2}\cdot Q_{{\mathbf{X}}}^{-e_{1}e_{2}+d/2}\leqslant c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot{E_{{\mathbf{X}}}(d{-}E_{{\mathbf{X}}})}\cdot Q_{{\mathbf{X}}}^{-E_{{\mathbf{X}}}(d{-}E_{{\mathbf{X}}})+d/2}.

Suppose first that
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
. Then
q=q^{u}\geqslant 3
, and we note that
y\leqslant q^{y/3}
if
q\geqslant 3
and
y\geqslant 7
. Hence
d-2\leqslant q^{(d-2)/3}
since
d-2>7
, and so

\displaystyle{\rm Prob}_{2}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})

\displaystyle\leqslant c_{{\mathbf{L}}}\cdot{2(d{-}2)}\cdot q^{-4(d{-}2)+d}<q^{(d-2)/3}\cdot q^{-3d{+}8}

\displaystyle=q^{(-5d+13)/3}\cdot q^{-d+3}<1.4\cdot 10^{-6}q^{-d+3},

as in Table
18
.
Now consider
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{U}}
and recall that here
e_{2}\geqslant 3
.
Note that
y\leqslant q^{5y/12}
if
q\geqslant 2
and
y\geqslant 7
. Then since
d\geqslant 10
we have

d-3\leqslant q^{5(d-3)/12}
and hence

\displaystyle{\rm Prob}_{2}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{U}})

\displaystyle\leqslant c_{{\mathbf{U}}}\cdot 3(d{-}3)\cdot q^{-6(d{-}3)+d}

\displaystyle\leqslant 3\cdot c_{{\mathbf{U}}}\cdot q^{5(d-3)/12}\cdot q^{-6(d{-}3)+d}=4.38\cdot q^{(-43d+165)/12}\cdot q^{-d+3}.

Inserting
q=2
and
q=3

yields the lower bounds in Table
18
. Finally consider

{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}
with
q
odd
and
d\geqslant 10
. Suppose first that
q\geqslant 5
, and note that
(d-2)<q^{(d-2)/5}
. Hence

\displaystyle{\rm Prob}_{2}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\leqslant 4.12\cdot 2(d-2)\cdot q^{-2(d-2)+d/2}<8.24\cdot q^{(d-2)/5}\cdot q^{-2(d-2)+d/2}

\displaystyle=8.24\cdot q^{-3d/10+3/5}q^{-d+3}\leqslant 0.18\cdot q^{-d+3}

as in Table
18
. It remains to consider the case
q=3
.
Here
e_{2}\geqslant 3
by Remark
2.3
(a), and we note that
(d-3)<3^{(d-2)/4}=q^{(d-2)/4}
. Therefore

\displaystyle{\rm Prob}_{2}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\leqslant 4.12\cdot 3(d-3)\cdot q^{-3(d-3)+d/2}\leqslant 12.36\cdot q^{(d-2)/4}\cdot q^{-3(d-3)+d/2}

\displaystyle=12.36\cdot q^{-5d/4+11/2}q^{-d+3}<0.0057\cdot q^{-d+3}\ll 0.18\cdot q^{-d+3}

as in Table
18
.

Case
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{3}
.
Proposition
7.1
identifies the cases where

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=0
(see Table
9
) and shows that
otherwise

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})<c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}/\alpha},

where
c_{{\mathbf{X}}}
and
\alpha\in\{1,2\}
are given in Table
8
.
Note that for
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
we have
\alpha=1
,
e_{2}\geqslant 2
, and
d\geqslant 9.
Using Remark
13.3
,

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})\leqslant c_{\bf L}\cdot q^{-2(d-2)}=c_{{\mathbf{L}}}\cdot q^{-d+1}q^{-d+3}.

If
q=2
then as
d\geqslant 9
, we have
c_{{\mathbf{L}}}\cdot q^{-d+1}q^{-d+3}<c_{\bf L}\cdot 2^{-8}\cdot q^{-d+3}
, while if
q\geqslant 3
this becomes
c_{{\mathbf{L}}}\cdot q^{-d+1}q^{-d+3}<c_{{\mathbf{L}}}\cdot 3^{-8}\cdot q^{-d+3}
, as in Table
18
.

For type
{\mathbf{U}}
, we have
\alpha=1
,
e_{2}\geqslant 3
, and
d\geqslant 10.
Using Remark
13.3
,

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{U}})\leqslant c_{{\mathbf{U}}}\cdot q^{-3(d-3)}=c_{{\mathbf{U}}}\cdot q^{-2d+6}q^{-d+3}.

If
q=2
then as
d\geqslant 10
, we have
c_{{\mathbf{U}}}\cdot q^{-2d+6}q^{-d+3}<c_{{\mathbf{U}}}\cdot 2^{-14}\cdot q^{-d+3}
, while if
q\geqslant 3
then
c_{{\mathbf{U}}}\cdot q^{-2d+6}q^{-d+3}<c_{{\mathbf{U}}}\cdot 3^{-14}\cdot q^{-d+3}
, as in Table
18
.

Now consider the cases
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
or
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{O}}^{\epsilon}
. Then by Table
10
,
d\geqslant 12
and one of
the lines of that table holds and by Table
8
,
\alpha=2
.
In the cases in Table
10
where
e_{2}\geqslant 4
we find

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}/2}\leqslant c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot q^{-2(d-4)}=c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot q^{-d+5}q^{-d+3}\leqslant c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot q^{-7}q^{-d+3}.

The worst cases for
e_{2}\geqslant 4
are
c_{\mathbf{Sp}}=9.42
,
c_{{\mathbf{O}}^{+}}=35.64
, and
c_{{\mathbf{O}}^{-}}=10.94
, all for
d=12
. These yield the
bounds in Table
10
for
{\mathbf{Sp}}
with
q=2
, and for
{\mathbf{O}}^{+}
and
{\mathbf{O}}^{-}
.

This only leaves the cases
({\mathbf{X}},e_{2})\in\{({\mathbf{Sp}},2),({\mathbf{O}}^{+},2)\}
(since by Table
10
we have
e_{2}\geqslant 4
for
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{O}}^{-}
);
where for
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
we have
q\geqslant 5
and for
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{O}}^{+}
we have
q\neq 3.
Here we have

{\rm Prob}_{3}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant c_{\mathbf{X}}\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}/2}=c_{\mathbf{X}}\cdot q^{-(d-2)}=c_{\mathbf{X}}\cdot q^{-1}q^{-d+3}.

The worst case for
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
has
c_{\mathbf{Sp}}=3.0
giving the bound
0.6
as in Table
18
for
q\geqslant 3
.
For
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{O}}^{+}
with
e_{2}=2
, the worst cases are
c_{{\mathbf{O}}^{+}}=25.62
with
q=2
giving bound
12.81
, and
c_{{\mathbf{O}}^{+}}=8.02
with
q\geqslant 4
giving bound
2.005
, as recorded in Remark
13.1
.

Case
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{5}
.
Recall
q=p^{a}
with
p
a prime.
By Proposition
9.1
,
{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=0
if
a=1
, or if
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{L}}
and
a=2
, so we may assume that
q\geqslant 4
for type
{\mathbf{L}}
and
q\geqslant 8
if
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{L}}
; and we use the bounds provided by Proposition
9.1
.
Consider first type
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
, where by Remark
13.3
, and using
d\geqslant 9
and
q\geqslant 4
,

\displaystyle{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})

\displaystyle\leqslant 4.04\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}+(d-1)/2}\leqslant 4.04\cdot q^{-2(d-2)+(d-1)/2}

\displaystyle=4.04\cdot q^{-(d-1)/2}q^{-d+3}\leqslant 4.04\cdot 4^{-4}q^{-d+3}<0.016\cdot q^{-d+3}.

For
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{L}}
we have
d\geqslant 10
and
q\geqslant 8
. Further
e_{2}\geqslant 3
for type
{\mathbf{U}}
and it follows that

\displaystyle{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{U}})

\displaystyle\leqslant 2.11\cdot q^{-4e_{1}e_{2}/3+2(d-1)/3}\leqslant 2.11\cdot q^{-4(d-3)+2(d-1)/3}

\displaystyle=2.11\cdot q^{-(7d-25)/3}q^{-d+3}\leqslant 6\cdot 10^{-14}q^{-d+3}.

For the other types let

c_{{\mathbf{Sp}}}=1.82
,

c_{{\mathbf{O}}^{+}}=2.47
, and

c_{{\mathbf{O}}^{-}}=5.46
. Then

{\rm Prob}_{5}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot q^{-2e_{1}e_{2}/3+d/3}\leqslant c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot q^{-2\cdot 2(d-2)/3+d/3}=c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot q^{-1/3}q^{-d+3}\leqslant c_{{\mathbf{X}}}/2\cdot q^{-d+3}

giving the bounds in Table
10
.

Case
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{6}
.
Suppose that
{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})>0
. Then by Proposition
10.2
,
(i)
q
is an odd prime so
q\geqslant 3
, (ii)
d=2^{n}
with
n\geqslant 4
,
(iii)
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{U}},{\mathbf{O}}^{-}
, and (iv) there are two possibilities for
(e_{1},e_{2})
which we consider separately for each type. Suppose first that
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
. If
(e_{1},e_{2})=(d/2,d/2)
then by Proposition
10.2
, as
d\geqslant 16
,

{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})\leqslant\frac{45}{1024}\cdot q^{-d^{2}/4}=\frac{45}{1024}\cdot q^{-d^{2}/4+d-3}q^{-d+3}\leqslant 2.1\cdot 10^{-26}\cdot q^{-d+3}.

If

e_{2}=2
then
d\geqslant 32
and
q\geqslant 5
, by Proposition
10.2
(ii), and

{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})\leqslant\frac{45}{640}\cdot q^{-\frac{59}{25}d+8}=\frac{45}{640}\cdot q^{-\frac{34}{25}d+5}q^{-d+3}\leqslant 8.4\cdot 10^{-29}\cdot q^{-d+3},

and we record the larger of these bounds in Table
10
.
Now suppose that
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}

or
{\mathbf{O}}^{+}
. If
(e_{1},e_{2})=(d/2,d/2),
then by Proposition
10.2
, as
q\geqslant 3
and
d\geqslant 16

{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant\frac{45}{1024}q^{-\frac{1}{4}d^{2}+\frac{23}{10}d}=\frac{45}{1024}\cdot q^{-\frac{1}{4}d^{2}+\frac{33}{10}d-3}q^{-d+3}\leqslant 7.4\cdot 10^{-9}\cdot q^{-d+3}.

Finally consider the case

e_{2}=2
. Here, by Proposition
10.2
,
either
d=32
,
q\in\{11,13,17\}
, and

{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant 6.9\cdot q^{-d+3},

or one of
q\geqslant 19
or
d\geqslant 128
, and

{\rm Prob}_{6}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant 5.3\cdot 10^{-3}\cdot q^{-d+3}.

The latter bound is recorded in Table
10
, and the former special case is recorded in Remark
13.1
.

Case
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{8}
.
Suppose that
{\rm Prob}_{8}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})>0
. Then by Proposition
11.1
,
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
,
d
is even so
d\geqslant 10
, and

{\rm Prob}_{8}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})\leqslant c_{\mathbf{L}}\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}+d/2}
, where
c_{\mathbf{L}}=1.46
if
q=2
, and
c_{\mathbf{L}}=3.18
if
q\geqslant 3
. We obtain using Remark
13.3
that

{\rm Prob}_{8}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{L}})\leqslant c_{\mathbf{L}}\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}+d/2}\leqslant c_{\mathbf{L}}\cdot q^{-2(d-2)+d/2}=c_{\mathbf{L}}\cdot q^{-d/2+1}\cdot q^{-d+3}.

Since
d\geqslant 10
, for
q=2
we have
c_{\mathbf{L}}\cdot q^{-d/2+1}<0.092
, while for

q\geqslant 3
we have
c_{\mathbf{L}}\cdot q^{-d/2+1}<0.04
, as in Table
18
.

Case
\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{C}_{9}
.
Suppose that
{\rm Prob}_{9}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})>0
. Then by Proposition
12.1
,
q^{u}=p
is a prime, and either
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
with
q=2
or

{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}
, and

{\rm Prob}_{9}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}+d/2}
,
where
d\geqslant 10
,

c_{{\mathbf{Sp}}}=6.38
, and

c_{{\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}}=8.24

if
q
is odd or

c_{{\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}}=28.1

if
q=2.
Using Remark
13.3
we have

{\rm Prob}_{9}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot q^{-e_{1}e_{2}+d/2}\leqslant c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot q^{-2(d-2)+d/2}=c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot q^{-(d-2)/2}\cdot q^{-d+3}.

Since
d\geqslant 10
, for
q=2
we have
c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot q^{-(d-2)/2}\leqslant c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot 2^{-4}
, while for

q\geqslant 3
we have
c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot q^{-(d-2)/2}\leqslant c_{{\mathbf{X}}}\cdot 3^{-4}
, giving the bounds in Table
18
.

Since
q^{-(d-2)/2}\leqslant 3^{-4}
for
q\geqslant 3
and
q^{-(d-2)/2}\leqslant 2^{-4}
for
q=2
, and upper bounds in Table
18
follow on substituting the values of
c_{{\mathbf{X}}}
.
∎

13.1.
Proof of Theorem
1.3

Recall that by Equation (
8
)
and Proposition
13.2
,

\rho_{\mathrm{nongen}}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})=\sum_{i=1}^{9}{\rm Prob}_{i}(g_{1},g_{2},{\mathbf{X}})\leqslant\lambda_{\mathbf{X}}(q^{-1}+q^{-2})+\sum_{i}p(i,{\mathbf{X}})\cdot q^{-d+3}

where
\lambda_{\mathbf{X}}=1
for type
{\mathbf{L}}
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{X}}=0
otherwise.
To determine
\kappa_{\mathbf{X}}(q)
, we consider
the different types
{\mathbf{X}}.

For most types, to determine the ‘constants’
\kappa_{\mathbf{X}}(q)
it is sufficient to add up the upper bounds
p(i,{\mathbf{X}})
stated in the column labelled

{\mathbf{X}}
in Table
18
for the corresponding value of
q
. We treat the exceptional cases in Remark
13.1
separately.
Let

p_{{\mathbf{X}}}\coloneq\sum_{i}p(i,{\mathbf{X}}).

Consider first type
{\mathbf{L}}
. If
q=2
then we may take
p_{{\mathbf{L}}}=0.1081
, so we may take
\kappa_{\mathbf{X}}(q)=0.11

while if
q\geqslant 3
then
p_{{\mathbf{L}}}\leqslant 0.05632
so we take
\kappa_{\mathbf{X}}(q)=0.06.

For type
{\mathbf{U}}
with
q=2

we find
p_{{\mathbf{U}}}\leqslant 5.3\cdot 10^{-6}
and we take
\kappa_{\mathbf{X}}(q)=5.3\cdot 10^{-6}

while if
q\geqslant 3
then we choose

p_{{\mathbf{U}}}<1.6\cdot 10^{-8}=\kappa_{\mathbf{X}}(q).

For type
{\mathbf{Sp}}
with
q=2

we find
p_{{\mathbf{Sp}}}=1.147<1.15=\kappa_{\mathbf{X}}(q)
,
while if
q\geqslant 3
then

p_{{\mathbf{Sp}}}=1.5154<1.52=\kappa_{\mathbf{X}}(q).

In the exceptional case where
e_{2}=2
with
q\geqslant 3
we have
p_{{\mathbf{Sp}}}=8.4101<8.42=\kappa_{\mathbf{X}}(q).

For type
{\mathbf{O}}^{+}
with
q=2

we find
p_{{\mathbf{O}}^{+}}=2.074<2.08=\kappa_{\mathbf{X}}(q)
,
while if
q\geqslant 3
then

p_{{\mathbf{O}}^{+}}=1.53984<1.54=\kappa_{\mathbf{X}}(q).

In the exceptional case where
e_{2}=2
, for
q=2
we have
p_{{\mathbf{O}}^{+}}=14.605<14.61=\kappa_{\mathbf{X}}(q)
. In the case with
e_{2}=2
and
q\geqslant 3

we have
p_{{\mathbf{O}}^{+}}=3.52784<3.53=\kappa_{\mathbf{X}}(q)
, while in the special case with
d=32
and
q\in\{11,13,17\}
, we have
p_{{\mathbf{O}}^{+}}=10.4273<10.43=\kappa_{\mathbf{X}}(q)
.

For type
{\mathbf{O}}^{-}
with
q=2

we find
p_{{\mathbf{O}}^{-}}=1.846<1.85=\kappa_{\mathbf{X}}(q)
,
while if
q\geqslant 3
then

p_{{\mathbf{O}}^{-}}=3.018<3.02=\kappa_{\mathbf{X}}(q).
This completes the proof of the main assertion of Theorem
1.3
. In particular, the values of
\kappa_{{\mathbf{X}}}(q)
in Table
2
are valid.
The final assertion is proved using the bounds in Table
2
. First we consider the bounds in columns 2 and 3 of Table
2
for the types
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{L}}
. Recall that in this case
d
is even and so
d\geqslant 10
. Hence

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{gen}}(g_{1},g_{2},G)

\displaystyle\geqslant\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}1-\max\{5.3\cdot 10^{-6}\cdot 2^{-d+3},1.6\cdot 10^{-8}\cdot 3^{-d+3}\}&>0.999&\mbox{if}\ {\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{U}}\\
1-\max\{1.15\cdot 2^{-d+3},1.52\cdot 3^{-d+3}\}&>0.991&\mbox{if}\ {\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}\\
1-\max\{2.08\cdot 2^{-d+3},1.54\cdot 3^{-d+3}\}&>0.983&\mbox{if}\ {\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{O}}^{+}\\
1-\max\{1.85\cdot 2^{-d+3},3.02\cdot 3^{-d+3}\}&>0.985&\mbox{if}\ {\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{O}}^{-}\\
\end{array}\right.

and so in these cases
\rho_{\mathrm{gen}}(g_{1},g_{2},G)>0.983
. Next consider the bounds in columns 2 and 3 of Table
2
for
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
(so here
d\geqslant 9
): for
q=2
we have
\rho_{\mathrm{gen}}(g_{1},g_{2},G)\geqslant 1-q^{-1}-q^{-2}-0.11\cdot q^{-d+3}>0.248
, while for
q\geqslant 3
we get
\rho_{\mathrm{gen}}(g_{1},g_{2},G)\geqslant 1-q^{-1}-q^{-2}-0.06\cdot q^{-d+3}>0.555
. Finally we consider the exceptional bounds in column 4 of Table
2
. Here
d\geqslant 10
. If
({\mathbf{X}},q,e_{2})=({\mathbf{O}}^{+},2,2)
, then we have
\rho_{\mathrm{gen}}(g_{1},g_{2},G)\geqslant 1-14.61\cdot 2^{-7}>0.885
. In the other three lines we have
q\geqslant 3
and for each line
\rho_{\mathrm{gen}}(g_{1},g_{2},G)\geqslant 1-10.43\cdot 3^{-7}>0.995
.
∎

13.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

We prove Theorem
1.1
as a corollary to the following more precise and technical result.

Proposition 13.4
.

Suppose that
G
is a classical group of type
({\mathbf{X}},n,q)
with
n>8
as in Table
1
, and that
h_{1},h_{2}
are independent randomly selected elements of
G
. Then with probability at least
c_{0}/\log n
, for some positive constant
c_{0}
, some powers
h_{1}^{m_{1}},h_{2}^{m_{2}}
generate a naturally embedded classical subgroup of type
({\mathbf{Y}},d,q)
, where
2\alpha\log n_{0}<d\leqslant 4\alpha\log n_{0}
with
\alpha,n_{0}
as in Table
19
, and
{\mathbf{X}},{\mathbf{Y}}
are as in Table
1
.

Table 19.
Values of
\alpha,n_{0}
for Proposition
13.4
.

Types

\alpha

n_{0}

L
or
U

1

n

Sp
or
{\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}

2

(n-\nu)/2
, where
\nu\in\{0,1\}
,

and
n\equiv\nu\pmod{2}

Proof.

Assume the hypotheses of Proposition
13.4
and let
V
denote the underlying space
{\mathbb{F}}^{n}
on which
G
acts naturally, with
{\mathbb{F}}
as in Table
1
. We write
n=n_{0}
if
{\bf X}={\bf L}
or
{\bf U}
,
n=2n_{0}
if
{\bf X}={\bf Sp}
or
{\bf O}^{\pm}
, and finally
n=2n_{0}+1
if
{\bf X}={\bf O}^{\circ}
with
q
odd. Thus
n_{0}
is as in Table
19
.
Then, for
i=1
or
2
, by
[
31
, Theorem 3.3(b)]
, the probability that some power
g_{i}\coloneq h_{i}^{m_{i}}
is an
e_{i}
-ppd stingray element, with
e_{i}\in(\alpha\log n_{0},2\alpha\log n_{0}]
, is at least
b/\log n
for an explicit positive constant
b
(where
\alpha,n_{0}
are as in Table
19
). Assume now that this is the case. Let
d\coloneq e_{1}+e_{2}
and note that
d\in(2\alpha\log n_{0},4\alpha\log n_{0}]
. For
i=1,2
, let
U_{g_{i}}
be the
g_{i}
-invariant
e_{i}
-subspace of
V
on which
g_{i}
acts irreducibly and let
F_{g_{i}}
be the fixed point space of
g_{i}
in
V
. Note also that if
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{L}}
then, by Lemma
2.9
and Table
5
, the
e_{i}
have the same parity and so
d
is even.

By
[
12
, Lemma 2.1]
if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
, or
[
13
, Theorem 1.1]
(and
[
11
, Theorem 1.1]
for
|{\mathbb{F}}|=2
) if
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{L}}
, there exists a positive constant
b^{\prime}
such that, with probability at least
b^{\prime}
we have
U_{g_{1}}\cap U_{g_{2}}=0
, so that
V_{0}\coloneq U_{g_{1}}\oplus U_{g_{2}}
is a
d
-subspace, and in addition
V_{0}
is nondegenerate if
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{L}}
. Assume now that
V_{0}
is a
d
-space on which
H\coloneq\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle
acts, fixing the complement
F_{g_{1}}\cap F_{g_{2}}
, and that
V_{0}
is nondegenerate if
{\mathbf{X}}\neq{\mathbf{L}}
. Note that
H
is a subgroup of a naturally embedded classical subgroup of
G
, acting on
V_{0}
and fixing
F_{g_{1}}\cap F_{g_{2}}
pointwise.
Then, by Theorem
1.3
, with probability at least a positive constant
b^{\prime\prime}
,
H
is a classical subgroup of type
({\mathbf{Y}},d,q)
on
V_{0}
, where
{\mathbf{X}},{\mathbf{Y}}
are as in Table
1
.

Putting these steps together we see that all the conditions of Proposition
13.4
hold for
h_{1},h_{2}
with probability at least
c_{0}/\log n
where
c_{0}=bb^{\prime}b^{\prime\prime}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Now we prove Theorem
1.1
. We take the constant
k(\eta)
in Theorem
1.1
to be
\log(\eta^{-1})/c_{0}
, with
c_{0}
as in Proposition
13.4
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.
We use the notation from Proposition
13.4
and its proof above.
First we note that, if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{L}}
or
{\mathbf{U}}
, then the dimension
d
in Proposition
13.4
satisfies
2\log n<d\leqslant 4\log n
. On the other hand if
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{Sp}}
or
{\mathbf{O}}^{\varepsilon}
with
\varepsilon\in\{+,-,\circ\}
, then
n=2n_{0}
or
2n_{0}+1
so
n_{0}=n/2
or
(n-1)/2
respectively (see Table
19
), and since
n>8
we have

2\log n<4\log((n-1)/2)<d\leqslant 8\log(n/2)<8\log n.

Thus for all types
2\log n<d\leqslant c({\mathbf{X}})\log n
with
c({\mathbf{X}})
as in Table
1
.

Now suppose that
\eta>0
, and that
h_{1},h_{2},\dots,h_{k+1}
are independent uniformly distributed random elements from
G
for some
k>1
. Then by Proposition
13.4
, for each
i
, the probability
\pi
that some powers
h_{i}^{m_{1}},h_{i+1}^{m_{2}}
generate a naturally embedded
d
-dimensional classical subgroup of
G
with
d
as above, satisfies
\pi\geqslant c_{0}/\log n
with
c_{0}
the positive constant in Proposition
13.4
. Thus the probability that at least one of these
k
pairs generates such a subgroup is
1-(1-\pi)^{k}
, and this probability is at least
1-\eta
if and only if
\tau\coloneq(1-\pi)^{k}\leqslant\eta
. We claim that this holds for all
k\geqslant k(\eta)\log n
, where
k(\eta)=\log(\eta^{-1})/c_{0}
(a positive constant).

Note that
0<c_{0}/\log n<\pi<1
, and hence
x\coloneq-c_{0}/\log n
satisfies
-1<x<0
. Therefore (see, for example,
[
30
, (2)]
),
\log(1+x)<x<0
. Thus our assumption
k\geqslant k(\eta)\log n
implies, since each of
x,\log(1+x)
, and
\log(\eta)
is negative,

k\geqslant k(\eta)\log n=\frac{\log(\eta^{-1})}{c_{0}}\log n=\frac{\log(\eta^{-1})}{-x}=\frac{\log(\eta)}{x}>\frac{\log(\eta)}{\log(1+x)}.

Therefore
\log(\eta)>k\log(1+x)=\log((1+x)^{k})
, and hence
\eta>(1+x)^{k}=(1-c_{0}/\log n)^{k}\geqslant(1-\pi)^{k}=\tau
. Thus the claim is proved, and hence also Theorem
1.1
is proved.∎

14.
